Quantized Weyl algebras, the double centralizer property, and a new First Fundamental Theorem for U q ​ ( 𝔤 ​ 𝔩 n ) U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
Gail Letzter, Siddhartha Sahi, Hadi Salmasian

Abstract
. Let 𝒫 := 𝒫 m × n \mathscr{P}:=\mathscr{P}_{m\times n} denote the quantized coordinate ring of the space of m × n m\times n matrices, equipped with natural actions of the quantized enveloping algebras U q ​ ( 𝔤 ​ 𝔩 m ) U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m}) and U q ​ ( 𝔤 ​ 𝔩 n ) U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n}) . Let ℒ \mathscr{L} and ℛ \mathscr{R} denote the images of U q ​ ( 𝔤 ​ 𝔩 m ) U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m}) and U q ​ ( 𝔤 ​ 𝔩 n ) U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n}) in End ⁡ ( 𝒫 ) \mathrm{End}(\mathscr{P}) , respectively. We define a q q -analogue of the algebra of polynomial-coefficient differential operators inside End ⁡ ( 𝒫 ) \mathrm{End}(\mathscr{P}) , henceforth denoted by 𝒫 ​ 𝒟 \mathscr{PD} , and we prove that ℒ ∩ 𝒫 ​ 𝒟 \mathscr{L}\cap\mathscr{PD} and ℛ ∩ 𝒫 ​ 𝒟 \mathscr{R}\cap\mathscr{PD} are mutual centralizers inside 𝒫 ​ 𝒟 \mathscr{PD} . Using this, we establish a new First Fundamental Theorem of invariant theory for U q ​ ( 𝔤 ​ 𝔩 n ) U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n}) . We also compute explicit formulas in terms of q q -determinants for generators of the algebras ℒ 𝔥 ∩ 𝒫 ​ 𝒟 \mathscr{L}_{\mathfrak{h}}\cap\mathscr{PD} and ℛ 𝔥 ∩ 𝒫 ​ 𝒟 \mathscr{R}_{\mathfrak{h}}\cap\mathscr{PD} , where ℒ 𝔥 \mathscr{L}_{\mathfrak{h}} and ℛ 𝔥 \mathscr{R}_{\mathfrak{h}} denote the images of the Cartan subalgebras of U q ​ ( 𝔤 ​ 𝔩 m ) U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m}) and U q ​ ( 𝔤 ​ 𝔩 n ) U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n}) in End ⁡ ( 𝒫 ) \mathrm{End}(\mathscr{P}) , respectively. Our algebra 𝒫 ​ 𝒟 \mathscr{PD} and the algebra Pol ​ ( Mat m , n ) q \mathrm{Pol}(\mathrm{Mat}_{m,n})_{q} that is defined in [ SSV04 ] are related by extension of scalars, but we give a new construction of 𝒫 ​ 𝒟 \mathscr{PD} using deformed twisted tensor products.



1.
Introduction

The First Fundamental Theorem (FFT) is one of the pinnacles of invariant theory with a history as old as Hermann Weyl’s influential book,
The Classical Groups

[
Wy39
]
. In its original form, the FFT for the group

\mathrm{GL}_{n}

describes the generators of the subalgebra of
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
-invariants in the polynomial algebra
\mathcal{P}(V^{\oplus k}\oplus(V^{*})^{\oplus l})
, where
V:=\mathbb{C}^{n}
denotes the standard
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
-module.

It was pointed out by R. Howe
[
Ho95
, Sec. 2.3]
that
the FFT has an equivalent formulation as a double centralizer property, which we now recall. Let
\mathrm{Mat}_{m\times n}
denote the vector space of complex
m\times n
matrices. Then
\mathrm{Mat}_{m\times n}
has a natural
\mathrm{GL}_{m}\times\mathrm{GL}_{n}
-module structure by left and right matrix multiplication. We equip the
algebra
\mathcal{P}:=\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{Mat}_{m\times n})
of polynomials on
\mathrm{Mat}_{m\times n}

and the
algebra
\mathcal{PD}:=\mathcal{PD}(\mathrm{Mat}_{m\times n})
of polynomial-coefficient differential operators
on
\mathrm{Mat}_{m\times n}
with their canonical

\mathrm{GL}_{m}\times\mathrm{GL}_{n}
-module structures. Recall that
\mathcal{P}
is a
\mathcal{PD}
-module. The (infinitesimal) actions of the Lie algebras
\mathfrak{gl}_{m}
and
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
on

\mathcal{P}

are given by certain differential operators of order one, which are usually called
polarization operators
.
It follows that there exists a homomorphism of algebras
\phi:U_{m,n}\to\mathcal{PD}
, where

U_{m,n}:=U(\mathfrak{gl}_{m})\otimes U(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
is the tensor product of the universal enveloping algebras of
\mathfrak{gl}_{m}
and
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
,
such that the diagram

(1)

\textstyle{U_{m,n}\otimes\mathcal{P}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{x\otimes f\mapsto\phi(x)\otimes f\hskip 14.22636pt}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 14.22636ptx\otimes f\mapsto x\cdot f}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{PD}\otimes\mathcal{P}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{D\otimes f\mapsto D\cdot f}

commutes.
The
operator commutant version
of the FFT, according to
[
Ho95
, Thm 2.3.3]
, states that
the subalgebra

\mathcal{PD}^{\mathrm{GL}_{m}}

of
\mathrm{GL}_{m}
-invariants in
\mathcal{PD}
is generated by the image of
U(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
.
Since

\mathcal{PD}^{\mathrm{GL}_{m}}=\mathcal{PD}^{U(\mathfrak{gl}_{m})}
,
the latter assertion is equivalent to the following: the images of
U(\mathfrak{gl}_{m})
and
U(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
in
\mathcal{PD}
are mutual centralizers.

In
[
LZZ11
, Sec. 6]
the authors
extend the original form of the FFT to the quantized enveloping algebra
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
by considering a
q
-analogue of
\mathcal{P}(V^{\oplus k}\oplus(V^{*})^{l})
that carries a
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
-action, and then describing the generators of the subalgebra of invariants. It is then natural to ask if the operator commutant version of the FFT
also has a
q
-analogue. It turns out that in the quantized setting, the situation for the operator commutant FFT is more subtle than in the classical case. One major issue is how to quantize the Weyl algebra
\mathcal{PD}
and, more importantly, the map
\phi:U_{m,n}\to\mathcal{PD}
. Indeed we provide some justification that the latter map cannot be fully quantized (see Proposition
3.14.4
).
Nevertheless, our first main result (Theorem
A
)
is a positive answer to the
above question.

From now on let

\Bbbk:=\mathbb{C}(q)
be the field of rational functions in a parameter
q
.
For the operator commutant FFT in the quantized setting we need a quantized Weyl algebra
\mathscr{PD}:=\mathscr{PD}_{m\times n}
. The
\Bbbk
-algebra
\mathscr{PD}
that we consider is closely related to the algebra

\mathrm{Pol}(\mathrm{Mat}_{m,n})_{q}

of

[
SSV04
,
BKV06
]
(see Corollary
3.16.1
).
We give a different construction of
\mathscr{PD}
as the
deformed twisted tensor product
of
\mathscr{P}:=\mathscr{P}_{m\times n}
, the quantized coordinate ring of
\mathrm{Mat}_{m\times n}
, and
\mathscr{D}:=\mathscr{D}_{m\times n}
, the quantized algebra of constant-coefficient differential operators
on
\mathrm{Mat}_{m\times n}

(see Section
3
for precise definitions).
The construction of
\mathscr{P}
and
\mathscr{D}
is analogous to the FRT
construction
[
KS97
, Sec. 9.1]
.
Concretely, the algebra
\mathscr{PD}
is generated by
2mn
generators
t_{i,j}
and
\partial_{i,j}
, where
1\leq i\leq m
and
1\leq j\leq n
, modulo the relations that are described in Section
3
(see Definition
3.7.3
).
From now on we set

U_{L}:=U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m})\quad,\quad U_{R}:=U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})\quad,\quad U_{LR}:=U_{L}\otimes U_{R}.

Both
\mathscr{P}
and
\mathscr{PD}
are

U_{LR}
-module algebras (the explicit formulas for the
U_{LR}
-action on generators are given in Remark
3.6.2
).
Furthermore,
\mathscr{P}
is naturally a
\mathscr{PD}
-module.
In particular, we have homomorphisms of associative algebras

\phi_{U}:U_{LR}\to\mathrm{End}_{\Bbbk}(\mathscr{P})\quad\text{and}\quad\phi_{PD}:\mathscr{PD}\to\mathrm{End}_{\Bbbk}(\mathscr{P}).

Since
\mathscr{P}
is a faithful
\mathscr{PD}
-module (see Proposition
3.11.4
), we can identify
\mathscr{PD}
with
\phi_{PD}(\mathscr{PD})
. Using the latter identification,
we set

\mathscr{L}:=\phi_{U}(U_{L}\otimes 1),\quad\mathscr{R}:=\phi_{U}(1\otimes U_{R}),\quad{\mathscr{L}_{\bullet}}:=\mathscr{L}\cap\mathscr{PD},\quad{\mathscr{R}_{\bullet}}:=\mathscr{R}\cap\mathscr{PD}.

Note that in general
we have
{\mathscr{L}_{\bullet}}\subsetneq\mathscr{L}
and

{\mathscr{R}_{\bullet}}\subsetneq\mathscr{R}
. In fact if
m\leq n
then the restriction of
\phi_{U}
to
U_{L}\otimes 1
yields an isomorphism
U_{L}\cong\mathscr{L}
but one can show that
\phi_{U}^{-1}({\mathscr{L}_{\bullet}})
is properly contained in the locally finite part of
U_{L}
(see Proposition
3.14.3

and Example
10.2.2
).
Set

(2)

\mathsf{L}_{i,j}:=\sum_{r=1}^{n}t_{i,r}\partial_{j,r}\ \text{ for }1\leq i,j\leq m\ \text{ and }\ \mathsf{R}_{i,j}:=\sum_{r=1}^{m}t_{r,i}\partial_{r,j}\ \text{ for }1\leq i,j\leq n.

The
\mathsf{L}_{i,j}
and the
\mathsf{R}_{i,j}
are natural analogues in
\mathscr{PD}
of the
polarization operators of the (non-quantized) Weyl algebra
\mathcal{PD}
.
We have
\mathsf{L}_{i,j}\in{\mathscr{L}_{\bullet}}
and
\mathsf{R}_{i,j}\in{\mathscr{R}_{\bullet}}
(see Corollary
6.1.6
). Of course, similar inclusions hold in the non-quantized case. However, the situation with the preimages of
the

\mathsf{L}_{i,j}
and the
\mathsf{R}_{i,j}
in
U_{L}
and
U_{R}
is more complicated. For example, the root vector
E_{\varepsilon_{i}-\varepsilon_{j}}
of
U_{L}
(respectively,
U_{R}
), where
i<j
, does not lie in the preimage of
\mathsf{L}_{i,j}
(respectively,
\mathsf{R}_{i,j}
).

Henceforth we adopt the following notation: for subsets
\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Z}
of an algebra
\mathcal{X}
, we set

(3)

\mathcal{Y}^{\mathcal{Z}}:=\{y\in\mathcal{Y}\,:\,yz=zy\text{ for all }z\in\mathcal{Z}\}.

Our first main theorem is the following.

Theorem A
.

Let
\mathscr{L}
,
\mathscr{R}
,
{\mathscr{L}_{\bullet}}
, and
{\mathscr{R}_{\bullet}}
be the subalgebras of
\mathrm{End}_{\Bbbk}(\mathscr{P})
defined above. We identify
\mathscr{PD}
with
\phi_{PD}(\mathscr{PD})\subseteq\mathrm{End}_{\Bbbk}(\mathscr{P})
. Then the following statements hold.

(i)

\mathscr{PD}^{{\mathscr{R}_{\bullet}}}=\mathscr{PD}^{\mathscr{R}}={\mathscr{L}_{\bullet}}
. Furthermore,
{\mathscr{L}_{\bullet}}
is generated
by the
\mathsf{L}_{i,j}
for
1\leq i,j\leq m
.

(ii)

\mathscr{PD}^{{\mathscr{L}_{\bullet}}}=\mathscr{PD}^{\mathscr{L}}={\mathscr{R}_{\bullet}}
.
Furthermore,
{\mathscr{R}_{\bullet}}
is generated by the
\mathsf{R}_{i,j}
for
1\leq i,j\leq n
.

Let us elucidate the relation between
Theorem
A
and the
literature on Howe duality and the
FFT in the quantized setting.
Quantized analogues of
(\mathfrak{gl}_{m},\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
-duality have been established in
[
Zh02
]
and
[
NYM93
]
, but these works do not consider the double centralizer property inside a quantized Weyl algebra.
To compare our results with those of
Lehrer–Zhang–Zhang
[
LZZ11
]
, we
briefly explain their formulation of the FFT for
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
. In
[
LZZ11
, Sec. 6]
the authors define a
q
-analogue of the algebra

\mathcal{P}(V^{\oplus k}\oplus(V^{*})^{\oplus l})
, which they call
\mathcal{A}_{k,l}
(this algebra tacitly depends on
n
as well). The algebra
\mathcal{A}_{k,l}
is isomorphic to a twisted tensor product of
\mathscr{P}_{k\times n}
and
\mathscr{D}_{l\times n}
, but the twisting is only with respect to the universal
R
-matrix of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
. In particular, in the special case
k=l
the relations on the generators of
\mathcal{A}_{k,k}
are not symmetric with respect to their indices. Because of this asymmetry,
\mathcal{A}_{k,k}
does not appear to be the desired object for proving a double centralizer statement. The twisting that we consider to define
\mathscr{PD}
uses the universal
R
-matrices of both
U_{L}
and
U_{R}
.
In addition, unlike
\mathcal{A}_{k,l}
whose relations are homogeneous, the relation (R6) of
\mathscr{PD}
is not homogeneous. From this viewpoint,
\mathscr{PD}
resembles the classical Weyl algebra more than
\mathcal{A}_{k,k}
.

Our second main result (Theorem
B
below)
is a new FFT for
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
,
in the spirit of the aforementioned result of
[
LZZ11
]
,
for a family of algebras

\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}
where
k,l,n
are positive integers.
The latter algebras generalize
\mathscr{PD}
and
indeed we have

\mathscr{A}_{m,m,n}=\mathscr{PD}_{m\times n}
.
An explicit presentation of
\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}
by generators and relations is given in Proposition
3.9.2
.
To state Theorem
B
, we need some notation.
For integers
1\leq a\leq m
and
1\leq b\leq n

there exists an embedding of associative algebras

(4)

\boldsymbol{e}=\boldsymbol{e}_{a\times b}^{m\times n}:\mathscr{PD}_{a\times b}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{PD}_{m\times n}

that is defined as follows.
We relabel the generators of
\mathscr{PD}_{a\times b}
by setting

(5)

\tilde{t}_{i,j}:=t_{a+1-i,b+1-j}\ \ \text{ and }\ \ \tilde{\partial}_{i,j}:=\partial_{a+1-i,b+1-j}\quad\text{ for }1\leq i\leq a,\ 1\leq j\leq b.

We relabel the generators of
\mathscr{PD}_{m\times n}
similarly, with
a
and
b
replaced by
m
and
n
respectively.
The map
\boldsymbol{e}
of (
4
) is uniquely determined by the assignments

\boldsymbol{e}(\tilde{t}_{i,j}):=\tilde{t}_{i,j}
and

\boldsymbol{e}(\tilde{\partial}_{i,j}):=\tilde{\partial}_{i,j}

(see Proposition
3.9.4
).
Concretely, the map
\boldsymbol{e}
identifies the
a\times b
matrices
[t_{i,j}]
and
[\partial_{i,j}]
formed by the generators of
\mathscr{PD}_{a\times b}
with the intersections of the lowest
a
rows and the rightmost
b
columns in the analogous
m\times n
matrices formed by the generators of
\mathscr{PD}_{m\times n}
.
Now fix integers
k,l,n\geq 1
and set
m:=\max\{k,l\}
.
We define
\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}
to be the subalgebra
of
\mathscr{PD}_{m\times n}
that is generated by
the
\tilde{t}_{i,j}
and the
\tilde{\partial}_{i^{\prime},j}
,
where

1\leq i\leq k
,
1\leq i^{\prime}\leq l
, and
1\leq j\leq n
.
The
U_{R}
-action on
\mathscr{PD}_{m\times n}
leaves
\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}
invariant and thus

\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}
is a
U_{R}
-module algebra.
The standard degree filtration of
\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}
(corresponding to setting
\deg\tilde{t}_{i,j}=\deg\tilde{\partial}_{i^{\prime},j}=1
)
is
U_{R}
-stable, hence the associated graded algebra
\mathrm{gr}(\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n})
is also a
U_{R}
-module algebra.
Let
\epsilon_{R}
be the counit of
U_{R}
and denote the subalgebra of
U_{R}
-invariants in
\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}

by

\left(\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}\right)_{(\epsilon_{R})}
, that is,

\left(\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}\right)_{(\epsilon_{R})}:=\left\{D\in\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}\,:\,x\cdot D=\epsilon_{R}(x)D\text{ for }x\in U_{R}\right\}.

We denote the subalgebra of
U_{R}
-invariants in

\mathrm{gr}(\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n})
by

\big(\mathrm{gr}(\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n})\big)_{(\epsilon_{R})}
as well.
For
1\leq i\leq k
and
1\leq j\leq l

we define elements
\tilde{\mathsf{L}}_{i,j}\in\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}

by the formula

(6)

\tilde{\mathsf{L}}_{i,j}:=\sum_{r=1}^{n}\tilde{t}_{i,r}\tilde{\partial}_{j,r}=\sum_{r=1}^{n}t_{m-i+1,r}\partial_{m-j+1,r}.

By the same formula we can define analogous elements in
\mathrm{gr}(\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n})
. By a slight abuse of the symbol
\mathrm{gr}(\cdot)
, we denote these elements of

\mathrm{gr}(\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n})

by

\mathrm{gr}(\tilde{\mathsf{L}}_{i,j})
.

Theorem B
.

The algebras
\left(\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}\right)_{(\epsilon_{R})}

and

\big(\mathrm{gr}(\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n})\big)_{(\epsilon_{R})}
are
generated by the
\tilde{\mathsf{L}}_{i,j}

and the

\mathrm{gr}(\tilde{\mathsf{L}}_{i,j})

respectively,
for
1\leq i\leq k
and
1\leq j\leq l
.

It would be interesting to relate Theorem
B
to the quantized FFT of
[
LZZ11
, Thm 6.10]
for example by a deformation argument. However, we are unable to establish such a connection.

Our third main theorem
(Theorem
C
below)
explicitly describes the images in
\mathscr{PD}=\mathscr{PD}_{m\times n}
of the Cartan subalgebras of
U_{L}
and
U_{R}
. To state Theorem
C

we need to define certain elements of
\mathscr{PD}
that are constructed using
q
-determinants.
Let
\mathbf{i}:=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{r})
and
\mathbf{j}:=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{r})
be
r
-tuples of integers satisfying
1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{r}\leq m
and
1\leq j_{1}<\cdots<j_{r}\leq n
. Define
quantum minors
M^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{j}}\in\mathscr{P}

and

\overline{M}^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{j}}\in\mathscr{D}

by

(7)

M^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{j}}:=\sum_{\sigma}(-q)^{\ell(\sigma)}t_{i_{\sigma(1)},j_{1}}\cdots t_{i_{\sigma(r)},j_{r}}\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{M}^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{j}}:=\sum_{\sigma}(-q^{-1})^{\ell(\sigma)}\partial_{i_{\sigma(1)},j_{1}}\cdots\partial_{i_{\sigma(r)},j_{r}},

where the summations are over permutations in
r
letters, and
\ell(\sigma)
denotes the length of
\sigma
(in the sense of Coxeter groups).
For
a,b,r\geq 1
define
\mathbf{D}(r,a,b)\in\mathscr{PD}_{a\times b}
by

(8)

\mathbf{D}(r,a,b):=\sum_{\mathbf{i}}\sum_{\mathbf{j}}M^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{j}}{\overline{M}}^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{j}},

where the summation indices

\mathbf{i}:=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{r})
and
\mathbf{j}:=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{r})

satisfy

1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{r}\leq a
and

1\leq j_{1}<\cdots<j_{r}\leq b
.
We also set
\mathbf{D}(0,a,b)=1
.
For
0\leq r\leq n
and
1\leq k\leq n

we define
\mathbf{D}_{k,r}\in\mathscr{PD}_{m\times n}
by

\mathbf{D}_{k,r}:=\boldsymbol{e}_{m\times k}^{m\times n}\left({\mathbf{D}}(r,m,k)\right).

Similarly, for
0\leq r\leq m
and
1\leq k\leq m
we define
\mathbf{D}_{k,r}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{PD}_{m\times n}
by

\mathbf{D}^{\prime}_{k,r}:=\boldsymbol{e}_{k\times n}^{m\times n}\left(\mathbf{D}(r,k,n)\right).

Note that
\mathbf{D}_{k,r}=\mathbf{D}^{\prime}_{k,r}=0
when
r>\min\{k,m,n\}
and
\mathbf{D}_{k,0}=\mathbf{D}^{\prime}_{k,0}=1
.
Set

(9)

\mathbf{R}_{a}:=\sum_{r=0}^{a}(q^{2}-1)^{r}\mathbf{D}_{a,r}\ \text{ for }1\leq a\leq n\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{L}_{b}:=\sum_{r=0}^{b}(q^{2}-1)^{r}\mathbf{D}^{\prime}_{b,r}\ \text{ for }1\leq b\leq m.

Furthermore, let
U_{\mathfrak{h},L}
and
U_{\mathfrak{h},R}
denote the Cartan subalgebras of
U_{L}
and
U_{R}
, respectively (see Subsection
3.1
).
We set

(10)

\mathscr{L}_{\mathfrak{h}}:=\phi_{U}(U_{\mathfrak{h},L}\otimes 1)\quad,\quad\mathscr{L}_{\mathfrak{h},\bullet}:=\mathscr{PD}\cap\mathscr{L}_{\mathfrak{h}}\quad,\quad\mathscr{R}_{\mathfrak{h}}:=\phi_{U}(1\otimes U_{\mathfrak{h},R})\quad,\quad\mathscr{R}_{\mathfrak{h},\bullet}:=\mathscr{PD}\cap\mathscr{R}_{\mathfrak{h}}.

Theorem C
.

The following statements hold.

(i)

\mathscr{R}_{\mathfrak{h},\bullet}
is generated by
\mathbf{R}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{R}_{n}
.

(ii)

\mathscr{L}_{\mathfrak{h},\bullet}
is generated by
\mathbf{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{L}_{m}
.

In the proofs of our theorems we borrow at least two key ideas from
[
LZZ11
]
. First, we
use the bialgebra structure of
\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}
to define a map
{{}\Gamma}_{k,l,n}

from

\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}

onto the subalgebra of
U_{R}
-invariants
in
\mathrm{gr}(\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n})
(assuming
n\geq\max\{k,l\}
). The map

{{}\Gamma}_{k,l,n}
, given in Definition
7.1.3
, is similar to the map introduced in
[
LZZ11
, Lem. 6.11]
. Second, we define a new product on
\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}
such that the map
{{}\Gamma}_{k,l,n}
becomes an isomorphism of algebras (see Definition
8.2.4
). This product is analogous to the one defined in
[
LZZ11
, Lem. 6.13]
. However our product is given by a more complicated (and asymmetric) formula, because it needs to be simutaneously compatible with
two
universal
R
-matrices. As a consequence, establishing the desired properties of this product requires new ideas (see Section
8
).
Because of this, and the fact that unlike
[
LZZ11
]
the generators
\mathsf{L}_{i,j}
(respectively,
\mathsf{R}_{i,j}
) or even their graded analogues are not weight vectors for the Cartan subalgebras of the two copies of
U_{L}
(respectively,
U_{R}
) that act on
\mathscr{PD}
, the proofs of Theorems
A
and
B
become substantially more complicated than the analogous results of
[
LZZ11
]
. See Subsection
9.2
for more details.

The results of this paper were obtained as part of a project on Capelli operators for quantum symmetric spaces. From
this standpoint, it is natural to ask if one can define quantized Weyl algebras in the latter setting and then realize the action of (a large subalgebra of) the quantized enveloping algebra via elements of this Weyl algebra. We address this question and its connection to Capelli operators in upcoming work
[
LSS22a
,
LSS22b
]
.

The structure of this paper is as follows. In Section
2
we review the required background material on Hopf algebras and twisted tensor products. In Section
3
we construct and study the quantized Weyl algebra
\mathscr{PD}
and its variations, namely
\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr}}
,
\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}
and
\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}}
. The main goal of Section
4
is to
prove that under
\phi_{U}
the elements
K_{\lambda_{L,a}}\otimes 1
and
1\otimes K_{\lambda_{R,b}}
, defined in (
78
),
of the Cartan subalgebra of
U_{LR}
are mapped into
\mathscr{PD}
. In
Section
5
we compute explicit formulas for
\phi_{U}(K_{\lambda_{L,a}}\otimes 1)
and
\phi_{U}(1\otimes K_{\lambda_{R,b}})
.
Section
6
is devoted to some general properties of the polarization operators

\mathsf{L}_{i,j}
,
\mathsf{R}_{i,j}
and their variants.
In Section
7
we define the map
{{}\Gamma}_{k,l,n}
and establish some of its properties.
Section
8
defines
the new product on
\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}

and establishes its properties. Theorems
A

and
B
are proved in
Section
9
and
Theorem
C
is proved in Section
10
. Finally, Section
11
lists the commonly used notation in the paper.

2.
Hopf algebras and deformed twisted tensor products

Throughout this section
\mathbb{K}
will denote an arbitrary field and
H
will be a Hopf algebra over
\mathbb{K}
. We denote the coproduct, counit, and antipode of
H
by
\Delta
,
\epsilon
, and
S
. The opposite and co-opposite of
H
are denoted by
H^{\mathrm{op}}
and
H^{\mathrm{cop}}

(we use the same notation for bialgebras as well). Throughout the paper, our notation
for specific Hopf algebras
H

will remain consistent with these choices.

If
A
is an associative algebra and
V
is an
A
-module,
a subspace
W\subseteq V
is called
A
-stable
if
A\cdot W\subseteq W
.
Finally, an associative algebra
A
is called an
H
-module algebra
if it is equipped by an
H
-module structure such that the product of
A
yields an
H
-module homomorphism
A\otimes A\to A
.

2.1.
Local finiteness modulo an ideal

Given a two-sided ideal
I
of
H
(considered as an associative algebra), we set
E(x,I):=\left\{\mathrm{ad}_{y}(x)+I\,:\,y\in H\right\}

for
x\in H
, where
\mathrm{ad}_{y}(x):=\sum y_{1}xS(y_{2})
is the left adjoint action of
H
(we use the Sweedler notation
\Delta(y)=\sum y_{1}\otimes y_{2}
for the coproduct). We set

(11)

\mathscr{F}(H,I):=\left\{x\in H\,:\,\dim_{\mathbb{K}}E(x,I)<\infty\right\}.

For
I=0
this is the locally finite part of
H
(in the sense of
[
JL94
]
), which we will denote by
\mathscr{F}(H)
. We have
E(xx^{\prime},I)\subseteq E(x,I)E(x^{\prime},I)
for
x,x^{\prime}\in H
. Consequently,
\mathscr{F}(H,I)
is a subalgebra of
H
.

2.2.
The finite dual of
H

Given a finite dimensional left
H
-module
V
, by the
right dual
of
V
we mean
the dual space
V^{*}
equipped with the
H
-action defined by

\langle x\cdot v^{*},v\rangle:=\langle v^{*},S^{-1}(x)\cdot v\rangle
for
v^{*}\in V^{*}
and
v\in V
, where
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:V^{*}\otimes V\to\mathbb{K}
is the canonical pairing.
The
matrix coefficients
of
V

are the linear functionals
\mathsf{m}_{v^{*},v}\in H^{*}

defined by

\mathsf{m}_{v^{*},v}(x):=\langle v^{*},x\cdot v\rangle\quad\text{ for }x\in H,\ v\in V,\ v^{*}\in V^{*}.

Indeed
\mathsf{m}_{v^{*},v}\in H^{\circ}
, where
H^{\circ}
denotes the finite dual of
H
(for the definition of
H^{\circ}
see
[
KS97
, Sec. 1.2.8]
).
Recall that
H^{\circ}
has a canonical Hopf algebra structure. The product of
H^{\circ}
is given by

(12)

\lambda\mu(x):=\sum\lambda(x_{1})\mu(x_{2})\quad\text{for }\lambda,\mu\in H^{\circ}\text{ and }x\in H,

where
\Delta(x)=\sum x_{1}\otimes x_{2}
.
Given two finite dimensional
H
-modules

V
and
W
,
we have

\mathsf{m}_{v^{*}\otimes w^{*},v\otimes w}=\mathsf{m}_{v^{*},v}\mathsf{m}_{w^{*},w}\quad\text{for }v,w\in V\text{ and }v^{*},w^{*}\in W.

Let
\Delta^{\circ}
denote the coproduct of
H^{\circ}
, so that
\Delta^{\circ}(\lambda)=\sum\lambda_{1}\otimes\lambda_{2}
for
\lambda\in H^{\circ}
, where
\sum\lambda_{1}\otimes\lambda_{2}
is uniquely determined by

(13)

\langle\lambda,xy\rangle=\sum\langle\lambda_{1},x\rangle\langle\lambda_{2},y\rangle\quad\text{for }x,y\in H.

If
\{v_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d}
is a basis of
V
and
\{v_{i}^{*}\}_{i=1}^{d}
is the dual basis of
V^{*}
, then

\Delta^{\circ}(\mathsf{m}_{v^{*},v})=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathsf{m}_{v^{*},v_{i}}\otimes\mathsf{m}_{v_{i}^{*},v}
.
The following remark will be used in Section
3
.

Remark 2.2.1
.

Let
H^{\bullet}\subseteq H^{\circ}
be a sub-bialgebra. Then
H^{\bullet}
is an
H
-module algebra with respect to
right translation
, where the action is defined by
\langle x\cdot\lambda,y\rangle:=\langle\lambda,yx\rangle
for
\lambda\in H^{\bullet}
and
x,y\in H
.
If
H
is equipped with a
\mathbb{K}
-linear map
x\mapsto x^{\natural}
that yields an isomorphism of Hopf algebras
H\to H^{\mathrm{op}}
, then
H^{\bullet}
has another
H
-module algebra structure defined by

\langle x\cdot\lambda,y\rangle:=\langle\lambda,x^{\natural}y\rangle
, which we call
left translation
.
Given any homomorphism of associative algebras

\tau:H\to H
, we can define
\tau
-twisted left and right translation actions of
H
on
H^{\bullet}
, given respectively by the formulas

\langle x\cdot\lambda,y\rangle:=\langle\lambda,\tau(x)^{\natural}y\rangle\quad\text{and}\quad\langle x\cdot\lambda,y\rangle:=\langle\lambda,y\tau(x)\rangle.

When
H^{\bullet}
is equipped with either one of the two
\tau
-twisted actions, the following statements hold.

(i)

If
\tau:H\to H
is a homomorphism of coalgebras, then
H^{\bullet}
is an
H
-module algebra.

(ii)

If
\tau:H\to H
is an anti-homomorphism of coalgebras, then
H^{\bullet}
is an
H^{\mathrm{cop}}
-module algebra.

2.3.
The isotypic component of the trivial
H
-module

For any
H
-module
V
we set

V_{(\epsilon)}:=\{v\in V\,:\,h\cdot v=\epsilon(h)v\},

where as before
\epsilon
denotes the counit of
H
.
Let
\psi:H\to\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{K}}(V)
be the algebra homomorphism corresponding to this module structure (hence
h\cdot v=\psi(h)v
for
h\in H
and
v\in V
).
We equip
\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{K}}(V)
with an
H
-module structure, defined by
h\cdot T:=\sum\psi(h_{1})T\psi(S(h_{2}))
for
h\in H
and
T\in\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{K}}(V)
, where
\Delta(h)=\sum h_{1}\otimes h_{2}
.

Lemma 2.3.1
.

\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{K}}(V)_{(\epsilon)}=\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{K}}(V)^{\psi(H)}
, where the right hand side is defined as in (
3
).

Proof.

The inclusion
\supseteq
follows from

h\cdot T=\sum\psi(h_{1})T\psi(S(h_{2}))=T\sum\psi(h_{1})\psi(S(h_{2}))=T\psi\left(\sum h_{1}S(h_{2})\right)=\epsilon(h)T,

for
T\in\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{K}}(V)^{\psi(H)}
.
For the inclusion
\subseteq
note that if
T\in\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{K}}(V)_{(\epsilon)}

then

\displaystyle\psi(h)T=\sum\psi(h_{1})\epsilon(h_{2})T

\displaystyle=\sum\psi(h_{1})T\psi(S(h_{2}))\psi(h_{3})

\displaystyle=\sum\epsilon(h_{1})T\psi(h_{2})=T\psi\left(\sum\epsilon(h_{1})h_{2}\right)=T\psi(h).\qed

2.4.
Braided triples, twisted tensor products and their deformations

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a full subcategory of the category of
H
-modules that
is closed under direct sums and tensor products. To ensure that
\mathcal{C}
is a monoidal category we assume that the trivial
H
-module (the one-dimensional vector space
\mathbb{K}
equipped with the action
h\mapsto\epsilon(h)
for
h\in H
) belongs to
\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
.

Assume that
\mathcal{C}
is
braided
. The braiding

\check{R}
of
\mathcal{C}
is a natural family of
H
-module isomorphisms

\check{R}_{V,W}:V\otimes W\to W\otimes V\quad\text{for $V,W\in\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{C})$}

that satisfies the usual hexagon axioms (see for example
[
EGNO15
, Def. 8.1.1]
).
Henceforth we call
(H,\mathcal{C},\check{R})
a
braided triple
.

Let
A
and
B
be two
H
-module algebras, with products
m_{A}
and
m_{B}
. Assume that
A,B\in\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
.

Lemma 2.4.1
.

The map
\check{R}_{B,A}:B\otimes A\to A\otimes B
satisfies the following relations:

(i)

\check{R}_{B,A}(1\otimes a)=a\otimes 1
for
a\in A
and

\check{R}_{B,A}(b\otimes 1)=1\otimes b
for
b\in B
.

(ii)

\check{R}_{B,A}\circ(\mathrm{id}_{B}\otimes m_{A})=(m_{A}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{B})(\mathrm{id}_{A}\otimes\check{R}_{B,A})(\check{R}_{B,A}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{A})
.

(iii)

\check{R}_{B,A}\circ(m_{B}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{A})=(\mathrm{id}_{A}\otimes m_{B})(\check{R}_{B,A}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{B})(\mathrm{id}_{B}\otimes\check{R}_{B,A})
.

Proof.

This is well known, but we supply a proof because we did not find a reference.

(i) Equip
\mathbb{K}
with the canonical
H
-module structure induced by the counit

\epsilon:H\to\mathbb{K}
.
It is well known (see for example
[
EGNO15
, Exer. 8.1.6]
) that one has a commuting triangle

\textstyle{\mathbb{K}\otimes A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\check{R}_{\mathbb{K},A}}
\textstyle{A\otimes\mathbb{K}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A}

where the lower sides of the triangle are the canonical left and right unit isomorphisms. It follows immediately that
\check{R}_{\mathbb{K},A}(1\otimes a)=a\otimes 1
. Furthermore, by naturality of
\check{R}
the diagram

\textstyle{\mathbb{K}\otimes A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\check{R}_{\mathbb{K},A}}
\scriptstyle{1_{B}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{A}}
\textstyle{A\otimes\mathbb{K}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}_{A}\otimes 1_{B}}
\textstyle{B\otimes A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\check{R}_{B,A}}
\textstyle{A\otimes B}

is commutative, hence
\check{R}_{B,A}(1\otimes a)=a\otimes 1
. The other
relation is proved similarly.

(ii) Consider the diagram of maps below:

\textstyle{A\otimes B\otimes A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}_{A}\otimes\check{R}_{B,A}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces B\otimes A\otimes A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\ \check{R}_{B,A}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\check{R}_{B,A\otimes A}}
\scriptstyle{\ \ \mathrm{id}_{B}\otimes m_{A}}
\textstyle{B\otimes A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\check{R}_{B,A}}
\textstyle{A\otimes A\otimes B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\ \ m_{A}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{B}}
\textstyle{A\otimes B}

The square is commutative by naturality of
\check{R}
(because
m_{A}
and
m_{B}
are
H
-module homomorphisms) and the triangle is commutative by the hexagon axiom. The assertion of (ii) follows from comparing two maps
B\otimes A\otimes A\to A\otimes B
in the diagram: one is obtained by composition of the top and the right edges of the square, the other is obtained by the outer edges of the triangle and the bottom edge of the square.

(iii) Similar to (ii).
∎

Definition 2.4.2
.

Let
A,B\in\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
be
H
-module algebras. We denote the products of
A
and
B
by
m_{A}
and
m_{B}
, respectively.
The

\check{R}
-twisted tensor product
of
A
and
B
, denoted by
A\otimes_{\check{R}}B
, is the vector space
A\otimes B
equipped with the binary operation

(14)

(m_{A}\otimes m_{B})\circ(\mathrm{id}_{A}\otimes\check{R}_{B,A}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{B}).

Proposition 2.4.3
.

A\otimes_{\check{R}}B

is an associative algebra.

Proof.

This is well known and proved for example in
[
VV94
, Prop. 2.2]
.
∎

From Lemma
2.4.1
(i) it follows that
for
a,a^{\prime}\in A
and
b,b^{\prime}\in B
the product (
14
) satisfies

(15)

(a\otimes 1)(a^{\prime}\otimes b)(1\otimes b^{\prime})=(aa^{\prime}\otimes bb^{\prime}).

Remark 2.4.4
.

The vector space
A\otimes_{\check{R}}B=A\otimes B
carries two module structures: as the outer tensor product
over
\mathbb{K}

of
H
-modules
A
and
B
, it is
an

H\otimes H
-module. As the inner tensor product of
A
and
B
, it is an
H
-module. Of course the latter
H
-module structure is obtained from the former one via restriction along the coproduct map
H\to H\otimes H
.

Proposition 2.4.5
.

A\otimes_{\check{R}}B
is an
H
-module algebra with the
H
-module structure of
Remark
2.4.4
.

Proof.

Given

a,a^{\prime}\in A
and
b,b^{\prime}\in B
, if we write
\check{R}_{B,A}(b\otimes a^{\prime})=\sum a^{\prime\prime}\otimes b^{\prime\prime}
then for
x\in H
we have

\displaystyle x\cdot\big((a\otimes b)(a^{\prime}\otimes b^{\prime})\big)

\displaystyle=x\cdot\sum aa^{\prime\prime}\otimes b^{\prime\prime}b=\sum(x_{1}\cdot(aa^{\prime\prime}))\otimes(x_{2}\cdot(b^{\prime\prime}b)).

Since
A
and
B
are
H
-module algebras, from the latter equalities and (
15
) it follows that

\displaystyle x\cdot\big((a\otimes b)(a^{\prime}\otimes b^{\prime})\big)

\displaystyle=\sum(x_{1}\cdot a)(x_{2}\cdot a^{\prime\prime})\otimes(x_{3}\cdot b^{\prime\prime})(x_{4}\cdot b)

\displaystyle=\sum((x_{1}\cdot a)\otimes 1)((x_{2}\cdot a^{\prime\prime})\otimes\big(x_{3}\cdot b^{\prime\prime})\big)(1\otimes(x_{4}\cdot b)).

Since

\check{R}_{A,B}
is an
H
-module isomorphism, from the latter equalities and (
15
)
we have

\displaystyle x\cdot\big((a\otimes b)(a^{\prime}\otimes b^{\prime})\big)

\displaystyle=\sum((x_{1}\cdot a)\otimes 1)\big(\check{R}_{B,A}\big((x_{2}\cdot b)\otimes(x_{3}\cdot a^{\prime})\big)\big)(1\otimes(x_{4}\cdot b^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\sum((x_{1}\cdot a)\otimes(x_{2}\cdot b))((x_{3}\cdot a^{\prime})\otimes(x_{4}\cdot b^{\prime}))=\sum(x_{1}\cdot(a\otimes b))(x_{2}\cdot(a^{\prime}\otimes b^{\prime})).

Thus
A\otimes_{\check{R}}B
is an
H
-module algebra.
∎

Let
E_{A}\subseteq A
and
E_{B}\subseteq B
be subspaces that generate
A
and
B
, respectively. Thus

A\cong T(E_{A})/I_{A}
and
B\cong T(E_{B})/I_{B}
, where
T(X)
denotes the tensor algebra on
X
, and

I_{A}
and
I_{B}
denote the corresponding ideals of relations.
We assume that
E_{A}
and
E_{B}
are
H
-stable.

Remark 2.4.6
.

The
H
-module structures
on
E_{A}
and
E_{B}
equip
T(E_{A})
and
T(E_{B})
with canonical
H
-module algebra structures. It is straightforward to verify that the maps
T(E_{A})\to A
and
T(E_{B})\to B
are
H
-module homomorphisms. In particular,
I_{A}
and
I_{B}
are
H
-stable subspaces of
T(E_{A})
and
T(E_{B})
, respectively.

Consider the linear map

\gamma_{A,B}:E_{A}\otimes E_{B}\to T(E_{A}\oplus E_{B})\ ,\ \gamma_{A,B}(a\otimes b):=ab.

Note that we can express
\gamma_{A,B}
as the composition

(16)

E_{A}\otimes E_{B}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{i}_{A}\otimes\mathsf{i}_{B}}T(E_{A}\oplus E_{B})\otimes T(E_{A}\oplus E_{B})\xrightarrow{\ a\otimes b\mapsto ab\ }T(E_{A}\oplus E_{B}),

where
\mathsf{i}_{A}:E_{A}\to T(E_{A}\oplus E_{B})
and
\mathsf{i}_{B}:E_{B}\to T(E_{A}\oplus E_{B})
are canonical embeddings.
For the next lemma, recall that the
H
-module structure on
E_{A}\oplus E_{B}
induces a canonical
H
-module algebra structure on
T(E_{A}\oplus E_{B})
.

Lemma 2.4.7
.

\gamma_{A,B}
is an
H
-module homomorphism.

Proof.

Since
\mathsf{i}_{A}
,
\mathsf{i}_{B}
and the product of
T(E_{A}\oplus E_{B})
are
H
-module homomorphisms, the assertion follows from the description of
\gamma_{A,B}
in (
16
).
∎

By the universal property of tensor algebras the map
E_{A}\oplus E_{B}\to A\otimes_{\check{R}}B
given by the assignment
a\oplus b\mapsto a\otimes 1+1\otimes b

induces a homomorphism of algebras

\pi:T(E_{A}\oplus E_{B})\to A\otimes_{\check{R}}B.

Proposition 2.4.8
.

Let
A
,
B
,
E_{A}
and
E_{B}
be as above.
Then
\pi
induces an isomorphism of algebras

T(E_{A}\oplus E_{B})/I_{A,B}\cong A\otimes_{\check{R}}B,

where
I_{A,B}
denotes the two-sided ideal of
T(E_{A}\oplus E_{B})
generated by
I_{A}
,
I_{B}
and the relations

(17)

ba-\gamma_{A,B}\circ\check{R}_{B,A}(b\otimes a)\quad\text{for }a\in E_{A},\ b\in E_{B}.

Proof.

We have
\pi(E_{A}\oplus E_{B})=E_{A,B}
where
E_{A,B}:=(E_{A}\otimes 1)\oplus(1\otimes E_{B})
. Furthermore,
A\otimes_{\check{R}}B
is generated as an algebra by
E_{A,B}
. Thus
\pi
is a surjection.
Next we prove that
\pi
is an injection.
From the definition of the product of
A\otimes_{\check{R}}B
it follows that
I_{A,B}\subseteq\ker\pi
. Thus, to complete the proof it suffices to verify the reverse inclusion.

By the relations (
17
), every element of

T(E_{A}\oplus E_{B})/I_{A,B}

can be expressed as a linear combination of products of elements of
E_{A}
and
E_{B}
in which elements of
E_{A}
occur before elements of
E_{B}
. Thus,

(18)

T(E_{A}\oplus E_{B})=T(E_{A})T(E_{B})+I_{A,B}.

Now choose
\{a_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathcal{I}_{A}}\subseteq T(E_{A})

and
\{b_{\beta}\}_{\beta\in\mathcal{I}_{B}}\subseteq T(E_{B})

such that
\{I_{A}+a_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathcal{I}_{A}}
is a basis of
T(E_{A})/I_{A}\cong A
and

\{I_{B}+b_{\beta}\}_{\beta\in\mathcal{I}_{B}}
is a basis of
T(E_{B})/I_{B}\cong B
.
From (
18
) it follows that the elements
I_{A,B}+a_{\alpha}b_{\beta}
for
\alpha\in I_{A}
and
\beta\in I_{B}
constitute a spanning set of
T(E_{A}\oplus E_{B})/I_{A,B}
.

From (
15
) it follows that
\pi(a_{\alpha})=\overline{a}_{\alpha}\otimes 1
for some
\overline{a}_{\alpha}\in A
and
\pi(b_{\beta})=1\otimes\overline{b}_{\beta}
for some
\overline{b}_{\beta}\in B
.
Also,
\pi(I_{A,B}+a_{\alpha}b_{\beta})=\overline{a}_{\alpha}\otimes\overline{b}_{\beta}
.
The sets
\{\overline{a}_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathcal{I}_{A}}
and

\{\overline{b}_{\beta}\}_{\beta\in\mathcal{I}_{B}}
are bases of
A
and
B
, respectively.
Thus
\pi
maps a spanning set of
T(E_{A}\oplus E_{B})/I_{A,B}
bijectively onto the basis

\{a_{\alpha}\otimes b_{\beta}\,:\,\alpha\in\mathcal{I}_{A},\beta\in\mathcal{I}_{B}\}

of
A\otimes_{\check{R}}B
. It follows that
\ker\pi\subseteq I_{A,B}
.
∎

Let
\psi:E_{B}\times E_{A}\to\mathbb{K}
be an
H
-invariant bilinear form, that is

(19)

\sum\psi(x_{1}\cdot b,x_{2}\cdot a)=\epsilon(x)\psi(b,a)\quad\text{for }a\in E_{A},\ b\in E_{B},\ x\in H,

where as before
\epsilon:H\to\mathbb{K}
denotes the counit of
H
. Let
I_{A,B,\psi}
denote
the two-sided ideal of
T(E_{A}\oplus E_{B})
that is generated by
I_{A}
,
I_{B}
, and relations of the form

(20)

ba-\gamma_{A,B}\circ\check{R}_{B,A}(b\otimes a)-\psi(b,a)\quad\text{for }a\in E_{A},\ b\in E_{B}.

Definition 2.4.9
.

We call the algebra

A\otimes_{\check{R},\psi}B:=T(E_{A}\oplus E_{B})/I_{A,B,\psi}
the

\psi
-deformed
\check{R}
-twisted tensor product
of
A
and
B
relative to
E_{A}
and
E_{B}
.

The
H
-module structure of
E_{A}\oplus E_{B}
equips
T(E_{A}\oplus E_{B})
with a canonical
H
-module algebra structure. We have the following statement.

Proposition 2.4.10
.

The canonical
H
-module algebra structure on
T(E_{A}\oplus E_{B})
descends to an
H
-module algebra structure on

A\otimes_{\check{R},\psi}B
.

Proof.

It suffices to verify that
I_{A,B,\psi}
is an
H
-stable subspace. By Remark
2.4.6

the subspaces
I_{A}
and
I_{B}
of
T(E_{A}\oplus E_{B})
are
H
-stable.
Thus it remains to show that the relations (
20
) span an
H
-submodule of
T(E_{A}\oplus E_{B})
.

Any
x\in H
acts on
T^{0}(E_{A}\oplus E_{B})\cong\mathbb{K}
by
\epsilon(x)
.
Next let
a\in E_{A}
,
b\in E_{B}
and
x\in H
.
By (
19
) and Lemma
2.4.7
we have

\displaystyle x\cdot\big(ba

\displaystyle-\gamma_{A,B}\circ\check{R}_{B,A}(b\otimes a)-\psi(b,a)\big)

\displaystyle=\sum\big((x_{1}\cdot b)(x_{2}\cdot a)-\gamma_{A,B}\circ\check{R}_{B,A}(x_{1}\cdot b\otimes x_{2}\cdot a)-\psi(x_{1}\cdot b,x_{2}\cdot a)\big),

which is a sum of relations of the form (
20
).
∎

By the universal property of tensor algebras the maps
\mathsf{i}_{A}
and
\mathsf{i}_{B}
induce embeddings of associative algebras

\mathsf{i}_{A}:T(E_{A})\to T(E_{A}\oplus E_{B})
and
\mathsf{i}_{B}:T(E_{B})\to T(E_{A}\oplus E_{B})
. The latter maps induce

H
-equivariant homomorphisms of associative algebras

A\cong T(E_{A})/I_{A}\xrightarrow{\ \ \bar{\mathsf{i}}_{A}\ \ }A\otimes_{\check{R},\psi}B\quad\text{ and }\quad B\cong T(E_{B})/I_{B}\xrightarrow{\ \ \bar{\mathsf{i}}_{B}\ \ }A\otimes_{\check{R},\psi}B.

By tensoring the latter maps and then composing with the products of the algebras
T(E_{A}\oplus E_{B})
and
A\otimes_{\check{R},\psi}B
we obtain the following
commutative diagram:

\textstyle{T(E_{A})\otimes T(E_{B})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{i}_{A}\otimes\mathsf{i}_{B}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}
\textstyle{T(E_{A}\oplus E_{B})\otimes T(E_{A}\oplus E_{B})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pta\otimes b\mapsto ab}
\textstyle{T(E_{A}\oplus E_{B})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\otimes B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{\mathsf{i}}_{A}\otimes\bar{\mathsf{i}}_{B}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}
\textstyle{(A\otimes_{\check{R},\psi}B)\otimes(A\otimes_{\check{R},\psi}B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pta\otimes b\mapsto ab}
\textstyle{A\otimes_{\check{R},\psi}B}

In the above diagram the vertical maps are the canonical quotients.

Remark 2.4.11
.

From Remark
2.4.6

it follows that

\mathsf{i}_{A}\otimes\mathsf{i}_{B}

and

\bar{\mathsf{i}}_{A}\otimes\bar{\mathsf{i}}_{B}
are
H\otimes H
-module homomorphisms. Also, the products of

T(E_{A}\oplus E_{B})
and
A\otimes_{\check{R},\psi}B

are
H
-module homomorphisms
(see
Proposition

2.4.10
).
Thus the composition of the bottom horizontal maps in (
2.4
), which is given by

(25)

A\otimes B\to A\otimes_{\check{R},\psi}B\ ,\ a\otimes b\mapsto I_{A,B,\psi}+ab,

is an
H
-module homomorphism (recall that an
H\otimes H
-module homomorphism is also an
H
-module homomorphism by restriction along the coproduct map

H\to H\otimes H
).

2.5.
Locally finite braided triples and their products

Given a braided triple

(H,\mathcal{C},\check{R})
we set

(26)

R_{V,W}:=\sigma_{W,V}\circ\check{R}_{V,W},

where

\sigma_{W,V}:W\otimes V\to V\otimes W\quad,\quad w\otimes v\mapsto v\otimes w

is the standard flip map.

Definition 2.5.1
.

We say a braided triple
(H,\mathcal{C},\check{R})
is
locally finite
if it satisfies the following conditions.

(i)

Every
V\in\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
is a sum of its finite dimensional submodules that belong to
\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
.

(ii)

For finite dimensional modules
V,W\in\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
there exist
\omega_{V,W},\overline{\omega}_{V,W}\in H\otimes H
such that

R_{V,W}(v\otimes w)=\omega_{V,W}\cdot(v\otimes w)\quad\text{and}\quad R_{V,W}^{-1}(v\otimes w)=\overline{\omega}_{V,W}\cdot(v\otimes w)\quad\text{ for }v\otimes w\in V\otimes W.

Let us briefly explain the idea behind Definition
2.5.1
. The braiding of the category of modules of the Hopf algebra
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})

is given by a formal series
that does not belong to
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})\otimes U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})

(see Subsection
3.2
). Thus, in order to give a rigorous proof of Proposition
2.6.2
below, we need
to be able to replace this formal series locally by a finite 2-tensor.

Lemma 2.5.2
.

Let
(H,\mathcal{C},\check{R})
be a locally finite braided triple and let
V_{1},\ldots,V_{r},W_{1},\ldots,W_{r}\in\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
be finite dimensional modules.
Let
\omega_{V,W},\overline{\omega}_{V,W}\in H\otimes H
be chosen as in Definition
2.5.1
(ii),
where
V:=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}V_{i}

and

W:=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}W_{i}
.
Then

R_{V_{i},W_{j}}(v_{i}\otimes w_{j})=\omega_{V,W}\cdot(v_{i}\otimes w_{j})\quad\text{and}\quad R^{-1}_{V_{i},W_{j}}(v_{i}\otimes w_{j})=\overline{\omega}_{V,W}\cdot(v_{i}\otimes w_{j}),

for all
v_{i}\in V_{i}
and
w_{j}\in W_{j}
where

1\leq i,j\leq r
.

Proof.

This
follows from naturality of
\check{R}
with respect to the canonical maps
V_{i}\otimes W_{j}\hookrightarrow V\otimes W
.
∎

We need to work with the braided triples associated to tensor products of Hopf algebras
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
. To this end, we need the following definition.

Definition 2.5.3
.

Let
(H,\mathcal{C},\check{R})
and

(H^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime},\check{R}^{\prime})
be locally finite braided triples.
Set
H^{\prime\prime}:=H\otimes H^{\prime}
and equip
H^{\prime\prime}
with the canonical tensor product Hopf algebra structure.
Furthermore, let
\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}
be the full subcategory of the category of
H^{\prime\prime}
-modules whose objects are direct sums of modules of the form
V\otimes V^{\prime}
where
V\in\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
and
V^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})
. We define a braiding on
\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}
as follows. First, for
V,W\in\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
and

V^{\prime},W^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})

we set

(27)

\check{R}^{\prime\prime}_{V\otimes V^{\prime},W\otimes W^{\prime}}:=\left(\check{R}_{V,W}\right)_{13}\circ\left(\check{R}^{\prime}_{V^{\prime},W^{\prime}}\right)_{24}.

Here

\left(\check{R}^{\prime}_{V^{\prime},W^{\prime}}\right)_{24}
means that
\check{R}^{\prime}_{V^{\prime},W^{\prime}}
acts on the
2^{\mathrm{nd}}
and the
4^{\mathrm{th}}
components of
(V\otimes V^{\prime})\otimes(W\otimes W^{\prime})
, resulting in a map

(V\otimes V^{\prime})\otimes(W\otimes W^{\prime})\to(V\otimes W^{\prime})\otimes(W\otimes V^{\prime}),

and
\big(\check{R}_{V,W}\big)_{13}
is defined analogously.
Next for
\widetilde{V}:=\bigoplus_{i}V_{i}\otimes V^{\prime}_{i}

and

\widetilde{W}:=\bigoplus_{j}W_{j}\otimes W^{\prime}_{j}

we define

\check{R}^{\prime\prime}_{\widetilde{V},\widetilde{W}}:=\bigoplus_{i,j}\check{R}^{\prime\prime}_{V_{i}\otimes V^{\prime}_{i},W_{j}\otimes W_{j}^{\prime}}.

Proposition 2.5.4
.

Let

H^{\prime\prime}
,
\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}
and
\check{R}^{\prime\prime}
be as in
Definition
2.5.3
. Then

(H^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime},\check{R}^{\prime\prime})
is a locally finite braided triple.

Proof.

It is trivial to check that
(H^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime},\check{R}^{\prime\prime})
meets the condition of Definition
2.5.1
(i).
Next we show that
(H^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime},\check{R}^{\prime\prime})
is a braided triple. It is straightforward to check that
\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}
is closed with respect to tensor products and arbitrary direct sums. The hexagon axioms for
\check{R}^{\prime\prime}
follow from those for
\check{R}
and
\check{R}^{\prime}
.
Next we verify naturality of
\check{R}^{\prime\prime}
. Since
\check{R}^{\prime\prime}
is defined by expansion on direct sums of modules,
it suffices to prove commutativity of the
diagram

\textstyle{(V\otimes V^{\prime})\otimes(W\otimes W^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\check{R}_{V\otimes V^{\prime},W\otimes W^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{f_{V}\otimes f_{W}}
\textstyle{(W\otimes W^{\prime})\otimes(V\otimes V^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{W}\otimes f_{V}}
\textstyle{(\underline{V}\otimes\underline{V}^{\prime})\otimes(\underline{W}\otimes\underline{W}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\check{R}_{\underline{V}\otimes\underline{V}^{\prime},\underline{W}\otimes\underline{W}^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{(\underline{W}\otimes\underline{W}^{\prime})\otimes(\underline{V}\otimes\underline{V}^{\prime})}

for all choices of
V,W,\underline{V},\underline{W}\in\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
,
V^{\prime},W^{\prime},\underline{V}^{\prime},\underline{W}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})
,
f_{V}\in\mathrm{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}}(V\otimes V^{\prime},\underline{V}\otimes\underline{V}^{\prime})
and
f_{W}\in\mathrm{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}}(W\otimes W^{\prime},\underline{W}\otimes\underline{W}^{\prime})
. The subtlety here is that we cannot assume that
f_{V}
and
f_{W}
can be decomposed into tensor products of maps on the tensor components.
Since
\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}
satisfies the condition of Definition
2.5.1
(i), using naturality of
\check{R}
and
\check{R}^{\prime}
we can assume that all of the modules in the commutative diagram are finite dimensional.
Using Lemma
2.5.2
for
the triples
(H,\mathcal{C},\check{R})
and
(H^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime},\check{R}^{\prime})
it follows that

R_{V\otimes V^{\prime},W\otimes W^{\prime}}

and
R_{\underline{V}\otimes\underline{V}^{\prime},\underline{W}\otimes\underline{W}^{\prime}}
are given by
the left action of the same 2-tensor
in
H^{\prime\prime}\otimes H^{\prime\prime}
. Commutativity of the diagram follows immediately.

Finally, we show that
the condition of Definition
2.5.1
(ii) holds.
If
R_{V,W}
and
R^{\prime}_{V^{\prime},W^{\prime}}
are equal to the actions of
\omega_{V,W}\in H\otimes H
and
\omega^{\prime}_{V^{\prime},W^{\prime}}\in H^{\prime}\otimes H^{\prime}
respectively, then
R^{\prime\prime}_{V\otimes V^{\prime},W\otimes W^{\prime}}
is equal to the action of

(\omega_{V,W})_{13}(\omega^{\prime}_{V^{\prime},W^{\prime}})_{24}\in H^{\prime\prime}\otimes H^{\prime\prime}.

This verifies
Definition
2.5.1
(ii)
for pairs of
H^{\prime\prime}
-modules in
\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}
of the form
V\otimes V^{\prime}
and
W\otimes W^{\prime}
. Using Lemma
2.5.2
for
the triples
(H,\mathcal{C},\check{R})
and
(H^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime},\check{R}^{\prime})
, the claim follows
for general
H^{\prime\prime}
-modules in
\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}
. A similar argument can be given for

\big(R^{\prime\prime}_{V\otimes V^{\prime},W\otimes W^{\prime}}\big)^{-1}
.
∎

2.6.
A result on
H^{\prime\prime}
-stable subalgebras of
A\otimes_{\check{R}^{\prime\prime}}B

We continue with the notation of
Definition
2.5.3
.
Let
A
and
B
be two
H^{\prime\prime}
-module algebras such that
A,B\in\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime})
. Recall from Remark
2.4.4
and Proposition
2.4.5
that
A\otimes_{\check{R}^{\prime\prime}}B
is an
H^{\prime\prime}
-module algebra and also an
H^{\prime\prime}\otimes H^{\prime\prime}
-module (but not necessarily an
H^{\prime\prime}\otimes H^{\prime\prime}
-module algebra). For
\omega\in H\otimes H
let

T_{\omega}:A\otimes_{\check{R}^{\prime\prime}}B\to A\otimes_{\check{R}^{\prime\prime}}B

denote the linear endomorphism obtained by the action of
\omega_{13}
. Here by definition
(x\otimes y)_{13}:=x\otimes 1\otimes y\otimes 1
for
x,y\in H
.

Lemma 2.6.1
.

Let

(H,\mathcal{C},\check{R})
and

(H^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime},\check{R}^{\prime})
be locally finite braided triples and let

(H^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime},\check{R}^{\prime\prime})
be defined as in
Definition
2.5.3
. Let
A,B\in\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime})
be
H^{\prime\prime}
-module algebras. Let
V^{\prime\prime}_{A}\subseteq A
and
V^{\prime\prime}_{B}\subseteq B
be finite dimensional
H^{\prime\prime}
-submodules such that

V^{\prime\prime}_{A},V^{\prime\prime}_{B}\in\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime})
.
We decompose
V_{A}^{\prime\prime}
and
V_{B}^{\prime\prime}

as

V_{A}^{\prime\prime}=\bigoplus_{i\in\Omega_{A}}V_{i,A}\otimes V_{i,A}^{\prime}\quad\text{and}\quad V_{B}^{\prime\prime}=\bigoplus_{j\in\Omega_{B}}V_{j,B}\otimes V_{j,B}^{\prime},

where
V_{i,A},V_{j,B}\in\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
and

V^{\prime}_{i,A},V^{\prime}_{j,B}\in\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})
for
i\in\Omega_{A}
and
j\in\Omega_{B}
(here
\Omega_{A}
and
\Omega_{B}
are index sets).
Let
\omega_{V,W},\overline{\omega}_{V,W}\in H\otimes H
be chosen as in Definition
2.5.1
(ii)
where

V:=\bigoplus_{i\in\Omega_{A}}V_{i,A}\quad\text{and}\quad W:=\bigoplus_{j\in\Omega_{B}}V_{j,B}.

For
x,y\in H
let
\omega_{x,y}\in H\otimes H
be defined by
\omega_{x,y}:=\overline{\omega}_{V,W}(y\otimes x)\omega_{V,W}
.
Then

\left((\check{R}^{\prime\prime}_{B,A})^{-1}\circ T_{x\otimes y}\circ\check{R}^{\prime\prime}_{B,A}\right)\Big|_{V^{\prime\prime}_{B}\otimes V^{\prime\prime}_{A}}=T_{\omega_{x,y}}\quad\text{for }1\leq i\leq r.

Proof.

By naturality of
\check{R}^{\prime\prime}
it follows that

\left(\check{R}^{\prime\prime}_{B,A}\right)\big|_{V^{\prime\prime}_{B}\otimes V^{\prime\prime}_{A}}=\check{R}^{\prime\prime}_{V^{\prime\prime}_{B},V^{\prime\prime}_{A}}\quad\text{and}\quad\left(\check{R}^{\prime\prime}_{B,A}\right)^{-1}\big|_{V^{\prime\prime}_{A}\otimes V^{\prime\prime}_{B}}=\left(\check{R}^{\prime\prime}_{V^{\prime\prime}_{B},V^{\prime\prime}_{A}}\right)^{-1}.

Thus
from (
27
) it follows that

\left((\check{R}^{\prime\prime}_{B,A})^{-1}\circ T_{x\otimes y}\circ\check{R}^{\prime\prime}_{B,A}\right)\big|_{V_{j,B}\otimes V^{\prime}_{j,B}\otimes V_{i,A}\otimes V^{\prime}_{i,A}}=\left(\check{R}_{V_{j,B},V_{i,A}}\right)^{-1}_{13}\circ T_{x\otimes y}\circ\left(\check{R}_{V_{j,B},V_{i,A}}\right)_{13}.

The relation (
26
)
and Lemma
2.5.2
for the triple
(H,\mathcal{C},\check{R})
imply
the assertion of the lemma.
∎

Proposition 2.6.2
.

Let

(H,\mathcal{C},\check{R})
and

(H^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime},\check{R}^{\prime})
be locally finite braided triples and let

(H^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime},\check{R}^{\prime\prime})
be defined as in
Definition
2.5.3
.
Let
A,B\in\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime})
be
H^{\prime\prime}
-module algebras. Let

\mathcal{E}
be a subspace of
A\otimes_{\check{R}^{\prime\prime}}B
and let
\mathcal{A}
denote the subalgebra of

A\otimes_{\check{R}^{\prime\prime}}B
that is generated by
\mathcal{E}
.
Finally, let

\overline{H}
be a sub-bialgebra of
H
.

(i)

Assume that for every pair of finite dimensional modules
V,W\in\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
, we can choose

\omega_{V,W},\overline{\omega}_{V,W}\in\overline{H}\otimes H
that satisfy Definition
2.5.1
(ii). If

\mathcal{E}
is stable under the action of the subalgebra
H\otimes 1\otimes\overline{H}\otimes 1
of
H^{\prime\prime}\otimes H^{\prime\prime}
, then so is
\mathcal{A}
.

(ii)

Assume that for every pair of finite dimensional modules
V,W\in\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
, we can choose

\omega_{V,W},\overline{\omega}_{V,W}\in H\otimes\overline{H}
that satisfy Definition
2.5.1
(ii). If

\mathcal{E}
is stable under the action of the subalgebra
\overline{H}\otimes 1\otimes H\otimes 1
of
H^{\prime\prime}\otimes H^{\prime\prime}
, then so is
\mathcal{A}
.

Proof.

For
r\geq 0
set
\mathcal{A}_{r}:=\mathrm{Span}\left\{w^{(1)}\cdots w^{(r)}\,:\,w^{(i)}\in\mathcal{E}\text{ for }1\leq i\leq r\right\}
, so that
\mathcal{A}:=\sum_{r\geq 0}\mathcal{A}_{r}
. We prove by induction on
r
that
under the assumptions of (i) (respectively, of (ii)), the subspace

\mathcal{A}_{r}
is
(H\otimes 1\otimes\overline{H}\otimes 1)
-stable (respectively,

(\overline{H}\otimes 1\otimes H\otimes 1)
-stable).
For
r=0
this is trivial and for
r=1
this follows from the assumption on
\mathcal{E}
. Next assume
r>1
.

Choose any
\alpha,\beta\in A\otimes_{\check{R}^{\prime\prime}}B
. We can express
\alpha
and
\beta
as finite sums
\alpha=\sum a\otimes b

and
\beta=\sum a^{\prime}\otimes b^{\prime}
where
a,a^{\prime}\in A
and
b,b^{\prime}\in B
. For each pair
(b,a^{\prime})
that occurs in these summations
we also express
\check{R}^{\prime\prime}_{B,A}(b\otimes a^{\prime})
as a summation, that is,

(28)

\check{R}^{\prime\prime}_{B,A}(b\otimes a^{\prime})=\sum a^{\prime\prime}\otimes b^{\prime\prime},

where of course the
a^{\prime\prime}\in A
and the
b^{\prime\prime}\in B
depend on
b
and
a^{\prime}
.
For
h,h^{\prime}\in H
we have

\displaystyle(h\otimes 1\otimes h^{\prime}\otimes 1)\cdot\alpha\beta

\displaystyle=(h\otimes 1\otimes h^{\prime}\otimes 1)\cdot\left(\sum(a\otimes b)(a^{\prime}\otimes b^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle=(h\otimes 1\otimes h^{\prime}\otimes 1)\cdot\left(\sum aa^{\prime\prime}\otimes b^{\prime\prime}b^{\prime}\right)=\sum\big((h\otimes 1)\cdot(aa^{\prime\prime})\big)\otimes\big((h^{\prime}\otimes 1)\cdot(b^{\prime\prime}b^{\prime})\big).

Since both
A
and
B
are
H^{\prime\prime}
-module algebras, from the above calculation and (
15
) we obtain

\displaystyle(h\otimes 1\otimes h^{\prime}\otimes 1)\cdot\alpha\beta

\displaystyle=\sum((h_{1}\otimes 1)\cdot a)((h_{2}\otimes 1)\cdot a^{\prime\prime})\otimes((h^{\prime}_{1}\otimes 1)\cdot b^{\prime\prime})((h^{\prime}_{2}\otimes 1)\cdot b^{\prime})

(29)

\displaystyle=\sum\big(((h_{1}\otimes 1)\cdot a)\otimes 1\big)\big((h_{2}\otimes 1\otimes h^{\prime}_{1}\otimes 1)\cdot(a^{\prime\prime}\otimes b^{\prime\prime})\big)\big(1\otimes((h^{\prime}_{2}\otimes 1)\cdot b^{\prime})\big),

where
\Delta(h)=\sum h_{1}\otimes h_{2}
and
\Delta(h^{\prime})=\sum h_{1}^{\prime}\otimes h_{2}^{\prime}
. From Lemma
2.6.1
it follows that for each pair
(h_{2},h_{1}^{\prime})
that occurs on the right hand side
of (
2.6
)
there exists a 2-tensor
\omega_{h_{2},h_{1}^{\prime}}=\sum u_{h_{2},h_{1}^{\prime}}\otimes u^{\prime}_{h_{2},h_{1}^{\prime}}

in
H\otimes H
such that

\displaystyle(h_{2}\otimes 1\otimes h^{\prime}_{1}\otimes 1)\cdot\sum(a^{\prime\prime}\otimes b^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\check{R}^{\prime\prime}_{B,A}\left(\big(\omega_{h_{2},h^{\prime}_{1}}\big)_{13}\cdot(b\otimes a^{\prime})\right)

(30)

\displaystyle=\check{R}^{\prime\prime}_{B,A}\left(\big((u_{h_{2},h^{\prime}_{1}}\otimes 1)\cdot b\big)\otimes\big((u^{\prime}_{h_{2},h^{\prime}_{1}}\otimes 1)\cdot a^{\prime}\big)\right).

Note that by Lemma
2.6.1

the 2-tensors

\omega_{h_{2},h_{1}^{\prime}}
are of the form

(31)

\omega_{h_{2},h_{1}^{\prime}}=\overline{\omega}_{V,W}(h_{1}^{\prime}\otimes h_{2})\omega_{V,W},

where
\omega_{V,W},\overline{\omega}_{V,W}\in H\otimes H
satisfy the constraint of
Definition
2.5.1
(ii) for suitable
H
-modules
V,W
.
By comparing (
2.6
) and (
2.6
) and then using (
15
) we obtain

\displaystyle(h\otimes 1\otimes h^{\prime}\otimes 1)\cdot\alpha\beta

\displaystyle=\sum\left(\big((h_{1}\otimes 1)\cdot a\big)\otimes\big((u_{h_{2},h^{\prime}_{1}}\otimes 1)\cdot b\big)\right)\left(\big((u^{\prime}_{h_{2},h^{\prime}_{1}}\otimes 1)\cdot a^{\prime}\big)\otimes\big((h_{2}^{\prime}\otimes 1)\cdot b^{\prime}\big)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\big((h_{1}\otimes 1\otimes u_{h_{2},h^{\prime}_{1}}\otimes 1)\cdot\alpha\big)\big((u^{\prime}_{h_{2},h^{\prime}_{1}}\otimes 1\otimes h^{\prime}_{2}\otimes 1)\cdot\beta\big).

In what follows we complete the proofs of part (i) and part (ii).

(i)
It suffices to prove that for
h\in H
and
h^{\prime}\in\overline{H}
and
w^{(1)},\ldots,w^{(r)}\in\mathcal{E}
we have

(32)

(h\otimes 1\otimes h^{\prime}\otimes 1)\cdot\left(w^{(1)}\cdots w^{(r)}\right)\in\mathcal{A}_{r}.

We set

\alpha:=w^{(1)}\quad\text{and}\quad\beta:=w^{(2)}\cdots w^{(r)}.

Since
\Delta(\overline{H})\subseteq\overline{H}\otimes\overline{H}
, we can assume that
h^{\prime}_{1},h^{\prime}_{2}\in\overline{H}
, hence by considering the first component on both sides of (
31
) we obtain
u_{h_{2},h_{1}^{\prime}}\in\overline{H}
.
Thus,
by the induction hypothesis we have

(33)

\big((h_{1}\otimes 1\otimes u_{h_{2},h_{1}^{\prime}}\otimes 1)\cdot\alpha\big)\in\mathcal{E}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{1}.\quad\text{and}\quad\big((u^{\prime}_{h_{2},h^{\prime}_{1}}\otimes 1\otimes h^{\prime}_{2}\otimes 1)\cdot\beta\big)\in\mathcal{A}_{r-1}.

The inclusion (
32
) follows from
\mathcal{A}_{1}\mathcal{A}_{r-1}=\mathcal{A}_{r}
.

(ii) It suffices to prove (
32
) for

h\in\overline{H}
and
h^{\prime}\in H
. We define
\alpha
and
\beta
as in (i). As
h\in\overline{H}
, we can assume that
h_{1},h_{2}\in\overline{H}
, hence
u^{\prime}_{h_{2},h_{1}^{\prime}}\in\overline{H}
. Again the induction hypothesis implies (
33
) and
the inclusion (
32
) follows from
\mathcal{A}_{1}\mathcal{A}_{r-1}=\mathcal{A}_{r}
.
∎

2.7.
Braidings and matrix coefficients

Let
(H,\mathcal{C},\check{R})
be a locally finite braided triple.
As in (
26
) we set

R_{V,W}:=\sigma_{W,V}\circ\check{R}_{V,W}
for
V,W\in\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
.
Let
H^{\circ}_{\mathcal{C}}\subseteq H^{\circ}
denote the

\mathbb{Z}
-span of matrix coefficients of the
H
-modules that belong to
\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
.
Since
\mathcal{C}
is closed under direct sums and tensor products, indeed
H^{\circ}_{\mathcal{C}}
is a sub-bialgebra of
H^{\circ}
.

Let
f\in H^{\circ}_{\mathcal{C}}
be a sum
of matrix coefficients of
V_{1},\ldots,V_{N}\in\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
,
that is

f=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathsf{m}_{v_{i}^{*},v_{i}},

where
v_{i}\in V_{i}
and
v_{i}^{*}\in V_{i}^{*}
. Similarly, let
g\in H^{\circ}_{\mathcal{C}}
be a sum of matrix coefficients of
W_{1},\ldots,W_{N^{\prime}}\in\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
, that is
g=\sum_{i=1}^{N^{\prime}}\mathsf{m}_{w_{i}^{*},w_{i}}
.
Choose
\omega_{V,W},\overline{\omega}_{V,W}\in H\otimes H

that satisfy the condition of Definition
2.5.1
(ii) for

V:=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N}V_{i}
and
W:=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N^{\prime}}W_{i}
.
We define

(34)

R(f\otimes g):=\omega_{V,W}\cdot(f\otimes g)\quad\text{and}\quad R^{-1}(f\otimes g):=\overline{\omega}_{V,W}\cdot(f\otimes g),

where the actions of
\omega_{V,W}
and
\overline{\omega}_{V,W}
are by right translation on tensor components. This means that for example if
\omega_{V,W}=\sum r\otimes r^{\prime}\in H\otimes H
, then

\big(\omega_{V,W}\cdot(f\otimes g)\big)(h\otimes h^{\prime}):=\sum f(hr)g(h^{\prime}r^{\prime}).

Remark 2.7.1
.

(i)
For
x,y\in H
we have

(35)

\displaystyle\left(\omega_{V,W}\cdot\left(\mathsf{m}_{v_{i}^{*},v_{i}}\otimes\mathsf{m}_{w_{j}^{*},w_{j}}\right)\right)(x\otimes y)=\langle v_{i}^{*}\otimes w_{j}^{*},(x\otimes y)\cdot R_{V,W}(v_{i}\otimes w_{j})\rangle,

hence the left hand side of (
35
) is independent of the choice of
\omega_{V,W}
. Summing over
i
and
j
it follows that
R(f\otimes g)
is independent of the choice of
\omega_{V,W}
as well.
The latter observation and Lemma
2.5.2
imply that

R(f\otimes g)
does not depend on how
f
and
g
are expressed as sums of matrix coefficients. An analogous statement holds for
R^{-1}(f\otimes g)
.

(ii) From Lemma
2.5.2
it also follows that the formulas (
34
) extend to linear maps

R,R^{-1}:H^{\circ}_{\mathcal{C}}\otimes H^{\circ}_{\mathcal{C}}\to H^{\circ}_{\mathcal{C}}\otimes H^{\circ}_{\mathcal{C}}.

Indeed the latter maps
R
and
R^{-1}
are mutual inverses.

We define

(36)

\langle f\otimes g,R\rangle:=\left(R(f\otimes g)\right)(1\otimes 1).

In the rest of this subsection
\Delta^{\circ}(f)=\sum f_{1}\otimes f_{2}
and
\Delta^{\circ}(g)=\sum g_{1}\otimes g_{2}
.

Lemma 2.7.2
.

Let
f,g\in H^{\circ}_{\mathcal{C}}
. Then the following relations hold.

(i)

R(f\otimes g)=\sum f_{1}\otimes g_{1}\langle f_{2}\otimes g_{2},R\rangle
.

(ii)

f\otimes g=\sum\langle R^{-1}(f_{1}\otimes g_{1}),R\rangle f_{2}\otimes g_{2}
.

Proof.

It suffices to verify the assertion
when

f
and
g
are matrix coefficients of finite dimensional
H
-modules
V,W\in\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
.
Suppose that
f:=\mathsf{m}_{v^{*},v}
and
g:=\mathsf{m}_{w^{*},w}
. If
R_{V,W}(v\otimes w)=\sum\tilde{v}\otimes\tilde{w}
then by (
35
) we have

R(f\otimes g)=\sum\mathsf{m}_{v^{*},\tilde{v}}\otimes\mathsf{m}_{w^{*},\tilde{w}}
.
Choose dual bases
\{v_{i}\}
and
\{v_{i}^{*}\}
for
V
and
V^{*}
and dual bases
\{w_{j}\}
and
\{w_{j}^{*}\}
for
W
and
W^{*}
.
Using the coproduct and counit identities of
H^{\circ}
we have

\displaystyle\sum f_{1}\otimes g_{1}\langle f_{2}\otimes g_{2},R\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum\mathsf{m}_{v^{*},v_{i}}\otimes\mathsf{m}_{w^{*},w_{j}}\left(\langle v_{i}^{*},\tilde{v}\rangle\langle w_{j}^{*},\tilde{w}\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\langle v_{i}^{*},\tilde{v}\rangle\mathsf{m}_{v^{*},v_{i}}\otimes\langle w_{j}^{*},\tilde{w}\rangle\mathsf{m}_{w^{*},w_{j}}=\sum\mathsf{m}_{v^{*},\tilde{v}}\otimes\mathsf{m}_{w^{*},\tilde{w}}=R(f\otimes g).

This proves (i). For (ii) note that if
R^{-1}_{V,W}(v\otimes w)=\sum\tilde{v}\otimes\tilde{w}
then

R^{-1}(f\otimes g)=\sum\mathsf{m}_{v^{*},\tilde{v}}\otimes\mathsf{m}_{w^{*},\tilde{w}}
.
Next we write
R^{-1}_{V,W}(v_{i}\otimes w_{j})=\sum\tilde{v}^{i,j}\otimes\tilde{w}^{i,j}
for each pair of indices
i,j
. Then

\displaystyle\sum\langle R^{-1}(f_{1}\otimes g_{1}),R\rangle f_{2}\otimes g_{2}

\displaystyle=\sum\langle\mathsf{m}_{v^{*},\tilde{v}^{i,j}}\otimes\mathsf{m}_{w^{*},\tilde{w}^{i,j}},R\rangle\mathsf{m}_{v_{i}^{*},v}\otimes\mathsf{m}_{w_{j}^{*},w}

\displaystyle=\sum\langle v^{*},v_{i}\rangle\mathsf{m}_{v_{i}^{*},v}\otimes\langle w^{*},w_{j}\rangle\mathsf{m}_{w_{j}^{*},w}=\mathsf{m}_{v^{*},v}\otimes\mathsf{m}_{w^{*},w}=f\otimes g.\qed

Fix finite dimensional
H
-modules
V,W\in\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
. Let
\{v_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d}
and
\{w_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d^{\prime}}
be bases of
V
and
W
. Also, let

\{v_{i}^{*}\}_{i=1}^{d}
and
\{w_{i}^{*}\}_{i=1}^{d^{\prime}}
be the dual bases of
V^{*}
and
W^{*}
.
We denote the matrix entries of
R_{V,W}
in the basis
v_{i}\otimes w_{j}
by
R_{ij}^{kl}
, so that

R_{V,W}(v_{i}\otimes w_{j})=\sum_{k,l}R_{ij}^{kl}v_{k}\otimes w_{l}.

Set

\mathsf{t}^{V}_{a,b}:=\mathsf{m}_{v_{a}^{*},v_{b}}
and

\mathsf{t}^{W}_{a,b}:=\mathsf{m}_{w_{a}^{*},w_{b}}
.
Then

R_{ij}^{kl}=\langle\mathsf{t}^{V}_{k,i}\otimes\mathsf{t}^{W}_{l,j},R\rangle
,
so that as in
[
Ja96
, Lem. 7.12]

we have the well known relations

(37)

\sum_{k,l}\langle\mathsf{t}^{V}_{k,i}\otimes\mathsf{t}^{W}_{l,j},R\rangle\mathsf{t}_{a,l}^{W}\mathsf{t}_{b,k}^{V}=\sum_{k,l}\langle\mathsf{t}^{V}_{b,k}\otimes\mathsf{t}^{W}_{a,l},R\rangle\mathsf{t}_{k,i}^{V}\mathsf{t}_{l,j}^{W}\quad\text{ for all }i,j,a,b.

From (
37
) it follows that

(38)

\sum g_{1}f_{1}\langle f_{2}\otimes g_{2},R\rangle=\sum f_{2}g_{2}\langle f_{1}\otimes g_{1},R\rangle\quad\text{for }f,g\in H^{\circ}_{\mathcal{C}}.

3.
The
q
-Weyl algebra
\mathscr{PD}

In this section we construct
\mathscr{PD}
as a deformed twisted tensor product of the algebras
\mathscr{P}
and
\mathscr{D}
with respect to the univeral
R
-matrix of
U_{LR}
. Recall that
\Bbbk:=\mathbb{C}(q)
.

3.1.
The algebra
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the quantized enveloping algebra
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
is the
\Bbbk
-algebra generated by
E_{i},F_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq n-1
and
K_{\varepsilon_{i}}^{\pm 1}
for
1\leq i\leq n
, that satisfy the relations

K_{\varepsilon_{i}}K_{\varepsilon_{i}}^{-1}=K_{\varepsilon_{i}}^{-1}K_{\varepsilon_{i}}=1
,

K_{\varepsilon_{i}}K_{\varepsilon_{j}}=K_{\varepsilon_{j}}K_{\varepsilon_{i}}
,

K_{\varepsilon_{i}}E_{j}K_{\varepsilon_{i}}^{-1}=q^{{\llbracket i,j\rrbracket}-\llbracket i,j+1\rrbracket}E_{j}\ ,\ K_{\varepsilon_{i}}F_{j}K_{\varepsilon_{i}}^{-1}=q^{-{\llbracket i,j\rrbracket}+{\llbracket i,j+1\rrbracket}}F_{j}\ ,\ E_{i}F_{j}-F_{j}E_{i}=\llbracket i,j\rrbracket\frac{K_{i}-K_{i}^{-1}}{q-q^{-1}},

where
K_{i}:=K_{\varepsilon_{i}}K_{\varepsilon_{i+1}}^{-1}

and

(39)

\llbracket a,b\rrbracket:=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }a=b\\
0&\text{if }a\neq b,\end{cases}

as well as the quantum Serre relations.
For
\lambda:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{i}\varepsilon_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}\varepsilon_{1}+\cdots+\mathbb{Z}\varepsilon_{n}
we set

(40)

K_{\lambda}:=\prod_{i=1}^{n}K_{\varepsilon_{i}}^{m_{i}}.

The
Cartan subalgebra
of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
is the subalgebra spanned by the
K_{\lambda}
for
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}\varepsilon_{1}+\cdots+\mathbb{Z}\varepsilon_{n}
. We denote the Cartan subalgebras of
U_{L}\cong U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m})
and
U_{R}\cong U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
by
U_{\mathfrak{h},L}
and
U_{\mathfrak{h},R}
, respectively.
Following
[
KS97
]
for the choice of
the coproduct
\Delta
on
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
, we set

\Delta(E_{i}):=E_{i}\otimes K_{i}+1\otimes E_{i}\quad,\quad\Delta(F_{i}):=F_{i}\otimes 1+K_{i}^{-1}\otimes F_{i}\quad,\quad\Delta(K_{\varepsilon_{i}}):=K_{\varepsilon_{i}}\otimes K_{\varepsilon_{i}}.

The counit and antipode of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
are given by

\epsilon(E_{i})=\epsilon(F_{i})=0\ \ ,\ \ \epsilon(K_{\varepsilon_{i}}^{\pm 1})=1\ \ ,\ \ S(E_{i})=-E_{i}K_{i}^{-1}\ \ ,\ \ S(F_{i})=-K_{i}F_{i}\ \ ,\ \ S(K_{\varepsilon_{i}})=K_{\varepsilon_{i}}^{-1}.

3.2.
The universal
R
-matrix of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})

Recall that a Hopf algebra
H
is called
quasitriangular if it has a
universal
R
-matrix
, i.e., if there exists an invertible 2-tensor
\mathcal{R}\in H\otimes H
satisfying

(41)

\Delta^{\mathrm{cop}}=\mathcal{R}\Delta\mathcal{R}^{-1}\quad,\quad(\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id})(\mathcal{R})=\mathcal{R}_{13}\mathcal{R}_{23}\quad,\quad(\mathrm{id}\otimes\Delta)(\mathcal{R})=\mathcal{R}_{13}\mathcal{R}_{12}.

Strictly speaking,
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
is not quasitriangular because
the formal series that is usually called the universal
R
-matrix of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
indeed belongs to a topological tensor product

U_{h}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})\widehat{\otimes}U_{h}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
where
U_{h}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
denotes the
h
-adic Drinfeld–Jimbo quantum group.
However, it turns out that
the setting of braided triples is a rigorous way to work with this universal
R
-matrix.

Let
\mathcal{C}^{(n)}
denote the full subcategory of the category of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
-modules whose objects are direct sums of irreducible finite dimensional
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
-modules with highest weight of the form
q^{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}\varepsilon_{i}}
, where
\lambda_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{n}
are integers. Such modules are sometimes called modules of type
(1,\ldots,1)
. We define a braiding on
\mathcal{C}^{(n)}
as follows. First we fix a formal series description of the universal
R
-matrix for
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
.
For more details see
[
VY20
, Thm 3.108]
or
[
KS97
, Sec. 8.3.2]
.

Definition 3.2.1
.

Given
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the standard root vectors of

U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
are

E_{\varepsilon_{i}-\varepsilon_{j}}:=(-1)^{j-i-1}[E_{i},[\ldots,E_{j-1}]_{q^{-1}}]_{q^{-1}}\quad\text{and}\quad F_{\varepsilon_{i}-\varepsilon_{j}}:=(-1)^{j-i-1}[F_{j-1},[\ldots,F_{i}]_{q}]_{q},

where

1\leq i<j\leq n
and

[x,y]_{q^{\pm 1}}:=xy-q^{\pm 1}yx
.
We set

(42)

\mathcal{R}^{(n)}:=\left(e^{h\sum_{i=1}^{n}H_{i}\otimes H_{i}}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{n\choose 2}\mathrm{Exp}_{q}\left((q-q^{-1})E_{\beta_{i}}\otimes F_{\beta_{i}}\right),

with the conventions
e^{hH_{i}}:=K_{\varepsilon_{i}}
,
e^{h}:=q
,

\mathrm{Exp}_{q}(x):=\sum_{r\geq 0}q^{r\choose 2}\frac{x^{r}}{[r]_{q}!}
and
\beta_{i+\frac{j(j-1)}{2}}:=\varepsilon_{i}-\varepsilon_{j+1}
for

1\leq i\leq j\leq n-1
. Also, set

\underline{\mathcal{R}}^{(n)}:=\left(\mathcal{R}^{(n)}\right)^{-1}_{21}.

In what follows, we need
\mathcal{R}^{(n)}
to define
\mathscr{P}
and
\mathscr{D}
, and we need
\underline{\mathcal{R}}^{(n)}
to define
\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr}}
and
\mathscr{PD}
.
The formal series
\mathcal{R}^{(n)}
and
\underline{\mathcal{R}}^{(n)}
equip the category
\mathcal{C}^{(n)}
with two braidings which we describe below.
Given
V,W\in\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{C}^{(n)})
, the formal series (
42
) defines a linear map

\mathcal{R}^{(n)}_{V,W}:V\otimes W\to V\otimes W.

To give sense to the action of

\mathcal{R}^{(n)}
on
V\otimes W
we make the following two observations. First, for any
v\otimes w\in V\otimes W
all but finitely many terms of
\mathrm{Exp}_{q}\left((q-q^{-1})E_{\beta_{i}}\otimes F_{\beta_{i}}\right)
vanish on
v\otimes w
. Second, if

v\in V
and
w\in W
are weight vectors of
weights
q^{\mu}
and
q^{\nu}
with

\mu:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mu_{i}\varepsilon_{i}

and
\nu:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\nu_{i}\varepsilon_{i}
respectively, then
the action of
e^{h\sum_{i=1}^{n}H_{i}\otimes H_{i}}
on
v\otimes w
is by multiplication by the scalar
q^{\langle\mu,\nu\rangle}

where

(43)

\langle\mu,\nu\rangle:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mu_{i}\nu_{i}.

By a similar reasoning, the action of
\underline{\mathcal{R}}^{(n)}
yields linear maps

\underline{\mathcal{R}}^{(n)}_{V,W}:V\otimes W\to V\otimes W.

It is well known that by setting

(44)

\check{\mathcal{R}}^{(n)}_{V,W}:=\sigma_{V,W}\circ{\mathcal{R}}^{(n)}_{V,W}\quad\text{and}\quad\check{\underline{\mathcal{R}}}^{(n)}_{V,W}:=\sigma_{V,W}\circ\underline{\mathcal{R}}^{(n)}_{V,W}

we obtain braidings
on
\mathcal{C}^{(n)}
, which we will denote by
\check{\mathcal{R}}^{(n)}
and

\check{\underline{\mathcal{R}}}^{(n)}

Proposition 3.2.2
.

Set
H:=U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
,
\mathcal{C}:=\mathcal{C}^{(n)}
, and
\check{R}:=\check{{\mathcal{R}}}^{(n)}
or
\check{\underline{\mathcal{R}}}^{(n)}
. Then
(H,\mathcal{C},\check{R})
is a locally finite braided triple.

Proof.

The only assertion that we need to prove is the property of Definition
2.5.1
(ii). Fix finite dimensional
V,W\in\mathrm{Obj}\left(\mathcal{C}^{(n)}\right)
. We construct an element of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})\otimes U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
that acts on
V\otimes W
as
\mathcal{R}^{(n)}_{V,W}
. Since
E_{\beta_{i}}^{N_{i}}\otimes F_{\beta_{i}}^{N_{i}}
vanishes on
V\otimes W

when
N_{i}
is sufficiently large, the exponential factor

\mathrm{Exp}_{q}\left((q-q^{-1})E_{\beta_{i}}\otimes F_{\beta_{i}}\right)

can be replaced by a finite sum. Next we provide a finite 2-tensor
that replaces
e^{h\sum_{i=1}^{n}H_{i}\otimes H_{i}}
.
Let
\mu^{(1)},\ldots,\mu^{(N)}
be the distinct weights of
W
.
Choose
\nu\in\mathbb{Z}\varepsilon_{1}+\cdots+\mathbb{Z}\varepsilon_{n}
such that the values
\langle\nu,\mu^{(i)}\rangle
are mutually distinct numbers. For
1\leq i\leq N
define
T_{i}\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
by

T_{i}:=\prod_{\tiny\begin{array}[]{c}1\leq j\leq N\\
j\neq i\end{array}}\left(\frac{K_{\nu}-q^{\langle\nu,\mu^{(j)}\rangle}}{q^{\langle\nu,\mu^{(i)}\rangle}-q^{\langle\nu,\mu^{(j)}\rangle}}\right).

Then
T_{i}
acts by 0 or 1 on the
\mu^{(j)}
-weight space of
W
, depending on if
j\neq i
or
j=i
respectively. It follows that the action of

\sum_{i=1}^{N}K_{\mu^{(i)}}\otimes T_{i}
on
V\otimes W
is identical to the action of
e^{h\sum_{i=1}^{n}H_{i}\otimes H_{i}}
.
Thus the action of
\mathcal{R}^{(n)}
on
V\otimes W
is identical to the action of a (finite) 2-tensor in
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})\otimes U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
. Analogous constructions can be given for

\left(\mathcal{R}^{(n)}_{V,W}\right)^{-1}

and

\left(\underline{\mathcal{R}}^{(n)}_{V,W}\right)^{\pm 1}
.
∎

As in Subsection
2.2
let
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})^{\circ}
be the finite dual of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
.

Definition 3.2.3
.

Let
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})^{\bullet}\subseteq U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})^{\circ}
denote the sub-bialgebra that is spanned by matrix coefficients of objects of
\mathcal{C}^{(n)}
.

For
f,g\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})^{\bullet}
we define
\big\langle f\otimes g,\mathcal{R}^{(n)}\big\rangle
and

\big\langle f\otimes g,\underline{\mathcal{R}}^{(n)}\big\rangle

as in
(
36
).
For finite dimensional
V\in\mathrm{Obj}\left(\mathcal{C}^{(n)}\right)
the right dual
V^{*}
also belongs to

\mathrm{Obj}\left(\mathcal{C}^{(n)}\right)
. From this and the fact that
S
and
S^{-1}
are conjugate by an element of the Cartan subalgebra it follows that
if
f\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})^{\bullet}
then
f\circ S^{\pm 1}\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})^{\bullet}
.
It is well known
(see for example
[
KS97
, Sec. 8.1.1]
) that

(45)

\left(\mathcal{R}^{(n)}\right)_{21}=\left(\underline{\mathcal{R}}^{(n)}\right)^{-1}=(1\otimes S^{-1})\left(\underline{\mathcal{R}}^{(n)}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad(S\otimes 1)\left({\mathcal{R}}^{(n)}\right)=\left({\mathcal{R}}^{(n)}\right)^{-1}

Consequently, for
f,g\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})^{\bullet}
we have

(46)

\big\langle f\otimes(g\circ S^{-1}),\underline{\mathcal{R}}^{(n)}\big\rangle=\big\langle g\otimes f,{\mathcal{R}}^{(n)}\big\rangle\quad\text{and}\quad\big\langle(f\circ S)\otimes g,{\mathcal{R}}^{(n)}\big\rangle=\big\langle g\otimes f,\underline{\mathcal{R}}^{(n)}\big\rangle.

3.3.
The involution
x\mapsto x^{\natural}

It is well known (for example see
[
No96
, Sec. 1.4]
) that there exists a unique

\Bbbk
-linear isomorphism of Hopf algebras

(47)

U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})\to U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})^{\mathrm{op}}\ ,\ x\mapsto x^{\natural},

such that

(48)

E_{i}^{\natural}:=qK_{i}F_{i}\quad,\quad F_{i}^{\natural}:=q^{-1}E_{i}K_{i}^{-1}\quad,\quad K_{\varepsilon_{i}}^{\natural}:=K_{\varepsilon_{i}}.

Lemma 3.3.1
.

S(x^{\natural})=S^{-1}(x)^{\natural}
for
x\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
.

Proof.

Both sides are automorphisms of the algebra
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
. Therefore it suffices to verify that they agree on the
E_{i}
, the
F_{i}
, and the
K_{\varepsilon_{i}}
. This is a straightforward calculation.
∎

By the canonical duality between
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
and
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})^{\circ}
, the map (
47
) induces an isomorphism of Hopf algebras
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})^{\circ}\to\left(U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})^{\circ}\right)^{\mathrm{cop}}
.
We denote the latter map by
u\mapsto u^{\natural}
as well, so that

(49)

\langle u^{\natural},x\rangle=\langle u,x^{\natural}\rangle\quad\text{for }u\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})^{\circ}\text{ and }x\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n}).

3.4.
The algebras
\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}
and
\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}

From now on we
denote the standard positive system of the root system of
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}

by

\Delta^{+}_{n}:=\{\varepsilon_{i}-\varepsilon_{j}\,:\,1\leq i<j\leq n\}

Let
V^{(n)}
denote the irreducible
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
-module of highest weight
q^{-\varepsilon_{n}}
(all highest weights are considered with respect to
\Delta_{n}^{+}
).
Thus
V^{(n)}\cong\Bbbk^{n}
as a vector space and the homomorphism of algebras
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})\to\mathrm{End}_{\Bbbk}\left(V^{(n)}\right)
is uniquely determined by the assignments

K_{\varepsilon_{i}}\mapsto\mathsf{1}+(q^{-1}-1)\mathsf{E}_{i,i}\quad,\quad E_{i}\mapsto\mathsf{E}_{i+1,i}\quad,\quad F_{i}\mapsto\mathsf{E}_{i,i+1},

where the
\mathsf{E}_{i,j}
are the elementary matrix units associated to the standard basis
\{e_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
of
V^{(n)}
and
\mathsf{1}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{E}_{i,i}
.
Using (
42
) the
R
-matrix of
V^{(n)}\otimes V^{(n)}
can be computed directly, and we obtain

\mathcal{R}^{(n)}_{V^{(n)},V^{(n)}}=\sum_{1\leq i\leq n}q\mathsf{E}_{i,i}\otimes\mathsf{E}_{i,i}+\sum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq n}\mathsf{E}_{i,i}\otimes\mathsf{E}_{j,j}+(q-q^{-1})\sum_{1\leq j<i\leq n}\mathsf{E}_{i,j}\otimes\mathsf{E}_{j,i}.

For
1\leq i,j\leq n
let
t_{i,j}
denote the matrix coefficient
\mathsf{m}_{e_{i}^{*},e_{j}}
of
V^{(n)}
. By (
38
) the
t_{i,j}
satisfy the following relations:

(R1)

t_{k,i}t_{k,j}=qt_{k,j}t_{k,i}
,
t_{i,k}t_{j,k}=qt_{j,k}t_{i,k}
for
i<j
.

(R2)

t_{i,l}t_{k,j}=t_{k,j}t_{i,l},\ t_{i,j}t_{k,l}-t_{k,l}t_{i,j}=(q-q^{-1})t_{i,l}t_{k,j}
for
i<k
and
j<l
.

Similarly, let
\breve{V}^{(n)}
denote the irreducible
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
-module with highest weight
q^{\varepsilon_{1}}
. Again
\breve{V}^{(n)}\cong\Bbbk^{n}
as vector spaces, but the map
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})\to\mathrm{End}_{\Bbbk}\big(\breve{V}^{(n)}\big)
is uniquely determined by the assignments

K_{\varepsilon_{i}}\mapsto\mathsf{1}+(q-1)\mathsf{E}_{i,i}\quad,\quad E_{i}\mapsto\mathsf{E}_{i,i+1}\quad,\quad F_{i}\mapsto\mathsf{E}_{i+1,i}.

Indeed
\breve{V}^{(n)}\cong\left(V^{(n)}\right)^{*}
.
The
R
-matrix of
\breve{V}^{(n)}\otimes\breve{V}^{(n)}

is

{\mathcal{R}}_{\breve{V}^{(n)},\breve{V}^{(n)}}^{(n)}=\sum_{1\leq i\leq n}q\mathsf{E}_{i,i}\otimes\mathsf{E}_{i,i}+\sum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq n}\mathsf{E}_{i,i}\otimes\mathsf{E}_{j,j}+(q-q^{-1})\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\mathsf{E}_{i,j}\otimes\mathsf{E}_{j,i}.

If
\partial_{i,j}
for
1\leq i,j\leq n
denotes the matrix coefficient
\mathsf{m}_{e_{i}^{*},e_{j}}
of
\breve{V}^{(n)}
, then again from (
38
) it follows that the
\partial_{i,j}
satisfy relations similar to those between the
t_{i,j}
, with
q
replaced by
q^{-1}
. Equivalently,

(R1
′
)

\partial_{k,j}\partial_{k,i}=q\partial_{k,i}\partial_{k,j}
,
\partial_{j,k}\partial_{i,k}=q\partial_{i,k}\partial_{j,k}
for
i<j
.

(R2
′
)

\partial_{k,j}\partial_{i,l}=\partial_{i,l}\partial_{k,j},\partial_{k,l}\partial_{i,j}-\partial_{i,j}\partial_{k,l}=(q-q^{-1})\partial_{k,j}\partial_{i,l}
for
i<k
and
j<l
.

Definition 3.4.1
.

Let
\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}
denote
the subalgebra of

U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})^{\circ}

generated by the
t_{i,j}
, for
1\leq i,j\leq n
. Similarly, let
\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}
denote the subalgebra of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})^{\circ}
generated by the
\partial_{i,j}
, for
1\leq i,j\leq n
.

It is well known
(for example see
[
Ta92
]
)
that the relations (R1)–(R2) yield a presentation of
\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}
by generators and relations.
Since
\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}\cong\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}^{\mathrm{op}}
, a similar statement holds for
\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}
.
From Section
2.2
it follows that both
\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}
and
\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}
are bialgebras with the coproducts satisfying

t_{i,j}\mapsto\sum_{k}t_{i,k}\otimes t_{k,j}\quad\text{and}\quad\partial_{i,j}\mapsto\sum_{k}\partial_{i,k}\otimes\partial_{k,j},

and the counits satisfying
t_{i,j},\partial_{i,j}\mapsto\llbracket i,j\rrbracket
. Henceforth we denote the coproducts of
\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}
and
\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}
by
\Delta_{\mathscr{P}}
and
