\Delta_{\mathscr{D}}
, respectively.
In the proof of Lemma
3.4.2
we use the relations

t_{i,j}(K_{\varepsilon_{k}})=\llbracket i,j\rrbracket q^{-\llbracket i,k\rrbracket}\quad,\quad t_{i,j}(E_{k})=\llbracket i,k+1\rrbracket\llbracket j,k\rrbracket\quad,\quad t_{i,j}(F_{k})=\llbracket i,k\rrbracket\llbracket j,k+1\rrbracket.

Lemma 3.4.2
.

t_{i,j}^{\natural}=t_{j,i}
and

\partial_{i,j}^{\natural}=\partial_{j,i}
, where

t_{i,j}^{\natural}
and
\partial_{i,j}^{\natural}
are defined by (
49
).

Proof.

We only give the proof for the
t_{i,j}
, as the argument for the
\partial_{i,j}
is similar. The assertion follows if we
verify that

(50)

\langle t_{i,j},x^{\natural}\rangle=\langle t_{j,i},x\rangle\quad\text{for $x\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})$.}

It suffices to check (
50
) when
x
is a generator of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
, because if (
50
) holds for
x,y\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
then

\langle t_{i,j},(xy)^{\natural}\rangle=\langle t_{i,j},y^{\natural}x^{\natural}\rangle=\sum_{a}\langle t_{i,a},y^{\natural}\rangle\langle t_{a,j},x^{\natural}\rangle=\sum_{a}\langle t_{a,i},y\rangle\langle t_{j,a},x\rangle=\langle t_{j,i},xy\rangle,

hence (
50
) also holds for
xy
. When
x
is one of the standard generators of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
, checking (
50
) is a direct calculation. For example for
x=E_{k}
we have

\langle t_{i,j},E_{k}^{\natural}\rangle=q\sum_{a}\langle t_{i,a},K_{k}\rangle\langle t_{a,j},F_{k}\rangle.

The right hand side vanishes unless

j=k+1
and
i=k
, in which case we have
\langle t_{i,j},E_{k}^{\natural}\rangle=1
. It follows immediately that
\langle t_{i,j},E_{k}^{\natural}\rangle=\langle t_{j,i},E_{k}\rangle
for all
i,j,k
.
∎

According to Remark
2.2.1
, the canonical

U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})\otimes U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
-module structure of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
by left and right translation equips both
\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}
and
\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}
with
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})\otimes U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
-module algebra structures.
Our next goal is to describe the latter actions explicitly (all of the actions are from the left side).

Let
\mathcal{R}_{\mathscr{D}}
be the action of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
on
\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}
by right translation, as in Remark
2.2.1
.
We have

\mathcal{R}_{\mathscr{D}}(x)u=\sum\langle u_{2},x\rangle u_{1}\quad\text{for }x\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n}),\ u\in\mathscr{D}_{n\times n},

where as usual

\Delta(u)=\sum u_{1}\otimes u_{2}
.
Similarly, let
\mathcal{L}_{\mathscr{D}}
be the action of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
on
\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}
by left translation. Thus

\mathcal{L}_{\mathscr{D}}(x)u=\sum\langle u_{1},x^{\natural}\rangle u_{2}\quad\text{for }x\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n}),\ u\in\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}.

By Remark
2.2.1
both
\mathcal{L}_{\mathscr{D}}
and
\mathcal{R}_{\mathscr{D}}
equip
\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}
with
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
-module algebra structures.

Next we define the left and right
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
-actions on
\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}
. For
c\in\Bbbk
let
\xi_{c}
denote the unique automorphism of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
defined by

\xi_{c}(E_{i}):=cE_{i}\quad,\quad\xi_{c}(F_{i}):=c^{-1}F_{i}\quad,\quad\xi_{c}(K_{\varepsilon_{i}})=K_{\varepsilon_{i}}.

Lemma 3.4.3
.

t_{i,j}\circ\xi_{c}=c^{i-j}t_{i,j}
and

\partial_{i,j}\circ\xi_{c}=c^{j-i}\partial_{i,j}
.

Proof.

We only give the proof of the assertion for the
t_{i,j}
. In this case we need to verify the equality

(51)

t_{i,j}(\xi_{c}(x))=c^{i-j}t_{i,j}(x)

for
x\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
. This is a straightforward calculation in the special case where
x
is one of the standard generators of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
. To complete the proof
of (
51
) note that if (
51
) holds for
x
and
x^{\prime}
, then it also holds for
xx^{\prime}
because

t_{i,j}(\xi_{c}(xx^{\prime}))=\sum_{a=1}^{n}t_{i,a}(\xi_{c}(x))t_{a,j}(\xi_{c}(x^{\prime}))=\sum_{a=1}^{n}c^{(i-a)+(a-j)}t_{i,a}(x)t_{a,j}(x^{\prime})=c^{i-j}t_{i,j}(xx^{\prime}).\qed

The map

\Xi:U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})\to U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})^{\mathrm{op,cop}}\ ,\ x\mapsto\xi_{-1/q}(S(x))

is an isomorphism of Hopf algebras. Thus, the pullback of
\Xi
induces an isomorphism of Hopf algebras

U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})^{\circ}\to\left(U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})^{\circ}\right)^{\mathrm{op,cop}}
, given by
u\mapsto u\circ\Xi
.
Set

(52)

\iota(u):=u\circ\Xi\quad\text{ for }u\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})^{\circ}.

Lemma 3.4.4
.

We have

(53)

\iota(t_{i,j})=\partial_{j,i}\quad\text{for }1\leq i,j\leq n.

In particular, the restriction of
\iota
to
\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}
is an isomorphism of bialgebras
\iota:\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}\to\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}^{\mathrm{op,cop}}
.

Proof.

We need to verify
t_{i,j}(\Xi(x))=\partial_{j,i}(x)
for
x\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
. It suffices to check the latter relation for the standard generators of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
, and this special case follows from a direct calculation.
∎

Next note that the map

\underline{\iota}:\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}\to\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}^{\mathrm{op}}\ ,\ \underline{\iota}(u):=\iota(u)^{\natural}

is an isomorphism of bialgebras (indeed
\underline{\iota}(t_{i,j})=\partial_{i,j}
). Let

\mathcal{R}_{\mathscr{D},\tau}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\mathscr{D},\tau}
denote the
\tau
-twists of
\mathcal{R}_{\mathscr{D}}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\mathscr{D}}

(see Remark
2.2.1
), where we set

\tau(x):=S^{-1}(x)^{\natural}
for
x\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
. For
u\in\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}
and
x\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
set

\mathcal{R}_{\mathscr{P}}(x)u:=\underline{\iota}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{R}_{\mathscr{D},\tau}(x)\underline{\iota}(u)\right)\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathcal{L}_{\mathscr{P}}(x)u:=\underline{\iota}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\mathscr{D},\tau}(x)\underline{\iota}(u)\right).

By Remark
2.2.1
(ii),
\mathcal{R}_{\mathscr{D},\tau}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\mathscr{D},\tau}
equip
\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}
with
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})^{\mathrm{cop}}
-module algebra structures. It follows that
\mathcal{R}_{\mathscr{P}}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\mathscr{P}}
equip
\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}
with
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
-module structures.
By a direct calculation

\mathcal{R}_{\mathscr{P}}(x)u=\sum\langle\iota(u_{2}),S^{-1}(x)\rangle u_{1}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{L}_{\mathscr{P}}(x)u=\sum\langle\iota(u_{1}),S^{-1}(x)^{\natural}\rangle u_{2},

for
x\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
and
u\in\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}
.
Using (
52
), Lemma
3.3.1
and the relation
S^{2}=\xi_{q^{2}}
we obtain

\mathcal{R}_{\mathscr{P}}(x)u=\sum\langle u_{2}\circ\xi_{-1/q},x\rangle u_{1}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{L}_{\mathscr{P}}(x)u=\sum\langle(u_{1}\circ\xi_{-q})^{\natural},x\rangle u_{2}.

3.5.
The algebras
\mathscr{P}
and
\mathscr{D}

Our next goal is to extend the constructions of Subsection
3.4
to the
m\times n
case.

Definition 3.5.1
.

Let
m
and
n
be positive integers and set
N:=\max\{m,n\}
. We define
the algebra
\mathscr{P}:=\mathscr{P}_{m\times n}

(respectively,
\mathscr{D}:=\mathscr{D}_{m\times n}
) to be the subalgebra of
\mathscr{P}_{N\times N}

(respectively,
\mathscr{D}_{N\times N}
)
that is generated by the
t_{i,j}
(respectively, the
\partial_{i,j}
) where
1\leq i\leq m
and
1\leq j\leq n
.

Note that by restricting the
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N})\otimes U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N})
-module algebra structures
on
\mathscr{P}_{N\times N}
and
\mathscr{D}_{N\times N}
we obtain
U_{LR}
-module algebra structures on
\mathscr{P}
and
\mathscr{D}
. Let us describe these
U_{LR}
-module algebras more precisely.
For convenience we first assume that
m\leq n
. Then the subalgebra of

U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})

generated by
E_{i},F_{i},K^{\pm 1}_{\varepsilon_{j}}
for

1\leq i\leq m-1
and
1\leq j\leq m

is isomorphic to
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m})\cong U_{L}
. With this identification of
U_{L}
with a subalgebra of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
we have the following lemma.

Lemma 3.5.2
.

Suppose that
m\leq n
and we identify
U_{L}
with a subalgebra of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
as above. Then
\mathcal{L}_{\mathscr{D}}(x)\mathscr{D}\subseteq\mathscr{D}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\mathscr{P}}(x)\mathscr{P}\subseteq\mathscr{P}
for
x\in U_{L}
.

Proof.

We only prove this for
\mathcal{L}_{\mathscr{D}}
(for
\mathcal{L}_{\mathscr{P}}
the proof is similar). Since
\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}
is a
U_{L}
-module algebra, it suffices to check that if
i\leq m

and
x
is one of the standard generators of
U_{L}

then
\mathcal{L}_{\mathscr{D}}(x)\partial_{i,j}\in\mathscr{D}
. If
x=E_{k}
for
1\leq k\leq m-1
then

(54)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathscr{D}}(E_{k})\partial_{i,j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a=1}^{n}\langle\partial_{i,a},E_{k}^{\natural}\rangle\partial_{a,j}=\sum_{a=1}^{n}\langle\partial_{i,a},qK_{k}F_{k}\rangle\partial_{a,j}=q\sum_{a=1}^{n}\sum_{b=1}^{n}\langle\partial_{i,b},K_{k}\rangle\langle\partial_{b,a},F_{k}\rangle\partial_{a,j}.

We have
\langle\partial_{b,a},F_{k}\rangle=\langle e_{b}^{*},\mathsf{E}_{k+1,k}e_{a}\rangle=\llbracket a,k\rrbracket\llbracket b,k+1\rrbracket
.
In particular,
\langle\partial_{b,a},F_{k}\rangle=0
unless
a\leq m-1
. It follows that the right hand side of (
54
) is a linear combination of the
\partial_{a,j}
where
a\leq m-1
, hence it lies in
\mathscr{D}
. The calculations for the cases
x=F_{k}
for
1\leq k\leq m-1
and
x=K_{\varepsilon_{k}}^{\pm 1}
for
1\leq k\leq m
are similar.
∎

Lemma
3.5.2
implies that

\mathscr{P}
and
\mathscr{D}
are
U_{LR}
-stable subspaces of
\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}
and
\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}
, where we consider

U_{LR}=U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m})\otimes U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})\cong U_{L}\otimes U_{R}

as a subalgebra of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})\otimes U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
via the aforementioned embedding
U_{L}\hookrightarrow U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
. Thus,
\mathscr{P}
and
\mathscr{D}
inherit
U_{LR}
-module algebra structures from
\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}
and
\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}
.

Henceforth we mostly drop the symbols
\mathcal{L}_{\mathscr{P}}
,
\mathcal{L}_{\mathscr{D}}
,
\mathcal{R}_{\mathscr{P}}
and
\mathcal{R}_{\mathscr{D}}
from our notation. Instead, we use the notation

(x\otimes y)\cdot u

to denote the action of
x\otimes y\in U_{LR}
on
u\in\mathscr{P}
(or
u\in\mathscr{D}
).
The actions of
x\otimes y\in U_{L}\otimes U_{R}
on
u\in\mathscr{P}
and on
v\in\mathscr{D}
are given explicitly by the formulas

(55)

(x\otimes y)\cdot u=\sum\langle\iota(u_{1}),S^{-1}(x)^{\natural}\rangle\langle\iota(u_{3}),S^{-1}(y)\rangle u_{2}=\sum\langle(u_{1}\circ\xi_{-q})^{\natural},x\rangle\langle u_{3}\circ\xi_{-1/q},y\rangle u_{2}

and

(56)

(x\otimes y)\cdot v=\sum\langle v_{1},x^{\natural}\rangle\langle v_{3},y\rangle v_{2}=\sum\langle v_{1}^{\natural},x\rangle\langle v_{3},y\rangle v_{2},

with
(\Delta_{\mathscr{P}}\otimes 1)\circ\Delta_{\mathscr{P}}(u)=\sum u_{1}\otimes u_{2}\otimes u_{3}
and

(\Delta_{\mathscr{D}}\otimes 1)\circ\Delta_{\mathscr{D}}(v)=\sum v_{1}\otimes v_{2}\otimes v_{3}
in Sweedler notation, where
\Delta_{\mathscr{P}}
and
\Delta_{\mathscr{D}}
denote the coproducts of
\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}
and
\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}
, respectively.

Remark 3.5.3
.

In the case
m>n
the construction of the
U_{LR}
-action is the same, except that we embed
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m})\otimes U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
in
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m})\otimes U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m})
. However,
formulas (
55
) and (
56
) remain the same.

Definition 3.5.4
.

The map
\phi_{U}:U_{LR}\to\mathrm{End}_{\Bbbk}(\mathscr{P})
is the homomorphism of algebras induced by the action (
55
).

3.6.
U_{LR}
-module decomposition of
\mathscr{P}
and
\mathscr{D}

For any integer partition
\lambda
satisfying
\ell(\lambda)\leq n
, where
\ell(\lambda)
denotes the length of
\lambda
, let
V_{\lambda}
denote the irreducible finite dimensional
U_{R}
-module of type

(1,\ldots,1)

with highest weight
q^{\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}\varepsilon_{i}}
(with respect to
\Delta_{n}^{+}
). If
\lambda
satisfies
\ell(\lambda)\leq m
we use the same notation
V_{\lambda}
to denote the analogously defined module of
U_{L}
.

The algebras
\mathscr{P}
and
\mathscr{D}
are naturally graded by degree of monomials. For
d\geq 0
let
\mathscr{P}^{(d)}
(respectively,
\mathscr{D}^{(d)}
) denote the graded component of degree
d
of
\mathscr{P}
(respectively,
\mathscr{D}
). Furthermore, let
\Lambda_{d,r}
be the set of integer partitions
\lambda
such that
\ell(\lambda)\leq d
and
|\lambda|=r
, where
|\lambda|
denotes the size of
\lambda
.
The following proposition is well known and its proof can be found for example
in
[
NYM93
,
Ta92
,
Zh02
]
.

Proposition 3.6.1
.

Set
d:=\min\{m,n\}
.
We have isomorphisms of
U_{LR}
-modules

\mathscr{P}^{(r)}\cong\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{d,r}}V_{\lambda}^{*}\otimes V_{\lambda}^{*}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathscr{D}^{(r)}\cong\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{d,r}}V_{\lambda}\otimes V_{\lambda}.

Remark 3.6.2
.

The action of
U_{L}\otimes U_{R}
on the generators of
\mathscr{P}
and
\mathscr{D}
can be computed explicitly.
For the subalgebra
U_{R}\cong 1\otimes U_{R}
of
U_{LR}
, the action is given by

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}E_{k}\cdot\partial_{i,j}=\llbracket k+1,j\rrbracket\partial_{i,k}&,&F_{k}\cdot\partial_{i,j}=\llbracket k,j\rrbracket\partial_{i,k+1}&,&K_{\varepsilon_{k}}\cdot\partial_{i,j}=q^{\llbracket k,j\rrbracket}\partial_{i,j},\\
E_{k}\cdot t_{i,j}=-\llbracket k,j\rrbracket q^{-1}t_{i,k+1}&,&F_{k}\cdot t_{i,j}=-\llbracket k+1,j\rrbracket qt_{i,k}&,&K_{\varepsilon_{k}}\cdot t_{i,j}=q^{-\llbracket k,j\rrbracket}t_{i,j},\end{array}

where
1\leq k\leq n-1
,
1\leq i\leq m
and
1\leq j\leq n
.
For
U_{L}\cong U_{L}\otimes 1
the formulas are similar but the action occurs in the first index (thus, they are obtained by replacing
\partial_{i,j}
by
\partial_{j,i}
and

t_{i,j}
by
t_{j,i}
).

3.7.
The algebras
{\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr}}}

and
\mathscr{PD}

For
n\geq 1
let
\mathcal{R}^{(n)}
and
\underline{\mathcal{R}}^{(n)}
be as in Definition
3.2.1
.

Definition 3.7.1
.

We set
\mathcal{C}_{L}:=\mathcal{C}^{(m)}
and

\mathcal{C}_{R}:=\mathcal{C}^{(n)}
. Furthermore, we set

{\mathcal{R}}_{L}:={\mathcal{R}}^{(m)}\quad,\quad{\underline{\mathcal{R}}}_{L}:={\underline{\mathcal{R}}}^{(m)}\quad,\quad{\mathcal{R}}_{R}:={\mathcal{R}}^{(n)}\quad,\quad{\underline{\mathcal{R}}}_{R}:={\underline{\mathcal{R}}}^{(n)}.

We define
braidings
\check{\mathcal{R}}_{L}
and

\check{\underline{\mathcal{R}}}_{L}

on
\mathcal{C}_{L}
, and

\check{\mathcal{R}}_{R}
and

\check{\underline{\mathcal{R}}}_{L}
on

\mathcal{C}_{R}
as in (
44
).

From Proposition
3.2.2
it follows that

(U_{L},\mathcal{C}_{L},\check{\underline{\mathcal{R}}}_{L})
and
(U_{R},\mathcal{C}_{R},\check{\underline{\mathcal{R}}}_{R})
are locally finite braided triples. Thus the product
(in the sense of
Definition
2.5.3
)
of

(U_{L},\mathcal{C}_{L},\check{\underline{\mathcal{R}}}_{L})
and
(U_{R},\mathcal{C}_{R},\check{\underline{\mathcal{R}}}_{R})

is also a locally finite braided triple of the form
(U_{LR},\mathcal{C}_{LR},\check{\underline{\mathcal{R}}}_{LR})
, where

\check{\underline{\mathcal{R}}}_{LR}
is defined as in (
27
).
Furthermore, Proposition
3.6.1
implies that

\mathscr{P},\mathscr{D}\in\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{C}_{LR})
.

Recall that
\mathscr{D}^{(1)}
and
\mathscr{P}^{(1)}
are
U_{LR}
-modules. Let

\psi_{\circ}:\mathscr{D}^{(1)}\times\mathscr{P}^{(1)}\to\Bbbk
be the
U_{LR}
-invariant
\Bbbk
-bilinear form, in the sense of (
19
), that is defined by

\psi_{\circ}(\partial_{i,j},t_{k,l}):=\llbracket i,k\rrbracket\llbracket j,l\rrbracket\quad\text{for }1\leq i,k\leq m,\text{ and }1\leq j,l\leq n.

Definition 3.7.2
.

We define the algebras

\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr}}
and
\mathscr{PD}
by

(57)

\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr}}:=\mathscr{P}\otimes_{\check{\mathcal{R}}}\mathscr{D}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathscr{PD}:=\mathscr{P}\otimes_{\check{\mathcal{R}},\psi_{\circ}}\mathscr{D},

according to Definition
2.4.2
and
Definition
2.4.9
, with

A:=\mathscr{P}
,
E_{A}:=\mathscr{P}^{(1)}
,
B:=\mathscr{D}
,
E_{B}:=\mathscr{D}^{(1)}
,
\psi:=\psi_{\circ}
, and
\check{\mathcal{R}}:=\check{\underline{\mathcal{R}}}_{LR}
.

It turns out that there is an equivalent description of
\mathscr{PD}
and
\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr}}
by generators and relations. Recall the notation
\llbracket a,b\rrbracket
that was defined in (
39
). We set

\llbracket a,b\rrbracket_{q}:=\begin{cases}q&\text{ if }a=b,\\
q-q^{-1}&\text{if }a\neq b.\end{cases}

Definition 3.7.3
.

The algebra
\mathscr{PD}
is generated by
2mn
generators
t_{i,j}
and
\partial_{i,j}
for
1\leq i\leq m
and
1\leq j\leq n
, modulo the relations (R1), (R2), (R1
′
), (R2
′
) of Subsection
3.4

and the relations

(58)

\displaystyle\partial_{\overline{a}_{1},\overline{a}_{2}}t_{a_{1},a_{2}}=\llbracket a_{1},\overline{a}_{1}\rrbracket\llbracket a_{2},\overline{a}_{2}\rrbracket+\sum_{b_{1}\geq a_{1}}\sum_{\overline{b}_{1}\geq\overline{a}_{1}}\sum_{b_{2}\geq a_{2}}\sum_{\overline{b}_{2}\geq\overline{a}_{2}}\left(\diamondsuit_{1}+\square_{1}\right)\left(\diamondsuit_{2}+\square_{2}\right)t_{b_{1},b_{2}}\partial_{\overline{b}_{1},\overline{b}_{2}},

where

\diamondsuit_{i}:=\llbracket a_{i},\overline{a}_{i}\rrbracket\llbracket b_{i},\overline{b}_{i}\rrbracket\llbracket a_{i},b_{i}\rrbracket_{q}\quad\text{ and }\quad\square_{i}:=\left(1-\llbracket a_{i},\overline{a}_{i}\rrbracket\right)\llbracket a_{i},b_{i}\rrbracket\llbracket\overline{a}_{i},\overline{b}_{i}\rrbracket.

The algebra

\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr}}
is also generated by

2mn
generators
t_{i,j}
and
\partial_{i,j}
for
1\leq i\leq m
and
1\leq j\leq n
modulo
the same relations, except that

\llbracket a_{1},\overline{a}_{1}\rrbracket\llbracket a_{2},\overline{a}_{2}\rrbracket
does not occur on
the
right hand side of (
58
).

Remark 3.7.4
.

The relation (
58
) of
\mathscr{PD}
can be written more explicitly as the relations
(R3)–(R6) below:

(R3)

\partial_{c,b}t_{d,a}=t_{d,a}\partial_{c,b}
if
b\neq a
and
c\neq d
.

(R4)

\displaystyle\partial_{c,b}t_{c,a}=qt_{c,a}\partial_{c,b}+\sum_{c^{\prime}>c}(q-q^{-1})t_{c^{\prime},a}\partial_{c^{\prime},b}
if
b\neq a
.

(R5)

\displaystyle\partial_{c,a}t_{d,a}=qt_{d,a}\partial_{c,a}+\sum_{a^{\prime}>a}(q-q^{-1})t_{d,a^{\prime}}\partial_{c,a^{\prime}}
if
c\neq d
.

(R6)

\displaystyle\partial_{c,d}t_{c,d}=1+\sum_{c^{\prime}\geq c}\,\sum_{d^{\prime}\geq d}q^{\llbracket c^{\prime},c\rrbracket+\llbracket d^{\prime},d\rrbracket}(q-q^{-1})^{2-\llbracket c^{\prime},c\rrbracket-\llbracket d^{\prime},d\rrbracket}t_{c^{\prime},a^{\prime}}\partial_{c^{\prime},a^{\prime}}
.

For
\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr}}
the
relation (
58
) has the same explicit form, except that (R6) should be replaced by

(R6
′
)

\displaystyle\partial_{c,d}t_{c,d}=\sum_{c^{\prime}\geq c}\,\sum_{d^{\prime}\geq d}q^{\llbracket c^{\prime},c\rrbracket+\llbracket d^{\prime},d\rrbracket}(q-q^{-1})^{2-\llbracket c^{\prime},c\rrbracket-\llbracket d^{\prime},d\rrbracket}t_{c^{\prime},d^{\prime}}\partial_{c^{\prime},d^{\prime}}
.

Proposition 3.7.5
.

Definition
3.7.2
and Definition
3.7.3
are equivalent.

Proof.

We just need to explain how to compute the mixed relations (
17
) and (
20
).
As a
U_{LR}
-module,

\mathscr{D}^{(1)}\cong\breve{V}^{(m)}\otimes\breve{V}^{(n)}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathscr{P}^{(1)}\cong V^{(m)}\otimes V^{(n)},

where the isomorphisms are
\partial_{i,j}\mapsto e_{i}\otimes e_{j}
and
t_{i,j}\mapsto e_{i}\otimes e_{j}
.
By a direct calculation using Definition
3.2.1

we obtain

\left(\underline{\mathcal{R}}_{L}\right)_{\breve{V}^{(m)},V^{(m)}}=q\sum_{1\leq i\leq m}\mathsf{E}_{i,i}\otimes\mathsf{E}_{i,i}+\sum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq m}\mathsf{E}_{i,i}\otimes\mathsf{E}_{j,j}+(q-q^{-1})\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq m}\mathsf{E}_{j,i}\otimes\mathsf{E}_{j,i}.

The formula for
\left(\underline{\mathcal{R}}_{R}\right)_{\breve{V}^{(n)},V^{(n)}}
is similar, with
m
replaced by
n
.
The mixed relations (
20
) of
\mathscr{PD}
and (
17
)
of

\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr}}
can now be computed
explicitly based on Definition
2.4.2
and Definition
2.4.9
.
∎

3.8.
Bases of monomials for
\mathscr{PD}
and
\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr}}

Consider the monomials

(59)

t_{1,1}^{a_{1,1}}\cdots t_{1,n}^{a_{1,n}}\cdots t_{m,1}^{a_{m,1}}\cdots t_{m,n}^{a_{m,n}}\partial_{m,n}^{b_{m,n}}\cdots\partial_{m,1}^{b_{m,1}}\cdots\partial_{1,n}^{b_{1,n}}\cdots\partial_{1,1}^{b_{1,1}},\quad a_{i,j},b_{i,j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\geq 0}.

The expression (
59
) makes sense both as an element of
\mathscr{PD}
and an element of
\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr}}
.

Proposition 3.8.1
.

The monomials (
59
) form a
\Bbbk
-basis of
\mathscr{PD}
.

Proof.

By a standard straightening argument we can show that by using the relations (R1), (R1
′
),
(R2), (R2
′
) and (R3)–(R6) any product of
the
t_{i,j}
and the
\partial_{i,j}
can be expressed as a linear combination of
the monomials (
59
).
The fact that the latter monomials are indeed linearly independent follows from Bergman’s Diamond Lemma and some straightforward (although tedious) computations.
This was also pointed out in
[
SSV04
, Sec. 10]
.
In
[
LSS22a
]
we give a more conceptual proof of this assertion using the theory of PBW deformations of quadratic algebras.
∎

Proposition 3.8.2
.

The algebra
\mathscr{P}
has a basis consisting of monomials (
59
)
where
b_{i,j}=0
for all
i,j
. The algebra
\mathscr{D}
has a basis consisting of monomials (
59
)
where
a_{i,j}=0
for all
i,j
.

Proof.

This follows from Proposition
3.8.1
.
It is also proved for example
in
[
NYM93
, Thm 1.4]
.
∎

By Proposition
2.4.8

the algebra
\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr}}
is a quotient of the free algebra on
2mn
generators
t_{i,j}
and
\partial_{i,j}
. Note that by a slight abuse of notation we use the same notation for generators of
\mathscr{PD}
and
\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr}}
. In the next proposition we describe a basis for this quotient.

Proposition 3.8.3
.

The monomials (
59
)
form a basis of
\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr}}
.

Proof.

This follows from the vector space decomposition
\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr}}=\mathscr{P}\otimes\mathscr{D}
and
Proposition
3.8.2
.
∎

Remark 3.8.4
.

From the results of this subsection it follows that
\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr}}

has two realizations:

(i)

According to Definition
3.7.2
we have
\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr}}\cong\mathscr{P}\otimes\mathscr{D}
as a
\Bbbk
-vector space. Thus,
\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr}}
is generated by
2mn
generators
t_{i,j}\otimes 1
and
1\otimes\partial_{i,j}
.

(ii)

By Proposition
2.4.8

we can realize

\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr}}

as a quotient of the free
\Bbbk
-algebra generated by
2mn
generators: the
t_{i,j}
and the
\partial_{i,j}
.

Definition 3.8.5
.

Define a total order
\prec
on the set of pairs
(i,j)
with
1\leq i\leq m
and
1\leq j\leq n
as follows: we set

(i,j)\prec(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
if either

i+j<i^{\prime}+j^{\prime}
, or
i+j=i^{\prime}+j^{\prime}
and
i<i^{\prime}
.

Remark 3.8.6
.

The algebra
\mathscr{PD}
has another basis consisting of monomials of the form

(60)

\left(\prod_{i,j}t_{i,j}^{a_{i,j}}\right)\left(\prod_{i,j}\partial_{i,j}^{b_{i,j}}\right),

where the
\partial_{i,j}
(respectively, the
t_{i,j}
) occur in ascending (respectively, descending) order relative to the total order
\prec
.
This can be deduced from Proposition
3.8.1
. Indeed by an elementary argument one can show that any monomial of the
t_{i,j}
of total degree
d

that is sorted in the order
given in Proposition
3.8.1
can be expressed as a linear combination of monomials of the
t_{i,j}
of total degree
d
that are sorted in the order given in (
60
).
A similar assertion holds for monomials in the
\partial_{i,j}
.
Thus the monomials of the form (
60
) span
\mathscr{PD}
. A dimension counting argument implies that the latter monomials also form a basis.

By an analogous reasoning we can also show that
\mathscr{PD}
has a basis that consists of the monomials

(61)

t_{m,n}^{a_{m,n}}\cdots t_{m,1}^{a_{m,1}}\cdots t_{1,n}^{a_{1,1}}\cdots t_{1,1}^{a_{1,1}}\partial_{1,1}^{b_{1,1}}\cdots\partial_{1,n}^{b_{1,n}}\cdots\partial_{m,1}^{b_{m,1}}\cdots\partial_{m,n}^{b_{m,n}},\quad a_{i,j},b_{i,j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\geq 0}.

Here the
\partial_{i,j}
(respectively, the
t_{i,j}
) are sorted according to the lexicographic order (respectively, the reverse lexicographic order) on indices.

3.9.
The algebras
\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}
and
\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}}

In this subsection we consider two families of algebras,
the
\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}
and the
\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}}
,
that
slightly generalize
\mathscr{PD}
and
\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr}}
.

Definition 3.9.1
.

Fix integers
k,l,n\geq 1
and set
m:=\max\{k,l\}
.
Let

\tilde{t}_{i,j}
and

\tilde{\partial}_{i,j}
be as in (
5
) where
a=m
and
b=n
.
We define
\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}

(respectively,
\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}}
)
to be the subalgebra of
\mathscr{PD}=\mathscr{PD}_{m\times n}

(respectively,

\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr}}\cong\mathscr{P}\otimes\mathscr{D}
)
that is generated by the
\tilde{t}_{i,j}
and the
\tilde{\partial}_{i^{\prime},j}

(respectively,
the
\tilde{t}_{i,j}\otimes 1
and the
1\otimes\tilde{\partial}_{i^{\prime},j}
)
where

(62)

1\leq i\leq k\quad,\quad 1\leq i^{\prime}\leq l\quad\text{and}\quad 1\leq j\leq n.

Proposition 3.9.2
.

The algebras

\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}
and
\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}}
have the following presentations:

(i)

\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}
is isomorphic to the quotient of the free
\Bbbk
-algebra generated by the symbols
t_{i,j}
and
\partial_{i^{\prime},j}
with
i
,
i^{\prime}
,
j
satisfying (
62
),
modulo the relations

(R1)
,
(R2)
,
(R1
′
)
,
(R2
′
)
of Subsection
3.4

and the relations

(R3)–(R6)
of Remark
3.7.4
.

(ii)

\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{k,l,n}
is isomorphic to the quotient of the free
\Bbbk
-algebra generated by the symbols
t_{i,j}
and
\partial_{i^{\prime},j}
with
i
,
i^{\prime}
,
j
satisfying (
62
),
modulo the relations

(R1)
,
(R2)
,
(R1
′
)
,
(R2
′
)
of Subsection
3.4

and the relations

(R3)–(R5)
and
(R6
′
)
of Remark
3.7.4
.

Proof.

(i) Denote the quotient of the free algebra
by
\mathscr{F}_{k,l,n}
. Since
\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}
is a subalgebra of
\mathscr{PD}=\mathscr{PD}_{m\times n}
for
m:=\max\{k,l\}
, from the explicit description of the relations of
\mathscr{PD}

it follows that there exists a natural epimorphism
\mathsf{f}_{k,l,n}:{\mathscr{F}}_{k,l,n}\to\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}
that is uniquely defined by the assignments
t_{i,j}\mapsto t_{m-k+i,j}
and
\partial_{i^{\prime},j}\mapsto\partial_{m-l+i^{\prime},j}
.
A standard straightening argument proves that every element of
\mathscr{F}_{k,l,n}
is a linear combination of monomials of the form (
59
). Proposition
3.8.1

implies that

\mathsf{f}_{k,l,n}
maps the latter monomials to
a linearly independent set of elements of
\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}
. Thus
\mathsf{f}_{k,l,n}
is an isomorphism.

(ii) Similar to the proof of (i), with Proposition
3.8.1
replaced by
Proposition
3.8.3
.
∎

Definition 3.9.3
.

For any
1\leq r\leq n
we can identify
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{r})
with a Hopf subalgebra of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
via the monomorphism of associative algebras

(63)

\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{r,n}:U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{r})\to U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n}),

defined by
\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{r,n}(E_{i}):=E_{i+n-r}
,
\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{r,n}(F_{i}):=F_{i+n-r}
, and
\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{r,n}(K_{\varepsilon_{i}}^{\pm 1}):=K_{\varepsilon_{i+n-r}}^{\pm 1}
.

In the next proposition we establish the existence of the map (
4
).
Recall
from
Remark
3.8.4
(ii)
that we consider both
\mathscr{PD}
and
\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr}}
as algebras generated by
2mn
generators
t_{i,j}
and
\partial_{i,j}
.

Proposition 3.9.4
.

Fix
1\leq m^{\prime}\leq m
and
1\leq n^{\prime}\leq n
. Let

\tilde{t}_{i,j},\tilde{\partial}_{i,j}\in\mathscr{PD}_{m^{\prime}\times n^{\prime}}

(respectively,

\tilde{t}_{i,j},\tilde{\partial}_{i,j}\in\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{m^{\prime}\times n^{\prime}}
)
for
1\leq i\leq m^{\prime}
and
1\leq j\leq n^{\prime}
be as in (
5
) for
a:=m^{\prime}
and
b:=n^{\prime}
,
that is

\tilde{t}_{i,j}:=t_{m^{\prime}+1-i,n^{\prime}+1-j}\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{\partial}_{i,j}:=\partial_{m^{\prime}+1-i,n^{\prime}+1-j}.

Also, let
\tilde{t}_{i,j},\tilde{\partial}_{i,j}\in\mathscr{PD}_{m\times n}

(respectively,

\tilde{t}_{i,j},\tilde{\partial}_{i,j}\in\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{m\times n}
)
for
1\leq i\leq m
and
1\leq j\leq n

be as in (
5
) for
a:=m
and
b:=n
, that is

\tilde{t}_{i,j}:=t_{m+1-i,n+1-j}\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{\partial}_{i,j}:=\partial_{m+1-i,n+1-j}.

Then the following assertions hold.

(i)

The assignments

\tilde{t}_{i,j}\mapsto\tilde{t}_{i,j}
and

\tilde{\partial}_{i,j}\mapsto\tilde{\partial}_{i,j}
for
1\leq i\leq m^{\prime}
and
1\leq j\leq n^{\prime}
define
unique embeddings
of algebras

\boldsymbol{e}:=\boldsymbol{e}_{m^{\prime}\times n^{\prime}}^{m\times n}:\mathscr{PD}_{m^{\prime}\times n^{\prime}}\to\mathscr{PD}_{m\times n}\quad\text{and}\quad\boldsymbol{e}^{\mathrm{gr}}:=(\boldsymbol{e}^{\mathrm{gr}})_{m^{\prime}\times n^{\prime}}^{m\times n}:\mathscr{PD}_{m^{\prime}\times n^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{gr}}\to\mathscr{PD}_{m\times n}^{\mathrm{gr}}.

(ii)

If we identify
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m^{\prime}})\otimes U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n^{\prime}})
with a subalgebra of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m})\otimes U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
via
\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{m^{\prime},m}\otimes\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{n^{\prime},n}
then
the maps
\boldsymbol{e}

and
\boldsymbol{e}^{\mathrm{gr}}
are

U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m^{\prime}})\otimes U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n^{\prime}})
-equivariant.

Proof.

We only give the details of the proofs of these assertions for
\mathscr{PD}
. The arguments for
\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr}}
are analogous.

(i) From Definition
3.7.3
and Remark
3.7.4
it follows that
the generators
\tilde{t}_{i,j}
and
\tilde{\partial}_{i,j}
of
\mathscr{PD}_{m^{\prime}\times n^{\prime}}
and
\mathscr{PD}_{m\times n}
satisfy identical relations. It follows that there exists a homomorphism of algebras
\mathscr{PD}_{m^{\prime}\times n^{\prime}}\to\mathscr{PD}_{m\times n}
.
By Proposition
3.8.1
the latter map takes a basis of
\mathscr{PD}_{m^{\prime}\times n^{\prime}}
to a basis of
\mathscr{PD}_{m\times n}
, hence it is an injection. The uniqueness assertion is trivial.

(ii) We give the proof for
\boldsymbol{e}
only, since the proof for
\boldsymbol{e}^{\mathrm{gr}}
is similar.
Since
\mathscr{PD}_{m^{\prime}\times n^{\prime}}
and
\mathscr{PD}_{m\times n}
are module algebras it suffices to verify equivariance for standard generators of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m^{\prime}})
and
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n^{\prime}})
on the
\tilde{t}_{i,j}
and the
\tilde{\partial}_{i,j}
. This can be done using the explicit formulas of Remark
3.6.2
.
∎

3.10.
The action of
\mathscr{PD}
on
\mathscr{P}
and the map
\phi_{PD}

Recall that by Proposition
2.4.10
,
\mathscr{PD}
is a
U_{LR}
-module algebra.
We denote the action of
x\in U_{LR}
on
D\in\mathscr{PD}
by
x\cdot D
.

Let
\mathscr{I}
denote the left ideal of
\mathscr{PD}
that is generated by
\mathscr{D}^{(1)}
.
By Proposition
3.8.1
we have a
U_{LR}
-invariant decomposition

\mathscr{PD}\cong\mathscr{I}\oplus\mathscr{P}.

This decomposition equips
\mathscr{P}\cong\mathscr{PD}/\mathscr{I}
with
a
\mathscr{PD}
-module structure given by

(64)

\mathscr{PD}\otimes\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{P}\ ,\ D\otimes(f+\mathscr{I})\mapsto D\cdot f\quad\text{for }D\in\mathscr{PD}\text{ and }f\in\mathscr{P},

where
D\cdot f:=(Df)+\mathscr{I}
.

Definition 3.10.1
.

The map
\phi_{PD}:\mathscr{PD}\to\mathrm{End}_{\Bbbk}(\mathscr{P})
is the homomorphism of algebras induced by (
64
).

To simplify our notation, henceforth for
X\in\mathscr{PD}
and
f\in\mathscr{P}
we write
X\cdot f
instead of
\phi_{PD}(X)f
.

Lemma 3.10.2
.

The map (
64
) is a
U_{LR}
-module homomorphism.

Proof.

This is a consequence of the following general fact: let
H
be a Hopf algebra,
A
be an
H
-module algebra, and
I\subseteq A
be an
H
-stable left ideal of
A
. Then the canonical
A
-module structure map
A\otimes A/I\to A/I
is an
H
-module homomorphism.
∎

3.11.
\mathscr{P}
is a faithful
\mathscr{PD}
-module

The goal of this subsection is to provide a purely algebraic proof of Proposition
3.11.4
.
This proposition is also proved in
[
SSV04
, Thm 2.6]
using analytic tools.

Lemma 3.11.1
.

Let
\mathscr{P}^{(\leq k)}:=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{k}\mathscr{P}^{(i)}

for
k\geq 0
.
Then
\partial_{i,j}\cdot\mathscr{P}^{(\leq k)}\subseteq\mathscr{P}^{(\leq k-1)}
.

Proof.

Follows by
induction on
k
and the mixed relations (R3)–(R6) in Subsection
3.7
.
∎

Recall the total order
\prec
on the set of pairs

(i,j)
with
1\leq i\leq m
and
1\leq j\leq n
from Definition
3.8.5
.

Lemma 3.11.2
.

Assume that
(i_{r},j_{r})\prec(i,j)
for
1\leq r\leq k
. Then

\partial_{i,j}\cdot(t_{i_{1},j_{1}}\cdots t_{i_{k},j_{k}})=0
.

Proof.

We use induction on
k
. From
(i_{1},j_{1})\prec(i,j)
it follows that either
i>i_{1}
or
j>j_{1}
. If
i>i_{1}
then
by the mixed relations (R3) or (R5) we have

\partial_{i,j}t_{i_{1},j_{1}}\cdots t_{i_{k},j_{k}}=c_{1}t_{i_{1},j_{1}}\partial_{i,j}t_{i_{2},j_{2}}\cdots t_{i_{k},j_{k}}+\delta_{j,j_{1}}c_{2}\sum_{j^{\prime}>j}t_{i_{1},j^{\prime}}\partial_{i,j^{\prime}}t_{i_{2},j_{2}}\cdots t_{i_{k},j_{k}},

for some
c_{1},c_{2}\in\Bbbk
.
The claim now follows from the induction hypothesis, because
i+j^{\prime}>i+j
and therefore
(i,j)\prec(i,j^{\prime})
. When
j>j_{1}
the argument is similar.
∎

For
a\in\mathbb{Z}
we set

(65)

\mathbf{c}(a):=\begin{cases}\sum_{i=0}^{a}q^{2i}&\text{ if }a\geq 0,\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Lemma 3.11.3
.

Assume that
(i_{r},j_{r})\prec(i,j)
for
1\leq r\leq k
. Then

\partial_{i,j}\cdot(t^{a}_{i,j}t_{i_{1},j_{1}}\cdots t_{i_{k},j_{k}})=\mathbf{c}(a-1)t_{i,j}^{a-1}t_{i_{1},j_{1}}\cdots t_{i_{k},j_{k}}\quad\text{for $a\geq 1$}.

Proof.

The mixed relation (R6) implies

\displaystyle\partial_{i,j}t_{i,j}^{a}t_{i_{1},j_{1}}

\displaystyle\cdots t_{i_{k},j_{k}}=t_{i,j}^{a-1}t_{i_{1},j_{1}}\cdots t_{i_{k},j_{k}}+q^{2}t_{i,j}\partial_{i,j}t_{i,j}^{a-1}t_{i_{1},j_{1}}\cdots t_{i_{k},j_{k}}

\displaystyle+(q^{2}-1)\sum_{i^{\prime}>i}t_{i^{\prime},j}\partial_{i^{\prime},j}t_{i,j}^{a-1}t_{i_{1},j_{1}}\cdots t_{i_{k},j_{k}}+(q^{2}-1)\sum_{j^{\prime}>j}t_{i,j^{\prime}}\partial_{i,j^{\prime}}t_{i,j}^{a-1}t_{i_{1},j_{1}}\cdots t_{i_{k},j_{k}}

\displaystyle+(q-q^{-1})^{2}\sum_{i^{\prime}>i,j^{\prime}>j}t_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}\partial_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}t_{i,j}^{a-1}t_{i_{1},j_{1}}\cdots t_{i_{k},j_{k}}.

Since
\min\{i^{\prime}+j,i+j^{\prime},i^{\prime}+j^{\prime}\}>i+j
,
by Lemma
3.11.2

the sums on the second and the third line lie in the ideal
\mathscr{I}
. The assertion follows by induction on
a
.
∎

Proposition 3.11.4
.

\mathscr{P}
is a faithful
\mathscr{PD}
-module.

Proof.

Let
D\in\mathscr{PD}
and assume that
D\neq 0
. Then
D=\sum_{d\geq 0}D_{d}
where each
D_{d}
is a linear combination of monomials of the form (
60
) with
\sum_{i,j}b_{i,j}=d
.
Set
d_{\circ}:=\min\{d:D_{d}\neq 0\}
. By Lemma
3.11.1
, for
f\in\mathscr{P}^{(d_{\circ})}
we have
D\cdot f=D_{d_{\circ}}\cdot f
.
Let
\mathcal{T}
denote the set of all the
mn
-tuples
\mathsf{b}:=(b_{i,j})
for which a monomial of the form (
60
) occurs in
D_{d_{\circ}}
with a nonzero coefficient. We sort the components of the
\mathsf{b}:=(b_{i,j})
according to
\prec
on the pairs
(i,j)
. In other words, we assume that

\mathsf{b}:=(b_{1,1},b_{1,2},b_{2,1},\ldots,b_{m-1,n},b_{m,n-1},b_{m,n})
.
Let
\tilde{\mathsf{b}}:=(\tilde{b}_{i,j})
be the minimum of
\mathcal{T}
in the reverse lexicographic order.
Thus, we have

\tilde{b}_{m,n}=\min\{b_{m,n}\,:\,(b_{i,j})\in\mathcal{T}\},

then also

\tilde{b}_{m-1,n}=\min\{b_{m-1,n}\,:\,(b_{i,j})\in\mathcal{T}\text{ and }b_{m,n}=\tilde{b}_{m,n}\}
, and so on.
From
Lemma
3.11.2

and Lemma
3.11.3
it follows that

D_{d_{\circ}}\cdot\prod_{i,j}t^{\tilde{b}_{i,j}}\neq 0
.
∎

3.12.
Two
U_{LR}
-actions on
\mathscr{PD}
are identical

By Proposition
3.11.4
the map
\phi_{PD}
is an injection and consequently we can consider
\mathscr{PD}
as a subalgebra of
\mathrm{End}_{\Bbbk}(\mathscr{P})
. Thus according to Lemma
2.3.1
there exists another action of
U_{LR}
on elements of
\mathscr{PD}
. We temporarily denote this action by
x\bullet D
for
x\in U_{LR}
and
D\in\mathscr{PD}
.
In the following proposition, we show that the latter action is identical to the action that is defined in the beginning of Subsection
3.10
.

Proposition 3.12.1
.

x\bullet D=x\cdot D

for
x\in U_{LR}
and
D\in\mathscr{PD}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.10.2
, for
f\in\mathscr{P}
we have

\displaystyle(x\bullet D)\cdot f=\sum x_{1}\cdot(D\cdot

\displaystyle(S(x_{2})\cdot f))=\sum(x_{1}\cdot D)\cdot(x_{2}\cdot(S(x_{3})\cdot f))

\displaystyle=\sum(x_{1}\cdot D)\cdot(\epsilon(x_{2})f)=\sum(x_{1}\epsilon(x_{2})\cdot D)\cdot f=(x\cdot D)\cdot f.

Since
\mathscr{P}
is a faithful module over
\mathrm{End}_{\Bbbk}(\mathscr{P})
, it follows that
x\bullet D=x\cdot D
.
∎

Henceforth we only use the notation
x\cdot D
to denote the
U_{LR}
-action on
\mathscr{PD}
.

3.13.
The maps
\mathsf{P}_{k,l,n}

Recall that
\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr}}\cong\mathscr{P}\otimes\mathscr{D}
as a vector space.
Let

(66)

\mathsf{P}:\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr}}\to\mathscr{PD}

be the linear map uniquely defined by

\mathsf{P}(a\otimes b):=ab
for
a\in\mathscr{P}
and
b\in\mathscr{D}
.

Proposition 3.13.1
.

The map
\mathsf{P}
is an isomorphism of
U_{LR}
-modules.

Proof.

The map
\mathsf{P}
is identical to the map (
25
)
when

A:=\mathscr{P}
,
B:=\mathscr{D}
and
H:=U_{LR}
. Hence by Remark
2.4.11
it is
a homomorphism of
U_{LR}
-modules.
From Proposition
3.8.1

and Proposition
3.8.3

it follows that
\mathsf{P}
maps a basis of
\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr}}
to a basis of
\mathscr{PD}
, hence it is indeed an isomorphism of
U_{LR}
-modules.
∎

Definition 3.13.2
.

Given
k,l,n\geq 1
, we set
m:=\max\{k,l\}
and define the map

(67)

\mathsf{P}_{k,l,n}:\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}}\to\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}\ ,\ D\mapsto\mathsf{P}(D),

where
\mathsf{P}:\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr}}\to\mathscr{PD}
is as in (
66
).

For
r,s\in\mathbb{Z}^{\geq 0}
we set

(68)

\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr},(r,s)}:=\mathscr{P}^{(r)}\otimes\mathscr{D}^{(s)}\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr},(r,s)}:=\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}}\cap\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr},(r,s)},

so that

\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr}}=\bigoplus_{r,s\geq 0}\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr},(r,s)}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}}:=\bigoplus_{r,s\geq 0}\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr},(r,s)}.

By
Proposition
3.6.1

we obtain an isomorphism of

U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{k})\otimes U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{l})\otimes U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})\otimes U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
-modules

(69)

\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr},(r,s)}\cong\bigoplus_{\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{c}\lambda\in\Lambda_{\underline{k},r}\\[-2.84526pt]
\mu\in\Lambda_{\underline{l},s}\end{array}}(V_{\lambda}^{*}\otimes V_{\mu})\otimes(V_{\lambda}^{*}\otimes V_{\mu}),

where
\underline{k}:=\min\{k,n\}
and
\underline{l}:=\min\{l,n\}
.
Here we consider the left copy of
V_{\lambda}^{*}\otimes V_{\mu}
as a module for
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{k})\otimes U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{l})
and the right copy of
V_{\lambda}^{*}\otimes V_{\mu}
as a module for
U_{R}\otimes U_{R}\cong U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})\otimes U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
. Of course by restriction along the coproduct map
U_{R}\to U_{R}\otimes U_{R}
we can also consider the right copy as a
U_{R}
-module.

Proposition 3.13.3
.

For
a\otimes a^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}^{(r)}\otimes_{\tilde{\mathcal{R}}}\mathscr{D}^{(r^{\prime})}
and
b\otimes b^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}^{(s)}\otimes_{\tilde{\mathcal{R}}}\mathscr{D}^{(s^{\prime})}
we have

(70)

\mathsf{P}(a\otimes a^{\prime})\mathsf{P}(b\otimes b^{\prime})-\mathsf{P}\big((a\otimes a^{\prime})(b\otimes b^{\prime})\big)\in\bigoplus_{i=1}^{\min\{u,u^{\prime}\}}\mathscr{PD}^{(u-i,u^{\prime}-i)},

where
u:=r+s
and
u^{\prime}:=r^{\prime}+s^{\prime}
.

Proof.

We have
\mathsf{P}(a\otimes a^{\prime})\mathsf{P}(b\otimes b^{\prime})=aa^{\prime}bb^{\prime}
.
Using the explicit relations of
\mathscr{PD}
(see Remark
3.7.4
) we can move the
\partial_{i,j}
past the
t_{i,j}
to express

a^{\prime}b
as
a^{\prime}b=\sum a^{\prime\prime}b^{\prime\prime}
where
a^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{P}
and
b^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{D}
. Thus

\mathsf{P}(a\otimes a^{\prime})\mathsf{P}(b\otimes b^{\prime})=\sum aa^{\prime\prime}b^{\prime\prime}b^{\prime}.

Similarly, using the relations of
\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr}}
we can move the
1\otimes\partial_{i,j}
past the
t_{i,j}\otimes 1
and as a result we obtain
(1\otimes a^{\prime})(b\otimes 1)=\sum\underline{a}^{\prime\prime}\otimes\underline{b}^{\prime\prime}
where
\underline{a}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{P}
and
\underline{b}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{D}
. It follows that

\mathsf{P}\big((a\otimes a^{\prime})(b\otimes b^{\prime})\big)=\sum a\underline{a}^{\prime\prime}\underline{b}^{\prime\prime}b^{\prime}.

The only difference between the relations of
\mathscr{PD}
and
\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr}}
is (R6) vs. (R6
′
). Since (R6
′
) is the homogenized form of (R6
′
),
it follows that

\sum a^{\prime\prime}b^{\prime\prime}-\sum\underline{a}^{\prime\prime}\underline{b}^{\prime\prime}\in\bigoplus_{i=1}^{\min\{r^{\prime},s\}}\mathscr{PD}^{(s-i,r^{\prime}-i)}.

From the latter inclusion (
70
) follows immediately.
∎

Recall that
\mathrm{gr}(\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n})
denotes the associated graded algebra corresponding to the degree filtration on
\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}
, i.e., the filtration obtained by setting
\deg(t_{i,j})=\deg(\partial_{i,j})=1
. Note that we have a canonical isomorphism
\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}}\cong\mathrm{gr}(\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}})
since
\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}}
is graded.

The maps
\mathsf{P}_{k,l,n}:\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}}\to\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}
do
not
induce isomorphisms of associative algebras. However, the following statement holds.

Corollary 3.13.4
.

The associated graded map
\mathrm{gr}(\mathsf{P}_{k,l,n})
induces a
U_{R}
-equivariant isomorphism between

\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}}\cong\mathrm{gr}(\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}})
and
\mathrm{gr}(\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n})
.
When
k=l=m
, the latter map is
U_{LR}
-equivariant.

Proof.

From Proposition
3.13.3
it follows that the associated graded map
\mathrm{gr}(\mathsf{P}_{k,l,n})
is an isomorphism of associative algebras from
\mathrm{gr}(\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}})\cong\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}}
onto

\mathrm{gr}(\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n})
.
The equivariance statements follow from Proposition
3.13.1
.
∎

3.14.
The algebras
\mathring{U}_{L}
,
\mathring{U}_{R}
and
\mathring{U}_{LR}

We set

\mathring{U}_{LR}:=\phi_{U}^{-1}(\mathscr{PD}):=\{x\in U_{LR}\,:\,\phi_{U}(x)\in\mathscr{PD}\}.

Also, we set

\mathring{U}_{L}:=\{x\in U_{L}\,:\,x\otimes 1\in\mathring{U}_{LR}\}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathring{U}_{R}:=\{x\in U_{R}\,:\,1\otimes x\in\mathring{U}_{LR}\}.

Recall that the adjoint action of
U_{LR}
is
\mathrm{ad}_{y}(x):=\sum y_{1}xS(y_{2})
for
x,y\in U_{LR}
.
Recall that we equip
\mathrm{End}_{\Bbbk}(\mathscr{P})
with the

U_{LR}
-module structure of
Lemma
2.3.1
and we denote the latter action by

x\cdot T
for

x\in U_{LR}
and
T\in\mathrm{End}_{\Bbbk}(\mathscr{P})
.
Then it is straightforward from the definition of
\phi_{U}
that

(71)

\phi_{U}({\mathrm{ad}_{y}(x)})=y\cdot\phi_{U}(x)\quad\text{
for $x,y\in U_{LR}$.
}

Lemma 3.14.1
.

\mathrm{ad}_{x}(\mathring{U}_{LR})\subseteq\mathring{U}_{LR}
for
x\in U_{LR}
.

Proof.

Follows immediately from (
71
) and Proposition
3.12.1
.
∎

Remark 3.14.2
.

When
m\leq n
, Proposition
3.6.1
implies that the map
\mathcal{L}_{\mathscr{P}}:U_{L}\to\mathrm{End}_{\Bbbk}(\mathscr{P})
is an injection. The argument is similar to the proof of
[
KS97
, Thm 7.1.5.13]
.

Let
\mathscr{K}_{n}
denote the kernel of

\mathcal{R}_{\mathscr{P}}:U_{R}\to\mathrm{End}_{\Bbbk}(\mathscr{P})
.
For the next proposition recall the notation
\mathscr{F}(H,I)
defined
in (
11
).

Proposition 3.14.3
.

Assume that
m\leq n
. Then
\mathring{U}_{L}\subseteq\mathscr{F}(U_{L})
and

\mathring{U}_{R}\subseteq\mathscr{F}(U_{R},\mathscr{K}_{n})
.

Proof.

Since the actions of
U_{LR}
on
\mathscr{P}
and
\mathscr{D}
are degree preserving, it follows that

\mathscr{PD}
is a locally finite
U_{LR}
-module. The assertions of the proposition follow from the fact that the maps

\mathring{U}_{L}\xrightarrow{\phi_{U}}\mathscr{PD}
and
\mathring{U}_{R}/(\mathring{U}_{R}\cap\mathscr{K}_{n})\xrightarrow{\phi_{U}}\mathscr{PD}
are injective and
U_{LR}
-equivariant (this is equivalent to
(
71
)).
∎

The following proposition is a “no-go theorem” that provides evidence that the commutative diagram (
1
) cannot be fully quantized.

Proposition 3.14.4
.

There does not exist a
\Bbbk
-algebra
\widetilde{\mathscr{PD}}
with the following properties:

(i)

\widetilde{\mathscr{PD}}
is a locally finite
U_{LR}
-module.

(ii)

\mathscr{P}
is a
\widetilde{\mathscr{PD}}
-module and the action map

\widetilde{\mathscr{PD}}\otimes\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{P}
is a homomorphism of
U_{LR}
-module.

(iii)

There exists a homomorphism of algebras
\widetilde{\phi}:U_{LR}\to\widetilde{\mathscr{PD}}
such that the diagram

\textstyle{U_{LR}\otimes\mathscr{P}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{x\otimes f\mapsto\widetilde{\phi}(x)\otimes f\hskip 14.22636pt}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 14.22636ptx\otimes f\mapsto x\cdot f}
\textstyle{\mathscr{P}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathscr{PD}}\otimes\mathscr{P}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{D\otimes f\mapsto D\cdot f}

is commutative.

Proof.

Let us denote the
U_{LR}
-action of (i) by
x\cdot D
for
x\in U_{LR}
and
D\in\widetilde{\mathscr{PD}}
.
Let
\mathscr{K}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathscr{PD}}
denote the kernel of the map
\widetilde{\mathscr{PD}}\to\mathrm{End}_{\Bbbk}(\mathscr{P})
that is induced by the
\widetilde{\mathscr{PD}}
-module structure on
\mathscr{P}
.
We define a new
U_{LR}
-action on
\widetilde{\mathscr{PD}}
by setting

x\bullet D:=\sum\widetilde{\phi}(x_{1})D\widetilde{\phi}(S(x_{2}))\quad\text{for }x\in U_{LR}\text{ and }D\in\widetilde{\mathscr{PD}}.

By the proof of Proposition
3.12.1
we obtain
(x\bullet D)-(x\cdot D)\in\mathscr{K}
for
x\in U_{LR}
and
D\in\widetilde{\mathscr{PD}}
.
In particular, for
x,y\in U_{LR}
if we set
D:=\widetilde{\phi}(y)
then we have

(72)

\widetilde{\phi}(\mathrm{ad}_{x}(y))+\mathscr{K}=x\bullet D+\mathscr{K}=x\cdot D+\mathscr{K}.

Now assume that
m\leq n
, so that the restriction of
\widetilde{\phi}
to a map
U_{L}\otimes 1\to\mathrm{End}_{\Bbbk}(\mathscr{P})
is faithful.
By (
72
) it follows that if
y\in U_{L}\otimes 1
then the image of
\widetilde{\phi}(\mathrm{ad}_{U_{L}\otimes 1}(y))
in
\widetilde{\mathscr{PD}}/\mathscr{K}
is finite dimensional. But
\widetilde{\phi}(\mathrm{ad}_{U_{L}\otimes 1}(y))\cap\mathscr{K}=\{0\}
, hence

\mathrm{ad}_{U_{L}\otimes 1}(y)
is also finite dimensional.
Consequently, we have shown that
U_{L}=\mathscr{F}(U_{L})
, which is a contradiction.
∎

3.15.
Relation between
Theorem
A
(i) and
Theorem
A
(ii)

Our goal in this subsection is to prove
Lemma
3.15.1
below, which implies that
Theorem
A
(ii) follows by symmetry from
Theorem
A
(i).

From the symmetry of the defining relations of
\mathscr{PD}_{m\times n}
with respect to the two indices of the generators
t_{i,j}
and
\partial_{i,j}
it follows that there exists an isomorphism of algebras

\eta_{m,n}:\mathscr{PD}_{m\times n}\to\mathscr{PD}_{n\times m},

such that
\eta_{m,n}(t_{i,j})=t_{j,i}
and
\eta_{m,n}(\partial_{i,j})=\partial_{j,i}
for
1\leq i\leq m
and
1\leq j\leq n
. Note that
\eta_{m,n}
restricts to an isomorphism
\mathscr{P}_{m\times n}\cong\mathscr{P}_{n\times m}
. This naturally results in an isomorphism of algebras
\mathrm{End}_{\Bbbk}(\mathscr{P}_{m\times n})\cong\mathrm{End}_{\Bbbk}(\mathscr{P}_{n\times m})
.

Lemma 3.15.1
.

The following assertions hold.

(i)

For
x\otimes y\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m})\otimes U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
and
D\in\mathscr{PD}_{m\times n}

we have

\eta_{m,n}((x\otimes y)\cdot D)=(y\otimes x)\cdot\eta_{m,n}(D).

(ii)

The induced isomorphism

\mathrm{End}_{\Bbbk}(\mathscr{P}_{m\times n})\cong\mathrm{End}_{\Bbbk}(\mathscr{P}_{n\times m})
maps the images of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m})
and
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
in
\mathrm{End}_{\Bbbk}(\mathscr{P}_{m\times n})
onto the images of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m})
and
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
in

\mathrm{End}_{\Bbbk}(\mathscr{P}_{n\times m})
.

Proof.

(i) It suffices to prove the assertion when
x
and
y
are selected from the standard generators of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m})
and
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
, respectively. If
D=t_{i,j}
or
D=\partial_{i,j}
, then the assertion follows from symmetry of the effect of the generators on the indices of the
t_{i,j}
and the
\partial_{i,j}
(see
Remark
3.6.2
). For general
D\in\mathscr{PD}_{m\times n}
the assertion follows from the fact that
\mathscr{PD}_{m\times n}
and
\mathscr{PD}_{n\times m}
are module algebras over
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m})\otimes U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
and
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})\otimes U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m})
, respectively.

(ii) Follows immediately from (i).
∎

3.16.
Relation to
\mathbb{C}[\mathrm{Mat}_{m,n}]_{q}
and
\mathrm{Pol}(\mathrm{Mat}_{m,n})_{q}

We can now relate our algebras
\mathscr{P}
and
\mathscr{PD}
to the
\mathbb{C}
-algebras

\mathbb{C}[\mathrm{Mat}_{m,n}]_{q}
and

\mathrm{Pol}(\mathrm{Mat}_{m,n})_{q}
(where
0<q<1
) that are introduced in
[
SSV04
,
BKV06
]
.
We remark that in
[
BKV06
]
only the special case
m=n
is considered, and the latter algebras
are denoted by

\mathbb{C}[\mathrm{Mat}_{n}]_{q}
and

\mathrm{Pol}(\mathrm{Mat}_{n})_{q}
,
respectively.
The algebra
\mathrm{Pol}(\mathrm{Mat}_{m,n})_{q}
is defined in
[
SSV04
, Sec. 2]
in terms of the generators
z^{i}_{j}
and
(z^{i}_{j})^{*}
where
1\leq i\leq m
and
1\leq j\leq n
. These generators satisfy
the relations (2.1)–(2.7) of
[
SSV04
]
.
For the reader’s convenience we describe the relations of
\mathrm{Pol}(\mathrm{Mat}_{m,n})_{q}
. The relations among the
z^{i}_{j}
(which are (2.1)–(2.3) of
[
SSV04
]
) are identical to the relations among the
t_{i,j}
, the only difference being that
q
becomes a complex-valued parameter. In a similar way, the relations among the
(z^{i}_{j})^{*}

(which are (2.4)–(2.6) of
[
SSV04
]
)
are identical to the relations among the
\partial_{i,j}
.
The mixed relations (which correspond to (2.7) of
[
SSV04
]
) are

(z^{i}_{j})^{*}z^{k}_{l}=q^{2}\sum_{1\leq a,b\leq n}\sum_{1\leq c,d\leq m}(r^{b,a}_{j,l})(r^{d,c}_{i,k})z^{c}_{a}(z^{d}_{b})^{*}+\llbracket j,l\rrbracket\llbracket i,k\rrbracket(1-q^{2}),

where
r^{i,i}_{i,i}=1
,
r^{i,j}_{i,j}=q^{-1}
for
i\neq j
,
r^{j,j}_{i,i}=1-q^{-2}
for
j>i
, and
r^{i,j}_{k,l}=0
otherwise.
The algebra
\mathbb{C}[\mathrm{Mat}_{m,n}]_{q}
is the subalgebra
of

\mathrm{Pol}(\mathrm{Mat}_{m,n})_{q}
that is generated by the
z^{i}_{j}
.

Throughout this subsection we set
A:=\mathbb{Z}[q,q^{-1}]
. The relations of
\mathscr{PD}
in Definition
3.7.3
are defined over
A
. Thus we obtain an integral form
\mathscr{PD}^{A}
of
\mathscr{PD}
by considering the free
A
-submodule of
\mathscr{PD}
that is generated by the monomials (
59
).
Evaluation at
q_{\circ}
for
0<q_{\circ}<1
results in a ring homomorphism
A\to\mathbb{C}
.
Set
\mathscr{PD}_{\langle q_{\circ}\rangle}:=\mathscr{PD}^{A}\otimes_{A}\mathbb{C}
.

Corollary 3.16.1
.

The algebras
\mathscr{PD}_{\langle q_{\circ}\rangle}
and

\mathrm{Pol}(\mathrm{Mat}_{m,n})_{q_{\circ}}

are isomorphic
by the assignments
t_{i,j}\mapsto\frac{z^{i}_{j}}{\sqrt{1-q_{\circ}^{2}}}
and

\partial_{i,j}\mapsto\frac{(z^{i}_{j})^{*}}{\sqrt{1-q_{\circ}^{2}}}
.

Proof.

It is straightforward to check that these assignments intertwine the relations (R1)–(R6) with the relations (2.1)–(2.7) of
[
SSV04
]
.
∎

In the rest of this subsection we relate the actions of quantized enveloping algebras on
\mathscr{P}
and
on
\mathbb{C}[\mathrm{Mat}_{m,n}]_{q}
.
Set
\mathscr{P}^{A}:=\mathscr{P}\cap\mathscr{PD}^{A}
. Then

\mathscr{P}^{A}

is a free
A
-module, with an
A
-basis that consists of the monomials

t_{1,1}^{a_{1,1}}\cdots t_{1,n}^{a_{1,n}}\cdots t_{m,1}^{a_{m,1}}\cdots t_{m,n}^{a_{m,n}}.

Let
U_{q}^{A}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
denote the restricted integral
A
-form of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
. For integral forms of quantized enveloping algebras see for example
[
CP94
, Sec. 9.3]
.
The explicit description of
U_{q}^{A}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})

is given
for example in
[
RT10
]
. We denote the analogous integral form of the algebra
U_{LR}\cong U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m})\otimes U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
by
U_{LR}^{A}
. Thus

U_{LR}^{A}\cong U_{q}^{A}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m})\otimes_{A}U_{q}^{A}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n}).

For
0<q_{\circ}<1

set
U_{q_{\circ}}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n}):=U_{q}^{A}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})\otimes_{A}\mathbb{C}
.
By Remark
3.6.2

the map
U_{LR}\otimes\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{P}
that describes the
U_{LR}
-module structure on
\mathscr{P}
restricts to a map

U_{LR}^{A}\otimes\mathscr{P}^{A}\to\mathscr{P}^{A}
. After the scalar extension
(\--)\otimes_{A}\mathbb{C}
and using the isomorphism
\mathscr{P}^{A}\otimes_{A}\mathbb{C}\cong\mathbb{C}[\mathrm{Mat}_{m,n}]_{q_{\circ}}
we obtain a structure of a

U_{q_{\circ}}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m})\otimes U_{q_{\circ}}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
-module
on
\mathbb{C}[\mathrm{Mat}_{m,n}]_{q_{\circ}}
that corresponds to a map

(73)

\big(U_{q_{\circ}}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m})\otimes U_{q_{\circ}}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})\big)\otimes\mathbb{C}[\mathrm{Mat}_{m,n}]_{q_{\circ}}\to\mathbb{C}[\mathrm{Mat}_{m,n}]_{q_{\circ}},

or equivalently a homomorphism of algebras

(74)

\phi_{U,q_{\circ}}:U_{q_{\circ}}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m})\otimes U_{q_{\circ}}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})\to\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{C}}\left(\mathbb{C}[\mathrm{Mat}_{m,n}]_{q_{\circ}}\right).

The next proposition relates the latter module structure
to the one given in
[
SSV04
, Sec. 9–10]
and
[
BKV06
, Sec. 3]
. We remark that in
[
SSV04
,
BKV06
]
, the module algebra structure on

\mathbb{C}[\mathrm{Mat}_{m,n}]_{q_{\circ}}

is with respect to

U_{q_{\circ}}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m})^{\mathrm{cop}}\otimes U_{q_{\circ}}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})^{\mathrm{cop}}
. We denote the latter module structure by the map

\phi_{\mathrm{SSV}}:U_{q_{\circ}}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m})^{\mathrm{cop}}\otimes U_{q_{\circ}}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})^{\mathrm{cop}}\to\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{C}}\left(\mathbb{C}[\mathrm{Mat}_{m,n}]_{q_{\circ}}\right).

Let
x\mapsto x^{\natural}
for
n\geq 1
be the
\mathbb{C}
-linear
isomorphism of Hopf algebras
U_{q_{\circ}}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})\to U_{q_{\circ}}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})^{\mathrm{op}}

that is given by the same relations as
(
48
) but for
q:=q_{\circ}
.

Proposition 3.16.2
.

With
\phi_{U,\langle q_{\circ}\rangle}
and
\phi_{\mathrm{SSV}}
as above, we have

\phi_{U,q_{\circ}}(x\otimes y)=\phi_{\mathrm{SSV}}\left(\vartheta_{L}(S(x^{\natural}))\otimes\vartheta_{R}(S(y^{\natural}))\right)\quad\text{for }x\otimes y\in U_{q_{\circ}}(\mathfrak{gl}_{m})\otimes U_{q_{\circ}}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n}),

where

\vartheta_{L}
and
\vartheta_{R}
are the automorphisms of the Hopf algebra
U_{q_{\circ}}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
that are uniquely defined by setting

\vartheta_{L}(E_{i}):=q_{\circ}^{-\frac{1}{2}}F_{m-i}\quad,\quad\vartheta_{L}(F_{i}):=q_{\circ}^{\frac{1}{2}}E_{m-i}\quad,\quad\vartheta_{L}(K_{\varepsilon_{i}}):=K_{\varepsilon_{m+1-i}}^{-1},

and

\vartheta_{R}(E_{i}):=q_{\circ}^{-\frac{1}{2}}F_{i}\quad,\quad\vartheta_{R}(F_{i}):=q_{\circ}^{\frac{1}{2}}E_{i}\quad,\quad\vartheta_{L}(K_{\varepsilon_{i}}):=K_{\varepsilon_{i}}.

Proof.

Follows from comparing Remark
3.6.2
with

[
SSV04
, Sec. 8]
or
[
BKV06
, Eqs (14), (15)]
. Note that the coproduct of
U_{q_{\circ}}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
in
[
BKV06
]
is co-opposite to the coproduct considered in the present paper, but this is corrected by composing with
x\mapsto S(x^{\natural})
.
∎

3.17.
Some technical statements about the action of
\mathscr{PD}
on
\mathscr{P}

In this subsection we prove several technical statements about the interaction between the
\partial_{i,j}
on
\mathscr{P}
. We will need these statements in the upcoming sections of this paper. In order to make our exposition more organized we have collected all of them in one subsection. The reader may find it easier to skip this subsection and return to it whenever there is a reference.

Recall that the action of
D\in\mathscr{PD}
on
f\in\mathscr{P}
is denoted by
D\cdot f
(see Subsection
3.10
).

Lemma 3.17.1
.

Assume that either
i\not\in\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{r}\}
or
j\not\in\{b_{1},\ldots,b_{r}\}
, then

\partial_{i,j}t_{a_{1},b_{1}}\cdots t_{a_{r},b_{r}}
belongs to the left ideal of
\mathscr{PD}
that is generated by the
\partial_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}
satisfying
i^{\prime}\geq i
and
j^{\prime}\geq j
. In particular,

\partial_{i,j}\cdot(t_{a_{1},b_{1}}\cdots t_{a_{r},b_{r}})=0
.

Proof.

We use induction on
r
. For
r=1
the assertion follows from relations (R3)–(R6). Next suppose
r>1
. If
i\neq a_{1}
and
j\neq b_{1}
then
\partial_{i,j}t_{a_{1},b_{1}}=t_{a_{1},b_{1}}\partial_{i,j}
and we can use the induction hypothesis. If
i=a_{1}
then
j\not\in\{b_{1},\ldots,b_{r}\}
and we can write

\partial_{i,j}t_{a_{1},b_{1}}\cdots t_{a_{r},b_{r}}=qt_{i,b_{1}}\partial_{i,j}t_{a_{2},b_{2}}\cdots t_{a_{r},b_{r}}+(q-q^{-1})\sum_{i^{\prime}>i}t_{i^{\prime},b_{1}}\partial_{i^{\prime},j}t_{a_{2},b_{2}}\cdots t_{a_{r},b_{r}},

and again the induction hypothesis is applicable to each summand on the right hand side. The argument for the case
j=b_{1}
is similar.
∎

Lemma 3.17.2
.

Assume that either
\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{s}\}\nsubseteq\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{r}\}

and

\{j_{1},\ldots,j_{s}\}\nsubseteq\{b_{1},\ldots,b_{r}\}
. Then

\partial_{i_{1},j_{1}}\cdots\partial_{i_{s},j_{s}}\cdot\left(t_{a_{1},b_{1}}\cdots t_{a_{r},b_{r}}\right)=0.

Proof.

Without loss of generality assume that
i_{1}\not\in\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{r}\}
.
Relations
(R
1^{\prime}
) and (R
2^{\prime}
) imply that we can replace

\partial_{i_{1},a}\partial_{b,c}
by
either

\partial_{b,c}\partial_{i_{1},a}
or

q^{\pm 1}\partial_{b,c}\partial_{i_{1},a}

or
\partial_{b,c}\partial_{i_{1},a}\pm(q-q^{-1})\partial_{b,a}\partial_{i_{1},c}
. Using the latter replacements we can express
\partial_{i_{1},j_{1}}\cdots\partial_{i_{s},j_{s}}
as a linear combination of monomials that belong to the left ideal
\breve{\mathscr{I}}:=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathscr{D}\partial_{i_{1},j}
of
\mathscr{D}
. From
Lemma
3.17.1
it follows that elements of
\breve{\mathscr{I}}
annihilate

t_{a_{1},b_{1}}\cdots t_{a_{r},b_{r}}
.
∎

For the following corollary recall that
\overline{M}^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{j}}
is the quantum minor defined
in (
7
).

Corollary 3.17.3
.

Let
\mathbf{i}:=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{r})
and
\mathbf{j}:=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{r})
be
r
-tuples of integers that satisfy

1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{r}\leq m\quad\text{and}\quad 1\leq j_{1}<\cdots<j_{r}\leq n.

Then
\overline{M}^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{j}}\cdot\left(t_{a_{1},b_{1}}\cdots t_{a_{s},b_{s}}\right)=0
when
a_{i}\geq i_{1}+1
for all
1\leq i\leq s
.

Proof.

This follows from Lemma
3.17.2
since
\overline{M}^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{j}}
is a linear combination of monomials of the form

\partial_{i_{\sigma(1)},j_{1}}\cdots\partial_{i_{\sigma(r)},j_{r}}
.
∎

Lemma 3.17.4
.

Suppose that
f,g\in\mathscr{P}
satisfy

\partial_{i_{1},j_{1}}\cdots\partial_{i_{r},j_{r}}\cdot f=g
for some

1\leq i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\leq m
and
1\leq j_{1},\ldots,j_{r}\leq n
.
Then for any
1\leq i^{\prime}_{1}\leq\ldots\leq i^{\prime}_{s}\leq m
and

1\leq j^{\prime}_{1}\leq\ldots\leq j^{\prime}_{s}\leq n
that satisfy
either

\min\{j_{u}\}_{u=1}^{r}>\max\{j^{\prime}_{u}\}_{u=1}^{s}

or

\min\{i_{u}\}_{u=1}^{r}>\max\{i^{\prime}_{u}\}_{u=1}^{s}

we have

\partial_{i_{1},j_{1}}\cdots\partial_{i_{r},j_{r}}\cdot(ft_{i^{\prime}_{1},j^{\prime}_{1}}\cdots t_{i^{\prime}_{s},j^{\prime}_{s}})=gt_{i^{\prime}_{1},j^{\prime}_{1}}\cdots t_{i^{\prime}_{s},j^{\prime}_{s}}.

Proof.

We assume
\min\{j_{u}\}_{u=1}^{r}>\max\{j^{\prime}_{u}\}_{u=1}^{s}
(the other case follows by symmetry).
Recall that
\mathscr{I}
is the left ideal of
\mathscr{PD}
generated by
\mathscr{D}^{(1)}
(see Subection
3.10
).
Set

f^{\prime}:=\partial_{i_{r},j_{r}}\cdot f
. Then

\partial_{i_{r},j_{r}}f=f^{\prime}+\sum_{(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})}b_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}\partial_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}
, where the
b_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}\in\mathscr{PD}
and the sum is over all
pairs
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
that satisfy
i_{r}\leq i^{\prime}\leq m
and
j_{r}\leq j^{\prime}\leq n
. In particular

j^{\prime}\not\in\{j^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,j^{\prime}_{s}\}
, hence by
Lemma
3.17.1
we obtain

\partial_{i_{r},j_{r}}ft_{i^{\prime}_{1},j^{\prime}_{1}}\cdots t_{i^{\prime}_{s},j^{\prime}_{s}}=f^{\prime}t_{i^{\prime}_{1},j^{\prime}_{1}}\cdots t_{i^{\prime}_{s},j^{\prime}_{s}}+\sum_{(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})}b_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}\partial_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}t_{i^{\prime}_{1},j^{\prime}_{1}}\cdots t_{i^{\prime}_{s},j^{\prime}_{s}}\in f^{\prime}t_{i^{\prime}_{1},j^{\prime}_{1}}\cdots t_{i^{\prime}_{s},j^{\prime}_{s}}+\mathscr{I}.

This means
\partial_{i_{r},j_{r}}\cdot(ft_{i^{\prime}_{1},j^{\prime}_{1}}\cdots t_{i^{\prime}_{s},j^{\prime}_{s}})=f^{\prime}t_{i^{\prime}_{1},j^{\prime}_{1}}\cdots t_{i^{\prime}_{s},j^{\prime}_{s}}
. The proof is completed by induction on
r
.
∎

Recall the operators
\mathbf{D}^{\prime}_{k,r}
from Section
1
. We have

\mathbf{D}^{\prime}_{1,0}+(q^{2}-1)\mathbf{D}^{\prime}_{1,1}=1+(q^{2}-1)\sum_{i=1}^{n}t_{m,i}\partial_{m,i}.

The next lemma is a consequence of
[
BKV06
, Thm 1]

but we give an elementary, independent proof. This also makes the proofs of Theorem
A
and Theorem
B
independent of
[
BKV06
, Thm 1]
.

Lemma 3.17.5
.

Set

D:=\mathbf{D}^{\prime}_{1,0}+(q^{2}-1)\mathbf{D}^{\prime}_{1,1}
.
Then

D\cdot t_{a_{1},b_{1}}\cdots t_{a_{r},b_{r}}=q^{2\sum_{i=1}^{r}\llbracket m,a_{i}\rrbracket}t_{a_{1},b_{1}}\cdots t_{a_{r},b_{r}}
.

Proof.

By Remark
3.8.6
the monomials

f=f(a_{1,1},\ldots,a_{m,n}):=t_{m,n}^{a_{m,n}}\cdots t_{m,1}^{a_{m,1}}\cdots t_{1,n}^{a_{1,1}}\cdots t_{1,1}^{a_{1,1}}

form a basis of
\mathscr{P}
. From relations (R1) and (R2) it follows that it suffices to prove the assertion for such monomials. By Lemma
3.17.4
the assertion is reduced to the case where
a_{i,j}=0
for
i<m
. For
j>i
we have

t_{m,i}\partial_{m,i}t_{m,j}=q^{2}t_{m,j}t_{m,i}\partial_{m,i}
. By successive application of the latter relation, followed by Lemma
3.11.3
and
Lemma
3.17.4
, we obtain

(q^{2}-1)t_{m,i}\partial_{m,i}\cdot f=q^{2\sum_{j=i+1}^{n}a_{m,j}}(q^{2a_{m,i}}-1)f.

After summing up over
1\leq i\leq n
, the assertion of the lemma is reduced to the algebraic identity

1+\sum_{i=1}^{n}q^{2\sum_{j=i+1}^{n}a_{m,j}}(q^{2a_{m,i}}-1)=q^{2\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{m,i}},

which can be verified by a straightforward computation.
∎

Definition 3.17.6
.

Given any two ordered pairs of integers
(i,j)
and
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
, we write
(i,j)\lhd(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
if
i\leq i^{\prime}
and
j\leq j^{\prime}
and at least one of the latter inequalities is strict.

Let
\mathscr{I}_{a,b}
denote the left ideal of
\mathscr{PD}
that is generated by the
\partial_{i,j}
where
i\geq a
and
j\geq b
.

Lemma 3.17.7
.

Let
a\geq 0
and let
1\leq k\leq n
. Then
\partial_{1,k}t_{1,k}^{a+1}=\mathbf{c}(a)t_{1,k}^{a}+D
where

\mathbf{c}(a)
is as in (
65
) and

D\in\mathscr{I}_{1,k}
.

Proof.

Follows by induction on
a
. For
a=0
the assertion follows from the relation

(75)

\partial_{1,k}t_{1,k}=1+q^{2}t_{1,k}\partial_{1,k}+D_{1}\quad\text{where }D_{1}\in\sum_{(1,k)\lhd(i,\ell)}\mathscr{PD}\partial_{i,\ell}.

Suppose that for a given
a\geq 0
we have
\partial_{1,k}t_{1,k}^{a}=\mathbf{c}(a-1)t_{1,k}^{a-1}+D_{2}
with
D_{2}\in\mathscr{I}_{1,k}
. Using (
75
) we obtain

\partial_{1,k}t_{1,k}^{a+1}=\left(1+q^{2}t_{1,k}\partial_{1,k}+D_{1}\right)t_{1,k}^{a}=(1+q^{2}\mathbf{c}(a-1))t_{1,k}^{a}+q^{2}t_{1,k}D_{2}+D_{1}t_{1,k}^{a}.

From Lemma
3.17.1

it follows that
D_{1}t_{1,k}^{a}\in\mathscr{I}_{1,k}
. Finally note that

\mathbf{c}(a)=1+q^{2}\mathbf{c}(a-1)
.
∎

Lemma 3.17.8
.

Let
a,b\geq 0
and let
1\leq k\leq n
.

(i)

If
b>a
then
\partial_{1,k}^{b+1}t_{1,k}^{a+1}\in\mathscr{I}_{1,k}
.

(ii)

If
b\leq a
then

\partial_{1,k}^{b+1}t_{1,k}^{a+1}=\mathbf{c}(a,b)t_{1,k}^{a-b}+D
for some

\mathbf{c}(a,b)\in\Bbbk
,
where

D\in\mathscr{I}_{1,k}
. Furthermore
\mathbf{c}(a,0)=\mathbf{c}(a)
and
\mathbf{c}(a,b+1)=\mathbf{c}(a,b)\mathbf{c}(a-b-1)
for
b<a
.

Proof.

(i) Follows from the equality

\partial_{1,k}^{b+1}t_{1,k}^{a+1}=\partial_{1,k}^{b-a}\partial_{1,k}^{a+1}t_{1,k}^{a+1}
and Lemma
3.17.7
.

(ii)
We use induction on
b
. For
b=0
this is Lemma
3.17.7
. If
b+1\leq a
then

\displaystyle\partial_{1,k}^{b+2}t_{1,k}^{a+1}

\displaystyle=\partial_{1,k}\partial_{1,k}^{b+1}t_{1,k}^{a+1}=\mathbf{c}(a,b)\partial_{1,k}t_{1,k}^{a-b}+\partial_{1,k}D=\mathbf{c}(a,b)\mathbf{c}(a-b-1)t_{1,k}^{a-b-1}+\mathbf{c}(a,b)D_{1}+\partial_{1,k}D,

where
D_{1},D\in\mathscr{I}_{1,k}
.
Part (ii) follows immediately.
∎

Lemma 3.17.9
.

Let
a,b\geq 0
and let
1\leq k\leq n
. Assume that
f\in\mathscr{P}
is a product of the
t_{1,j}
for
j\leq k-1
.
Then the following hold:

(i)

If
b>a
then
\partial_{1,k}^{b}t_{1,k}^{a}f\in\mathscr{I}_{1,k}
.

(ii)

If
b\leq a
then

\partial_{1,k}^{b+1}t_{1,k}^{a+1}f=\mathbf{c}(a,b)t_{1,k}^{a-b}f+D
where

D\in\mathscr{I}_{1,k}

and
\mathbf{c}(a,b)
is as in Lemma
3.17.8
.

Proof.

(i) Follows from
Lemma
3.17.8
(i) and
Lemma
3.17.1
.

(ii)
From Lemma
3.17.8
(ii) we have

\partial_{1,k}^{b+1}t_{1,k}^{a+1}f=\mathbf{c}(a,b)t_{1,k}^{a-b}f+Df
,
where
D\in\mathscr{I}_{1,k}
. The assumption on
f
and Lemma
3.17.1
imply that
Df\in\mathscr{I}_{1,k}
.
∎

Remark 3.17.10
.

It is easy to verify that

\mathbf{c}(a,b)=\mathbf{c}(a)\mathbf{c}(a-1)\cdots\mathbf{c}(a-b)
for
a\geq b\geq 0
. We extend the domain of
\mathbf{c}(a,b)
to pairs
(a,b)
satisfying
a,b\geq-1
by setting

\mathbf{c}(a,b)=0
for
-1\leq a<b
and
\mathbf{c}(a,b)=1
for
a\geq b=-1
.
Note that
\mathbf{c}(a,b)
is always a polynomial in
q^{2}
with integer coefficients. Furthermore, when
a\geq b
the degree of
\mathbf{c}(a,b)
as a polynomial in
q
is
(b+1)(2a-b)
.

For a
k
-tuple of non-negative integers
\mathsf{a}:=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})
, where
k\leq n
, we set
t^{\mathsf{a}}:=t_{1,k}^{a_{k}}\cdots t_{1,1}^{a_{1}}
and
\partial^{\mathsf{a}}:=\partial_{1,1}^{a_{1}}\cdots\partial_{1,k}^{a_{k}}
.

Lemma 3.17.11
.

Let
1\leq k^{\prime}<k_{r}<\ldots<k_{1}\leq n
. Also, let

a_{1},\ldots,a_{k^{\prime}}\geq 0
and
b_{1},\ldots,b_{r}\geq 0
.
Set
\mathsf{a}:=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k^{\prime}})
and
f:=t^{\mathsf{a}}:=t_{1,k^{\prime}}^{a_{k^{\prime}}}\cdots t_{1,1}^{a_{1}}
. Then

\partial_{1,k^{\prime}}^{b}t_{1,k_{1}}^{b_{1}}\cdots t_{1,k_{r}}^{b_{r}}f=f_{1}+D,

where
f_{1}\in\mathscr{P}
and
D\in\mathscr{I}_{1,k^{\prime}}
. If
a_{k^{\prime}}<b
then

f_{1}=0
. If
a_{k^{\prime}}\geq b
then

f_{1}=q^{b(b_{1}+\cdots+b_{r})}\mathbf{c}(a_{k^{\prime}}-1,b-1)t_{1,k_{1}}^{b_{1}}\cdots t_{1,k_{r}}^{b_{r}}t^{\mathsf{a}^{\prime}}\quad\text{ where }\quad\mathsf{a}^{\prime}:=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k^{\prime}-1},a_{k^{\prime}}-b).

Proof.

The assertion is trivial for
b=0
. If
b_{1}=\cdots=b_{r}=0
then the assertion follows from Lemma
3.17.9
(ii) and
Remark
3.17.10
.
Next assume without loss of generality that
b_{1}\geq 1
. First suppose that
b=1
. Using Lemma
3.17.1
we obtain

\displaystyle\partial_{1,k^{\prime}}t_{1,k_{1}}^{b_{1}}\cdots t_{1,k_{r}}^{b_{r}}f

\displaystyle=qt_{1,k_{1}}\partial_{1,k^{\prime}}t_{1,k_{1}}^{b_{1}-1}t_{1,k_{2}}^{b_{2}}\cdots t_{1,k_{r}}^{b_{r}}f

\displaystyle+(q-q^{-1})\sum_{1<i\leq m}t_{i,k_{1}}\partial_{i,k^{\prime}}t_{1,k_{1}}^{b_{1}-1}t_{1,k_{2}}^{b_{2}}\cdots t_{1,k_{r}}^{b_{r}}f=qt_{1,k_{1}}\partial_{1,k^{\prime}}t_{1,k_{1}}^{b_{1}-1}t_{1,k_{2}}^{b_{2}}\cdots t_{1,k_{r}}^{b_{r}}f+D_{1},

where
D_{1}\in\mathscr{I}_{2,k^{\prime}}
. By repeating the above calculation
and then using Lemma
3.17.7
we obtain

\displaystyle\partial_{1,k^{\prime}}t_{1,k_{1}}^{b_{1}}\cdots t_{1,k_{r}}^{b_{r}}f

\displaystyle=q^{b_{1}+\cdots+b_{r}}t_{1,k_{1}}^{b_{1}}\cdots t_{1,k_{r}}^{b_{r}}\partial_{1,k^{\prime}}t^{\mathsf{a}}+D_{2},

\displaystyle=q^{b_{1}+\cdots+b_{r}}\mathbf{c}(a_{k^{\prime}}-1)t_{1,k_{1}}^{b_{1}}\cdots t_{1,k_{r}}^{b_{r}}t^{\mathsf{a}-\mathsf{e}_{k^{\prime}}}+D_{2},

where
D_{2}\in\mathscr{I}_{1,k^{\prime}}
,

\mathsf{a}-\mathsf{e}_{k^{\prime}}:=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k^{\prime}-1},a_{k^{\prime}}-1)

and we define

\mathbf{c}(-1):=0
. This completes the proof for
b=1
. For
b>1
we just repeat the
above argument.
∎

Lemma 3.17.12
.

Let
\mathsf{a}:=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
and

\mathsf{b}:=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{n})
be
n
-tuples of non-negative integers. Then the following statements hold.

(i)

\partial^{\mathsf{b}}\cdot t^{\mathsf{a}}=0
if
b_{i}>a_{i}
for at least one
1\leq i\leq n
.

(ii)

Assume that
a_{i}\geq b_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq n
. Then

(76)

\partial^{\mathsf{b}}\cdot t^{\mathsf{a}}=\left(q^{\sum_{i=2}^{n}(a_{i}-b_{i})(b_{1}+\cdots+b_{i-1})}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{c}(a_{i}-1,b_{i}-1)\right)t^{\mathsf{a}-\mathsf{b}},

and

(77)

t^{\mathsf{b}}\partial^{\mathsf{b}}\cdot t^{\mathsf{a}}=\left(q^{\sum_{i=2}^{n}(2a_{i}-2b_{i})(b_{1}+\cdots+b_{i-1})}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{c}(a_{i}-1,b_{i}-1)\right)t^{\mathsf{a}}.

Proof.

(i) Follows from Lemma
3.17.9
and Lemma
3.17.11
.

(ii)
By Lemma
3.17.11
,

\partial^{\mathsf{b}}t^{\mathsf{a}}=\mathbf{c}(a_{n}-1,b_{n}-1)\partial^{\mathsf{b}^{\prime}}t_{1,n}^{a_{n}-b_{n}}t^{\mathsf{a}^{\prime}}+D_{1}

where
\mathsf{a}^{\prime}:=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n-1})
,
\mathsf{b}^{\prime}:=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{n-1})
and
D_{1}\in\mathscr{I}_{1,n}
.
Again by Lemma
3.17.11
,

\partial^{\mathsf{b}^{\prime}}t_{1,n}^{a_{n}-b_{n}}t^{\mathsf{a}^{\prime}}=q^{(a_{n}-b_{n})b_{n-1}}\mathbf{c}(a_{n-1}-1,b_{n-1}-1)\partial^{\mathsf{b}^{\prime\prime}}t_{1,n}^{a_{n}-b_{n}}t_{1,n-1}^{a_{n-1}-b_{n-1}}t^{\mathsf{a}^{\prime\prime}}+D_{2},

where
\mathsf{a}^{\prime\prime}:=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n-2})
,
\mathsf{b}^{\prime\prime}:=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{n-2})
and
D_{2}\in\mathscr{I}_{1,n-1}
. Continuing in this fashion we finally obtain (
76
).
For (
77
) we should compute the scalar relating

t^{\mathsf{b}}t^{\mathsf{a-b}}
and
t^{\mathsf{a}}
. This is straightforward using the relations

t_{1,i}t_{1,j}=qt_{1,j}t_{1,i}
for
i<j
.∎

4.
Differential operators associated to the
K_{\lambda}

Let
\mathring{U}_{L}
,
\mathring{U}_{R}
and
\mathring{U}_{LR}
be defined as in Subsection
3.14
.
The main goal of this section is to prove that certain elements of the Cartan subalgebras of
U_{L}
and
U_{R}
belong to
\mathring{U}_{L}
and
\mathring{U}_{R}
.
This is established in
Proposition
4.1.1
.

4.1.
Cartan elements in
\mathring{U}_{L}
,
\mathring{U}_{R}
and
\mathring{U}_{LR}

For
1\leq a\leq m
and
1\leq b\leq n
we set

(78)

\lambda_{L,a}:=-\sum_{i=a}^{m}2\varepsilon_{i}\quad\text{and}\quad\lambda_{R,b}:=-\sum_{i=b}^{n}2\varepsilon_{i}.

As in (
40
) these weights correspond to
K_{\lambda_{L,a}}\in U_{\mathfrak{h},L}
and
K_{\lambda_{R,b}}\in U_{\mathfrak{h},R}
, respectively.

Proposition 4.1.1
.

Let
K_{\lambda_{L,a}}\in U_{L}
and
K_{\lambda_{R,b}}\in U_{R}
be as in (
78
), where
1\leq a\leq m
and
1\leq b\leq n
. Then
K_{\lambda_{L,a}}\in\mathring{U}_{L}
and
K_{\lambda_{R,b}}\in\mathring{U}_{R}
.

Proof.

We only prove the assertion for
K_{\lambda_{L,a}}
(for
K_{\lambda_{R,b}}
the argument is similar).
First we verify the case
a=m
. By a straightforward computation based on Remark
3.6.2
we have

K_{\lambda_{L,m}}\cdot t_{a_{1},b_{1}}\cdots t_{a_{r},b_{r}}=q^{2\sum_{i=1}^{r}\llbracket m,a_{i}\rrbracket}t_{a_{1},b_{1}}\cdots t_{a_{r},b_{r}}.

By Lemma
3.17.5
the action of
\mathbf{D}^{\prime}_{1,0}+(q^{2}-1)\mathbf{D}^{\prime}_{1,1}
on
\mathscr{P}
is the same as the action of
K_{\lambda_{L,m}}
. Thus, by Proposition
3.11.4
we obtain

(79)

\phi_{U}(K_{\lambda_{L,m}}\otimes 1)=\mathbf{D}^{\prime}_{1,0}+(q^{2}-1)\mathbf{D}^{\prime}_{1,1}.

To complete the proof,
by Lemma
3.14.1

it suffices to verify that for any
a<m
, the
\mathrm{ad}(U_{L})
-invariant subalgebra of
U_{L}
that is generated by
K_{\lambda_{L,a+1}}
and
K_{\lambda_{L,m}}
also contains
K_{\lambda_{L,a}}
.
Denoting the standard generators of
U_{L}
by
E_{i}
,
F_{i}
,
K_{i}^{\pm 1}
,
we set

E^{\prime}_{\varepsilon_{i}-\varepsilon_{j}}:=[E_{i},[\ldots,E_{j-1}]_{q}]_{q}\quad\text{ and }F^{\prime}_{\varepsilon_{i}-\varepsilon_{j}}:=[F_{j-1},[\ldots,F_{i}]_{q^{-1}}]_{q^{-1}}\quad\text{ for $1\leq i<j\leq m$}.

Let
u:=E^{\prime}_{\varepsilon_{a}-\varepsilon_{m}}K_{-\varepsilon_{a}-\varepsilon_{m}}

and
v:=F^{\prime}_{\varepsilon_{a}-\varepsilon_{m}}K_{\lambda_{L,a+1}}
.
By a simple induction we can verify that

u=(1-q^{2})^{-1}\mathrm{ad}_{E_{a}}\cdots\mathrm{ad}_{E_{m-1}}(K_{\lambda_{L,m}})\ \text{ and }\ v=(1-q^{-2})^{-1}\mathrm{ad}_{F_{m-1}}\cdots\mathrm{ad}_{F_{a}}(K_{\lambda_{L,a+1}}),

so that
u,v\in\mathring{U}_{L}
by Lemma
3.14.1
.
For
x,y\in U_{L}
set
[x,y]:=xy-yx
.
Since
\mathring{U}_{L}
is an algebra,

(80)

\left[E^{\prime}_{\varepsilon_{a}-\varepsilon_{m}},F^{\prime}_{\varepsilon_{a}-\varepsilon_{m}}\right]K_{-\varepsilon_{a}-\varepsilon_{m}}K_{\lambda_{L,a+1}}=uv-q^{-2}vu\in\mathring{U}_{L}.

But the left hand side of (
80
) is equal to

(q-q^{-1})^{-1}(K_{\varepsilon_{a}-\varepsilon_{m}}-K_{\varepsilon_{a}-\varepsilon_{m}}^{-1})K_{-\varepsilon_{a}-\varepsilon_{m}}K_{\lambda_{L,a+1}}=(q-q^{-1})^{-1}(K_{-2\varepsilon_{m}}K_{\lambda_{L,a+1}}-K_{\lambda_{L,a}}).

It follows that

K_{\lambda_{L,a}}=-(q-q^{-1})(uv-q^{-2}vu)+K_{\lambda_{L,m}}K_{\lambda_{L,a+1}}\in\mathring{U}_{L}.\qed

Proposition 4.1.2
.

U_{L}
is generated as an algebra by
\mathring{U}_{L}
and
\{K_{\varepsilon_{i}}\}_{i=1}^{m}
. Similarly,

U_{R}
is generated as an algebra by
\mathring{U}_{R}
and
\{K_{\varepsilon_{i}}\}_{i=1}^{n}
.

Proof.

We give the proof for
U_{L}
(for
U_{R}
the proof is similar).
Let
\mathcal{A}
denote the subalgebra of
U_{L}
generated by
\mathring{U}_{L}
and
\{K_{\varepsilon_{i}}\}_{i=1}^{m}
.
Set
\rho:=\sum_{i=1}^{m}i\varepsilon_{i}
.
Then
K_{-2\rho}=K_{\lambda_{L,1}}\cdots K_{\lambda_{L,m}}
, hence by Proposition
4.1.1
we have
K_{-2\rho}\in\mathring{U}_{L}
.
Lemma
3.14.1
implies that

E_{i}K_{-2\rho}=(1-q^{2})^{-1}\mathrm{ad}_{E_{i}}(K_{-2\rho})\in\mathring{U}_{L}
, so that
E_{i}\in\mathcal{A}
.
By a similar argument we can prove that
F_{i}\in\mathcal{A}
as well. Also by our assumption
K_{\varepsilon_{i}}\in\mathcal{A}
for
1\leq i\leq m
, hence
\mathcal{A}=U_{L}
.
∎

5.
Explicit formulas for
\phi_{U}(K_{\lambda_{L,a}}\otimes 1)
and
\phi_{U}(1\otimes K_{\lambda_{R,b}})

In this section we
compute explicit formulas for
\phi_{U}(K_{\lambda_{L,a}}\otimes 1)
and
\phi_{U}(1\otimes K_{\lambda_{R,b}})
, where
K_{\lambda_{L,a}}
and
K_{\lambda_{R,b}}
are defined
in
(
78
).
These explicit formulas are used in the proof of Theorem
C
.

5.1.
Eigenvalues of
\mathbf{D}_{n,r}
and
q
-factorial Schur polynomials

For any integer partition
\nu
such that
\ell(\nu)\leq n
, let
s_{\nu}
denote the
q
-factorial Schur polynomial in
n
variables associated to
\nu
, defined by

s_{\nu}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n};q):=\frac{\det\left(\prod_{k=0}^{\nu_{j}+n-j-1}(x_{i}-q^{k})\right)_{1\leq i,j\leq n}}{\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}(x_{i}-x_{j})}.

Recall that
\mathbf{D}(r,a,b)\in\mathscr{PD}_{a\times b}
is the
q
-differential operator defined in (
8
).
We will need the following statement, which is a variation of
[
BKV06
, Thm 1]
.

Proposition 5.1.1
.

Let
\lambda
be an integer partition satisfying

\ell(\lambda)\leq n
.
Then the restriction of

\mathbf{D}(r,n,n)\in\mathscr{PD}_{n\times n}

to the irreducible
U_{LR}
-submodule
V_{\lambda}^{*}\otimes V_{\lambda}^{*}
of
\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}

is a scalar multiple of identity,
the scalar being

(81)

\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\lambda,r,n}(q):=\frac{(-1)^{r}q^{r-r^{2}-2r(n-r)}}{(1-q^{2})^{r}}s_{(1^{r})}(q^{2(\lambda_{1}+n-1)},\ldots,q^{2(\lambda_{n-1}+1)},q^{2\lambda_{n}};q^{2}).

Proof.

We show that the assertion follows from an analogous result in the setting of operators in
\mathrm{Pol}(\mathrm{Mat}_{n})_{q}
acting on
\mathbb{C}[\mathrm{Mat}_{n}]_{q}
(see Subsection
3.16
)
that is proved in
[
BKV06
, Thm 1]
. In the following proof we use the notation introduced in Subsection
3.16
. In particular we set

A:=\mathbb{Z}[q,q^{-1}]
.

Step 1.

For each irreducible component
V_{\lambda}^{*}\otimes V_{\lambda}^{*}
of
\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}
(see Proposition
3.6.1
) we choose a lowest weight vector

v_{\lambda}\in\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}^{A}

for the
U_{LR}
-action and set
W_{\lambda}^{A}:=U_{LR}^{A}\cdot v_{\lambda}
. From the explicit formulas of the action of
U_{LR}
(see Remark
3.6.2
) it follows that
W_{\lambda}^{A}\subseteq\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}^{A}
.
Furthermore,
the canonical map
W_{\lambda}^{A}\otimes_{A}\Bbbk\to V_{\lambda}^{*}\otimes V_{\lambda}^{*}
is an isomorphism.

Step 2.

By Corollary
3.16.1
we obtain a map

(82)

\mathscr{PD}_{n\times n}^{A}\xrightarrow{\ D\mapsto D\otimes 1\ }\mathscr{PD}_{n\times n}^{A}\otimes_{A}\mathbb{C}\xrightarrow{\ \ \cong\ \ }\mathrm{Pol}(\mathrm{Mat}_{n})_{q_{\circ}},

that restricts to a map

(83)

\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}^{A}\xrightarrow{\ D\mapsto D\otimes 1\ }\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}^{A}\otimes_{A}\mathbb{C}\xrightarrow{\ \ \cong\ \ }\mathbb{C}[\mathrm{Mat}_{n}]_{q_{\circ}}.

We also have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{U_{LR}^{A}\otimes_{A}\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}^{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(-)\otimes_{A}\mathbb{C}}
\textstyle{\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}^{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(-)\otimes_{A}\mathbb{C}}
\textstyle{U_{q_{\circ}}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})\otimes U_{q_{\circ}}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})\otimes\mathbb{C}[\mathrm{Mat}_{n}]_{q_{\circ}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}[\mathrm{Mat}_{n}]_{q_{\circ}}}

where the top horizontal map is the restriction of the
U_{LR}
-module structure on

\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}

and the bottom horizontal map is (
73
) in the special case
m=n
.
Let us denote both of the maps (
82
)
and (
83
)
by
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{q_{\circ}}
.
Then

\boldsymbol{\beta}_{q_{\circ}}(t_{i,j})=(1-q_{\circ}^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}z^{i}_{j}
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{q_{\circ}}(\partial_{i,j})=(1-q_{\circ}^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}(z^{i}_{j})^{*}
.
From the definition of
\mathbf{D}(r,n,n)
it is clear that
\mathbf{D}(r,n,n)\in\mathscr{PD}_{n\times n}^{A}
.
In addition

\boldsymbol{\beta}_{q_{\circ}}\left((1-q^{2})^{r}\mathbf{D}(r,n,n)\right)=y_{r}
, where
y_{r}
is the operator defined in
[
BKV06
, Eq. (11)]
.

Step 3.

From Proposition
3.16.2
it follows that
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{q_{\circ}}(v_{\lambda})
is the joint highest weight vector for the irreducible submodule

\mathbb{C}[\mathrm{Mat}_{n}]_{q_{\circ},\lambda}
of

\mathbb{C}[\mathrm{Mat}_{n}]_{q_{\circ}}
that is defined in
[
BKV06
, Sec. 2]
. Thus Step 1 and the commutative diagram of Step 2 imply that
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{q_{\circ}}(W_{\lambda}^{A})=\boldsymbol{\beta}_{q_{\circ}}(U_{LR}^{A}\cdot v_{\lambda})\subseteq\mathbb{C}[\mathrm{Mat}_{n}]_{q_{\circ},\lambda}
.

Step 4.
Fix
\lambda
such that
\ell(\lambda)\leq n
, choose any vector
w\in W_{\lambda}^{A}
, and set

w^{\prime}:=(1-q^{2})^{r}\left(\mathbf{D}(r,n,n)-\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\lambda,r,n}\right)\cdot w.

From Steps 1–2 above it follows that

\boldsymbol{\beta}_{q_{\circ}}(w^{\prime})=y_{r}\cdot\boldsymbol{\beta}_{q_{\circ}}(w)-(1-q_{\circ}^{2})\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\lambda,r,n}(q_{\circ})\boldsymbol{\beta}_{q_{\circ}}(w)
. Since
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{q_{\circ}}(w)\in\mathbb{C}[\mathrm{Mat}]_{q_{\circ},\lambda}
, by
[
BKV06
, Thm 1]
we obtain
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{q_{\circ}}(w^{\prime})=0
. Since
evaluations at
q_{\circ}
for infinitely many
q_{\circ}
separate the points of
\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}^{A}
, it follows that
w^{\prime}=0
.

Step 5.
By Step 4 we have
\mathbf{D}(r,n,n)\cdot w=\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\lambda,r,n}(q)w
for
w\in W_{\lambda}^{A}
. Since
W_{\lambda}^{A}
spans
V_{\lambda}^{*}\otimes V_{\lambda}^{*}
over
\Bbbk
, the same assertion holds for all
w\in V_{\lambda}^{*}\otimes V_{\lambda}^{*}
.
∎

Remark 5.1.2
.

In our forthcoming work
[
LSS22b
]
, we prove a broad extension of
Proposition
5.1.1

for Capelli operators on quantum symmetric spaces.

The polynomials
s_{\nu}
are specializations of the
interpolation Macdonald polynomials

R_{\lambda}

defined in
[
Sah96
]
(see also
[
Kn97
]
and
[
Ok97
]
).
In the rest of this section we follow the notation of
[
Sah11
, Sec. 0.3]
. Let
