R_{\lambda}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n};q,t)
denote the unique symmetric polynomial with coefficients in
\mathbb{Q}(q,t)
that satisfies the following conditions:

(i)

\deg R_{\lambda}=|\lambda|
.

(ii)

R_{\lambda}(q^{\mu_{1}},\ldots,q^{\mu_{i}}t^{1-i},\ldots,q^{\mu_{n}}t^{1-n};q,t)=0
for all partitions

\mu\neq\lambda
that satisfy
|\mu|\leq|\lambda|
.

(iii)

R_{\lambda}
can be expressed as

R_{\lambda}=m_{\lambda}+\sum_{\mu\neq\lambda}c_{\mu,\lambda}m_{\mu}
, where the
m_{\mu}
denote
the monomial symmetric polynomials.

It is known

[
Kn97
, Prop. 2.8]

that

s_{\lambda}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n};q)=q^{(n-1)|\lambda|}R_{\lambda}(q^{1-n}x_{1},\ldots,q^{1-n}x_{n};q,q).

The proof of Lemma
5.1.4
below uses Okounkov’s binomial theorem for interpolation Macdonald polynomials
[
Ok97
]
. We remark that in
[
Ok97
]

the interpolation Macdonald polynomials are defined slightly differently, and are denoted by the
P_{\lambda}^{*}
, but one can show that

(84)

P_{\lambda}^{*}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n};q,t)=R_{\lambda}(x_{1},x_{2}t^{-1},x_{n}t^{-n+1};q,t).

For two integer partitions
\lambda,\mu
such that
\ell(\lambda),\ell(\mu)\leq n
, let

\left[\lambda\atop\mu\right]_{q,t}

denote the
(q,t)
-binomial coefficient defined in
[
Ok97
]
. Thus

(85)

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\,\,\lambda\,\,\\
\mu\end{bmatrix}_{q,t}:=\frac{P^{*}_{\mu}(q^{\lambda_{1}},\ldots,q^{\lambda_{n}};q,t)}{P^{*}_{\lambda}(q^{\lambda_{1}},\ldots,q^{\lambda_{n}};q,t)}.

Lemma 5.1.3
.

For
0\leq r\leq n
we have

\left[1^{n}\atop 1^{r}\right]_{q,q}=q^{-r(n-r)}\frac{(q^{n}-1)\cdots(q^{n-r+1}-1)}{(q^{r}-1)\cdots(q-1)}
.

Proof.

The proof is a straightforward but somewhat tedious calculation based on a general combinatorial formula
in
[
Sah11
, Thm 0.8]

for the
(q,t)
-binomial coefficients.
We give a brief outline of this calculation. In the notation
of
[
Sah11
]
, the value of (
85
) can be expressed as a sum of the form
\sum_{T}wt(T)
, where
T
is a standard tableau of shape
\lambda\backslash\mu
. For
\lambda:=(1^{n})
and
\mu:=(1^{r})
, there is only one such tableau.
By direct calculation one obtains

\lambda^{i}=(1^{n-i})\ ,\ a_{\lambda^{i},\lambda^{i+1}}=\frac{t^{-n+i+1}(1-t^{n-i})}{1-t}\ ,\ \frac{|\overline{\lambda^{i}}|-|\overline{\lambda^{i+1}}|}{|\overline{\lambda}|-|\overline{\lambda^{i+1}}|}=\frac{t^{i}(1-t)}{1-t^{i+1}}.

From these, the assertion of the lemma follows immediately.
∎

Lemma 5.1.4
.

Set
\nu_{r}:=(1^{r})
for
0\leq r\leq n
.
Then

\sum_{r=0}^{n}q^{-{r\choose 2}-r(n-r)}s_{\nu_{r}}(q^{n-1}x_{1},\ldots,q^{n-i}x_{i},\ldots,x_{n};q)=x_{1}\cdots x_{n}.

Proof.

This is stated in
[
BKV06
, Prop. 10]
without a proof. We show that it is a special case of Okounkov’s binomial
theorem
[
Ok97
, Eq. (1.11)]
.
More specifically, from (
84
) it follows that

P_{\nu_{r}}^{*}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n};q,q)=q^{(1-n)r}s_{\nu_{r}}(q^{n-1}x_{1},\ldots,x_{n};q).

We now consider the
identity
[
Ok97
, Eq. (1.11)]

for
t:=q
and
\lambda:=(1^{n})
. Then the left hand side of
[
Ok97
, Eq. (1.11)]
is equal to

x_{1}\cdots x_{n}
, whereas its right hand side is equal to

\sum_{r=0}^{n}\begin{bmatrix}1^{n}\\
1^{r}\end{bmatrix}_{q,q}q^{-{r\choose 2}}\frac{(q^{r}-1)\cdots(q-1)}{(q^{n}-1)\cdots(q^{n-r+1}-1)}s_{\nu_{r}}(q^{n-1}x_{1},\ldots,x_{n};q).

To complete the proof, we use Lemma
5.1.3
.
∎

5.2.
The explicit formulas

Let
\mathbf{D}_{n,r}
and
\mathbf{D}_{m,r}^{\prime}
be as in Section
1
.
For

0\leq r\leq m
we set

(86)

\mathbf{D}_{r}:=\mathbf{D}_{n,r}=\mathbf{D}^{\prime}_{m,r}.

Proposition 5.2.1
.

\phi_{U}(K_{\lambda_{L,1}}\otimes 1)=\phi_{U}(1\otimes K_{\lambda_{R,1}})=\sum_{r=0}^{m}(q^{2}-1)^{r}\mathbf{D}_{r}
.

Proof.

Both
K_{\lambda_{L,1}}\otimes 1
and
1\otimes K_{\lambda_{R,1}}
act on

\mathscr{P}^{(d)}
by the scalar
q^{2d}
(this is easy to verify using Remark
3.6.2
).
Since
\mathscr{P}
is a faithful
\mathscr{PD}
-module,
by Proposition
3.6.1
it suffices to verify that for every partition
\lambda
that satisfies
\ell(\lambda)\leq\min\{m,n\}
and
|\lambda|=d
,
the restriction of
\sum_{r=0}^{m}(q^{2}-1)^{r}\mathbf{D}_{r}
to
the irreducible
U_{LR}
-submodule
V_{\lambda}^{*}\otimes V_{\lambda}^{*}
of
\mathscr{P}
is multiplication by the scalar
q^{2d}
.

Step 1.
First we prove the assertion in the case
m=n
. In this case
\mathbf{D}_{r}=\mathbf{D}(r,n,n)
, hence by Proposition
5.1.1
it is enough to verify that

(87)

\sum_{r=0}^{n}q^{r-r^{2}-2r(n-r)}s_{\nu_{r}}(q^{2(\lambda_{1}+n-1)},\ldots,q^{2(\lambda_{n-1}+1)},q^{2\lambda_{n}};q^{2})=q^{2(\lambda_{1}+\cdots+\lambda_{n})}.

Equality (
87
) follows from
Lemma
5.1.4
after substituting
q
by
q^{\frac{1}{2}}
.

Step 2.
Henceforth assume
m<n
(by Lemma
3.15.1

the proof when
m>n
is similar).
Let

\boldsymbol{e}=\boldsymbol{e}_{m\times n}^{n\times n}:\mathscr{PD}\to\mathscr{PD}_{n\times n}

be the embedding of algebras defined in (
4
), so that

\boldsymbol{e}(t_{i,j})=t_{i+n-m,j}
and
\boldsymbol{e}(\partial_{i,j})=\partial_{i+n-m,j}
.
Set
\tilde{\mathbf{D}}_{r}:=\mathbf{D}(r,n,n)
.
By Corollary
3.17.3
,
\overline{M}^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{j}}\cdot\mathsf{(}\boldsymbol{e}(\mathscr{P}))=0
unless
\mathbf{i}=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{r})
satifies
u_{1}\geq n-m+1
.
Thus, for every
f\in\mathscr{P}
we have
\tilde{\mathbf{D}}_{r}\cdot\boldsymbol{e}(f)=\boldsymbol{e}(\mathbf{D}_{r})\cdot\boldsymbol{e}(f)
when
0\leq r\leq m
, and
\tilde{\mathbf{D}}_{r}\cdot\boldsymbol{e}(f)=0
when
m<r\leq n
.

Step 3.

Recall that
\mathscr{I}
denotes the left ideal of
\mathscr{PD}
that is generated by
\mathscr{D}^{(1)}
. Let
\mathscr{I}^{\prime}
denote the left ideal of
\mathscr{PD}_{n\times n}
that is generated by
\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}^{(1)}
. Let
\boldsymbol{e}:\mathscr{PD}\to\mathscr{PD}_{n\times n}
be as in Step 2. For
D\in\mathscr{PD}
and
f\in\mathscr{P}
we have

(D\cdot f-Df)\in\mathscr{I}
, hence

\boldsymbol{e}(D\cdot f)-\boldsymbol{e}(D)\boldsymbol{e}(f)=\boldsymbol{e}(D\cdot f-Df)\in\mathscr{I}^{\prime}.

But also

\boldsymbol{e}(D)\cdot\boldsymbol{e}(f)-\boldsymbol{e}(D)\boldsymbol{e}(f)\in\mathscr{I}^{\prime}
. From the last two relations we obtain
\boldsymbol{e}(D)\cdot\boldsymbol{e}(f)-\boldsymbol{e}(D\cdot f)\in\mathscr{I}^{\prime}
. But in addition
\boldsymbol{e}(D)\cdot\boldsymbol{e}(f)-\boldsymbol{e}(D\cdot f)\in\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}
, hence

\boldsymbol{e}(D)\cdot\boldsymbol{e}(f)=\boldsymbol{e}(D\cdot f)
.

Step 4.
Let
f\in\mathscr{P}^{(d)}
. From Step 3 and Step 2 it follows that

(88)

\boldsymbol{e}\left(\sum_{r=0}^{m}(q^{2}-1)^{r}\mathbf{D}_{r}\cdot f\right)=\boldsymbol{e}\left(\sum_{r=0}^{m}(q^{2}-1)^{r}\mathbf{D}_{r}\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{e}(f)=\sum_{r=0}^{n}(q^{2}-1)^{r}\tilde{\mathbf{D}}_{r}\cdot\boldsymbol{e}(f).

From Step 1 it follows that

\sum_{r=0}^{n}(q^{2}-1)^{r}\tilde{\mathbf{D}}_{r}\cdot\boldsymbol{e}(f)=q^{2d}\boldsymbol{e}(f)
. Since
\boldsymbol{e}
is an injection, from (
88
) we obtain

\sum_{r=0}^{m}(q^{2}-1)^{r}\mathbf{D}_{r}\cdot f=q^{2d}f
.
∎

Proposition 5.2.2
.

For
1\leq a\leq m
and
1\leq b\leq n
we have

(89)

\phi_{U}(K_{\lambda_{L,a}}\otimes 1)=\sum_{r=0}^{m-a+1}(q^{2}-1)^{r}\mathbf{D}^{\prime}_{m-a+1,r}\quad\text{and}\quad\phi_{U}(1\otimes K_{\lambda_{R,b}})=\sum_{r=0}^{n-b+1}(q^{2}-1)^{r}\mathbf{D}_{n-b+1,r}.

Proof.

We give the proof for
K_{\lambda_{L,a}}\otimes 1
only (the proof for
1\otimes K_{\lambda_{R,b}}
is similar). Every element of
\mathscr{P}
is expressible as a linear combination of monomials of the form

t_{i_{1},j_{1}}\cdots t_{i_{k},j_{k}}
where
i_{1}\geq\cdots\geq i_{k}
. Choose
k^{\prime}\leq k
such that
i_{k^{\prime}}\geq a

and
i_{k^{\prime}+1}<a
. Then

(K_{\lambda_{L,a}}\otimes 1)\cdot t_{i_{1},j_{1}}\cdots t_{i_{k},j_{k}}=q^{2k^{\prime}}t_{i_{1},j_{1}}\cdots t_{i_{k},j_{k}}.

Set
D:=\sum_{r=0}^{m-a+1}(q^{2}-1)\mathbf{D}_{m-a+1,r}^{\prime}
. From Lemma
3.17.4
it follows that

D\cdot\left(t_{i_{1},j_{1}}\cdots t_{i_{k},j_{k}}\right)=(D\cdot t_{i_{1},j_{1}}\cdots t_{i_{k^{\prime}},j_{k^{\prime}}})t_{i_{k^{\prime}+1},j_{k^{\prime}+1}}\cdots t_{i_{k},j_{k}}.

Hence it suffices to prove that

(90)

D\cdot t_{i_{1},j_{1}}\cdots t_{i_{k^{\prime}},j_{k^{\prime}}}=q^{2k^{\prime}}t_{i_{1},j_{1}}\cdots t_{i_{k^{\prime}},j_{k^{\prime}}}.

Set
\tilde{m}:=m-a+1
. Let
\boldsymbol{e}=\boldsymbol{e}_{\tilde{m}\times n}^{m\times n}:\mathscr{PD}_{\tilde{m}\times n}\to\mathscr{PD}
be the embedding of algebras defined in (
4
), so that

\boldsymbol{e}(t_{i,j})=t_{i+a-1,j}
and
\boldsymbol{e}(\partial_{i,j})=\partial_{i+a-1,j}
.
Set
\tilde{\mathbf{D}}_{r}:=\mathbf{D}(r,\tilde{m},n)\in\mathscr{PD}_{\tilde{m}\times n}
.
Similar to the proof of Proposition

5.2.1

we have
\boldsymbol{e}(\tilde{\mathbf{D}}_{r})=\mathbf{D}^{\prime}_{m-a+1,r}
and
\boldsymbol{e}(\tilde{\mathbf{D}}_{r}\cdot f)=\boldsymbol{e}(\tilde{\mathbf{D}}_{r})\cdot\boldsymbol{e}(f)
for
f\in\mathscr{P}_{\tilde{m}\times n}
.
Proposition
5.2.1
for
\mathscr{PD}_{\tilde{m}\times n}
yields

\sum_{r=0}^{m-a+1}\tilde{\mathbf{D}}_{r}\cdot f=q^{2k^{\prime}}f
for
f:=t_{i_{1}-a+1,j_{1}}\cdots t_{i_{k^{\prime}}-a+1,j_{k^{\prime}}}\in\mathscr{P}_{\tilde{m}\times n}
.
By applying
\boldsymbol{e}
to both sides we obtain (
90
).
∎

6.
Some properties of polarization operators

In this section we investigate invariance and generation properties of the
\mathsf{L}_{i,j}
, the
\mathsf{R}_{i,j}
, and their variants.

6.1.
Invariants and the operators
\mathsf{L}_{i,j}
,
\mathsf{R}_{i,j}

Recall from (
3
)
that
\mathcal{Y}^{\mathcal{Z}}
denotes the centralizer of
\mathcal{Z}
in
\mathcal{Y}
.

Lemma 6.1.1
.

\mathrm{End}_{\Bbbk}(\mathscr{P})^{{\mathscr{L}_{\bullet}}}=\mathrm{End}_{\Bbbk}(\mathscr{P})^{\mathscr{L}}

and

\mathrm{End}_{\Bbbk}(\mathscr{P})^{{\mathscr{R}_{\bullet}}}=\mathrm{End}_{\Bbbk}(\mathscr{P})^{\mathscr{R}}
.

Proof.

We only give the proofs of the two assertions for
\mathscr{L}
.
The inclusion
\mathrm{End}_{\Bbbk}(\mathscr{P})^{{\mathscr{L}_{\bullet}}}\supseteq\mathrm{End}_{\Bbbk}(\mathscr{P})^{\mathscr{L}}
is trivial because
{\mathscr{L}_{\bullet}}\subseteq\mathscr{L}
. To prove

\mathrm{End}_{\Bbbk}(\mathscr{P})^{{\mathscr{L}_{\bullet}}}\subseteq\mathrm{End}_{\Bbbk}(\mathscr{P})^{\mathscr{L}}
, choose any
T\in\mathrm{End}_{\Bbbk}(\mathscr{P})^{{\mathscr{L}_{\bullet}}}
.
From Proposition
5.2.2
it follows that
T
commutes with

\phi_{U}(K_{2\varepsilon_{a}}\otimes 1)=\phi_{U}\left((K_{\lambda_{L,a}}^{-1}\otimes 1)(K_{\lambda_{L,a+1}}\otimes 1)\right)\quad\text{for }1\leq a\leq m,

where we assume
K_{\lambda_{L,m+1}}:=1
. From
Proposition
3.6.1

(and also from Remark
3.6.2
) it follows that

\phi_{U}(K_{\varepsilon_{i}}\otimes 1)
is a diagonalizable operator whose eigenvalues are powers of
q
.
In particular, the eigenspaces of
\phi_{U}(K_{2\varepsilon_{i}}\otimes 1)
and
\phi_{U}(K_{\varepsilon_{i}}\otimes 1)
are the same. Thus
T
also commutes with
\phi_{U}(K_{\varepsilon_{i}}\otimes 1)
.
Finally, Proposition
4.1.2
implies that
T\in\mathrm{End}_{\Bbbk}(\mathscr{P})^{\mathscr{L}}
.
∎

As in Subsection
2.3
set

\mathscr{PD}_{(\epsilon_{L})}:=\left\{D\in\mathscr{PD}\,:\,x\cdot D:=\epsilon_{L}(x)D\text{ for }x\in U_{L}\right\},

where
\epsilon_{L}
denotes the counit of
U_{L}
. We define
\mathscr{PD}_{(\epsilon_{R})}
,
\left(\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}\right)_{(\epsilon_{R})}
,

\left(\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{k,l,n}\right)_{(\epsilon_{R})}
and
\left(\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr},(r,s)}\right)_{(\epsilon_{R})}

similarly (where
\epsilon_{R}
denotes the counit of
U_{R}
).

Lemma 6.1.2
.

\mathscr{PD}^{\mathscr{L}_{\bullet}}=\mathscr{PD}^{\mathscr{L}}=\mathscr{PD}_{(\epsilon_{L})}

and

\mathscr{PD}^{\mathscr{R}_{\bullet}}=\mathscr{PD}^{\mathscr{R}}=\mathscr{PD}_{(\epsilon_{R})}
.

Proof.

From Lemma
6.1.1
it follows that

\mathscr{PD}^{\mathscr{L}_{\bullet}}=\mathscr{PD}^{\mathscr{L}}
and

\mathscr{PD}^{\mathscr{R}_{\bullet}}=\mathscr{PD}^{\mathscr{R}}
. By Proposition
3.12.1
, the action of
U_{LR}
on
\mathscr{PD}
is the restriction of the action of
U_{LR}
on
\mathrm{End}_{\Bbbk}(\mathscr{P})
that is defined in Lemma
2.3.1
. Thus
Lemma
2.3.1
implies that

\mathscr{PD}^{\mathscr{L}}=\mathscr{PD}_{(\epsilon_{L})}
and
\mathscr{PD}^{\mathscr{R}}=\mathscr{PD}_{(\epsilon_{R})}
.
∎

Lemma 6.1.3
.

\left(\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}\right)_{(\epsilon_{R})}
is a subalgebra of
\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}
and
\left(\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{k,l,n}\right)_{(\epsilon_{R})}

is a subalgebra of
\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}}
.

Proof.

Follows immediately from the fact that both

\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}
and
\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}}
are
U_{R}
-module algebras.
∎

Recall that by definition,
\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}}
is a subalgebra of
\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr}}:=\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{m\times n}
where
m:=\max\{k,l\}
.
For

1\leq i\leq k
and
1\leq j\leq l
define

\tilde{\mathsf{L}}_{i,j}^{\mathrm{gr}}\in\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}}
by

(91)

\tilde{\mathsf{L}}_{i,j}^{\mathrm{gr}}:=\sum_{r=1}^{n}\tilde{t}_{i,r}\tilde{\partial}_{j,r}=\sum_{r=1}^{n}t_{m-i+1,r}\partial_{m-j+1,r}.

Under the isomorphism of Corollary
3.13.4
the
\tilde{\mathsf{L}}_{i,j}^{\mathrm{gr}}
correspond to the

\mathrm{gr}(\tilde{\mathsf{L}}_{i,j})\in\mathrm{gr}(\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n})
.

Lemma 6.1.4
.

\tilde{\mathsf{L}}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{i,j}\in\left(\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{k,l,n}\right)_{(\epsilon_{R})}

and

\tilde{\mathsf{L}}_{i,j}\in\left(\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}\right)_{(\epsilon_{R})}

for
1\leq i\leq k
and
1\leq j\leq l
.

Proof.

Recall that
\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}}
is a
U_{R}
-module algebra because it is a
U_{R}
-stable subalgebra of
\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr}}
. For
\tilde{\mathsf{L}}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{i,j}
the assertion follows from the formulas of Remark
3.6.2
. For example

\displaystyle E_{s}\cdot\tilde{\mathsf{L}}_{i,j}^{\mathrm{gr}}=E_{s}\cdot\sum_{r=1}^{n}\tilde{t}_{i,r}\tilde{\partial}_{j,r}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=1}^{n}(E_{s}\cdot\tilde{t}_{i,r})(K_{s}\cdot\tilde{\partial}_{j,r})+\sum_{r=1}^{n}\tilde{t}_{i,r}(E_{s}\cdot\tilde{\partial}_{j,r})

\displaystyle=(-q^{-1}\tilde{t}_{i,n-s})(q\tilde{\partial}_{j,n+1-s})+(\tilde{t}_{i,n-s})(\tilde{\partial}_{j,n+1-s})=0.

Since the map
\mathsf{P}_{k,l,n}:\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}}\to\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}
is a
U_{R}
-module homomorphism, we have

\tilde{\mathsf{L}}_{i,j}\in\left(\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}\right)_{(\epsilon_{R})}
.
∎

Lemma 6.1.5
.

The
U_{L}
-submodule of
\mathscr{PD}
that is generated by
\mathsf{L}_{m,m}
contains
\mathsf{L}_{i,j}
for
1\leq i,j\leq m
. Similarly, the
U_{R}
-submodule of
\mathscr{PD}
that is generated by
\mathsf{R}_{n,n}
contains
\mathsf{R}_{i,j}
for
1\leq i,j\leq n
.

Proof.

We only give the proof for the assertion about the
U_{R}
-submodule generated by
\mathsf{R}_{n,n}
(the other assertion is proved similarly). Denote this submodule by
\mathscr{M}
. First we prove the following relations for the
U_{R}
-action:

(92)

E_{j}\cdot\mathsf{R}_{i,j+1}=\mathsf{R}_{i,j}\text{ and }F_{i}\cdot\mathsf{R}_{i+1,j}=-q\mathsf{R}_{i,j}\text{ for }j\neq i\quad,\quad F_{i}\cdot\mathsf{R}_{i+1,i}=-q\mathsf{R}_{i,i}+q^{-1}\mathsf{R}_{i+1,i+1}.

The proofs of these relations are similar and based on the explicit formulas given in
Remark
3.6.2
.
For example using
\Delta(F_{i})=F_{i}\otimes 1+K_{i}^{-1}\otimes F_{i}
we have

\displaystyle F_{i}\cdot\mathsf{R}_{i+1,i}=F_{i}\cdot\sum_{r=1}^{n}t_{r,i+1}\partial_{r,i}

\displaystyle=\sum\left((F_{i}\cdot t_{r,i+1})(\partial_{r,i})+(K_{i}^{-1}\cdot t_{r,i+1})(F_{i}\cdot\partial_{r,i})\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=1}^{n}\left(-qt_{r,i}\partial_{r,i}+q^{-1}t_{r,i+1}\partial_{r,i+1}\right)=-q\mathsf{R}_{i,i}+q^{-1}\mathsf{R}_{i+1,+1}.

Since
\mathsf{R}_{n,n}\in\mathscr{M}
,
from the second relation
in (
92
)
for
j=n
we obtain

\mathsf{R}_{i,n}\in\mathscr{M}
for
i\leq n
.
Then using the first relation in (
92
) successively for
j=n-1,\ldots,i+1
we obtain
\mathsf{R}_{i,j}\in\mathscr{M}
for
i<j
.
The above argument can be repeated with the roles of
E_{i}
and
F_{i}
switched. This yields
\mathsf{R}_{i,j}\in\mathscr{M}
for
i>j
. Finally, from
\mathsf{R}_{n,n}
and the third relation in (
92
) we obtain
\mathsf{R}_{i,i}\in\mathscr{M}
for
1\leq i\leq n
.
∎

Corollary 6.1.6
.

\mathsf{L}_{i,j}\in{\mathscr{L}_{\bullet}}
for all
1\leq i,j\leq m
and
\mathsf{R}_{i,j}\in{\mathscr{R}_{\bullet}}
for
1\leq i,j\leq n
.

Proof.

From
(
71
) it follows that
{\mathscr{L}_{\bullet}}
is
U_{L}
-stable.
By (
79
)
we have
\phi_{U}(K_{\lambda_{L,m}}\otimes 1)=1+(q^{2}-1)\mathsf{L}_{m,m}
, hence
\mathsf{L}_{m,m}\in{\mathscr{L}_{\bullet}}
. Hence by Lemma
6.1.5
we have
\mathsf{L}_{i,j}\in{\mathscr{L}_{\bullet}}
. The proof of
\mathsf{R}_{i,j}\in{\mathscr{R}_{\bullet}}
is similar.
∎

6.2.
The
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{k})\otimes U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{l})
-module decomposition of
\left(\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}}\right)_{(\epsilon_{R})}

Given two irreducible
U_{R}
-modules
V_{\lambda}
and
V_{\mu}
, the canonical isomorphism

\left(V_{\lambda}^{*}\otimes V_{\mu}\right)_{(\epsilon_{R})}\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{U_{R}}(V_{\lambda},V_{\mu})
implies that

(93)

\dim\left(V_{\lambda}^{*}\otimes V_{\mu}\right)_{(\epsilon_{R})}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }\lambda=\mu,\\
0&\text{if }\lambda\neq\mu.\end{cases}

Thus, from (
69
) it follows that
as
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{k})\otimes U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{l})
-modules we have

(94)

\left(\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{gr},(r,s)}_{k,l,n}\right)_{(\epsilon_{R})}=0\ \text{ for }\ r\neq s\ \text{ and }\ \left(\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{gr},(r,r)}_{k,l,n}\right)_{(\epsilon_{R})}\cong\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{d,r}}V_{\lambda}^{*}\otimes V_{\lambda},\ \text{ where }\ d:=\min\{k,l,n\}.

7.
The map
{{}\Gamma}_{k,l,n}

Let
k,l,n\geq 1
be integers such that
k,l\leq n
. In this section we define a map

{{}\Gamma}_{k,l,n}:\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}\to\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{k,l,n}

that is a bijection onto the subalgebra
\big(\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}}\big)_{(\epsilon_{R})}
of
\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}}
.
A similar map was also used in
[
LZZ11
]
.
The ideas of the proofs of Lemma
7.1.1
and Lemma
7.1.5
are taken from
[
LZZ11
]
. Recall from Definition
3.7.2
that
\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{n\times n}\cong\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}\otimes\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}
as a vector space.

7.1.
Construction of
{{}\Gamma}_{k,l,n}

For
n\geq 1
set

(95)

{}\Gamma_{n}:\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}\to\mathscr{PD}_{n\times n}^{\mathrm{gr}}\ ,\ {}\Gamma_{n}:=(1\otimes\iota)\circ\Delta_{\mathscr{P}},

where

\iota:\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}\to\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}
is the anti-isomorphism of bialgebras defined in (
53
) and

\Delta_{\mathscr{P}}
is the coproduct of
\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}
. In particular in Sweedler’s notation we have

{{}\Gamma}_{n}(u)=\sum u_{1}\otimes\iota(u_{2})
for
u\in\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}
.

Lemma 7.1.1
.

Let
\epsilon_{\mathscr{D}}
be the counit of

\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}
.
Then
the map
1\otimes\epsilon_{\mathscr{D}}:\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{n\times n}\to\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}
is a left inverse to
{{}\Gamma}_{n}
. In particular,
{{}\Gamma}_{n}
is an injection.

Proof.

This is equivalent to the relation
\sum\epsilon_{\mathscr{D}}(\iota(u_{2}))u_{1}=u
for
u\in\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}
. Since
\iota
is a linear bijection, it suffices to verify that

\sum\epsilon_{\mathscr{D}}(\iota(u_{2}))\iota(u_{1})=\iota(u)
. Since
\iota
is an anti-isomorphism of coalgebras, the latter relation follows from the defining property of the counit
\epsilon_{\mathscr{D}}
.
∎

Recall from Proposition
3.9.4
(i)
that the assignments
\tilde{t}_{i,j}\mapsto\tilde{t}_{i,j}
and
\tilde{\partial}_{i,j}\mapsto\tilde{\partial}_{i,j}
result in an embedding of algebras

(96)

\boldsymbol{e}_{k\times l}^{n\times n}:\mathscr{PD}_{k\times l}\to\mathscr{PD}_{n\times n}.

We denote the restriction of the map (
96
)
to the subalgebra
\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}
by the same notation, that is

(97)

\boldsymbol{e}_{k\times l}^{n\times n}:\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}\to\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}.

Lemma 7.1.2
.

For
\boldsymbol{e}=\boldsymbol{e}_{k\times l}^{n\times n}
as
in (
97
)
we have

{}\Gamma_{n}\left(\boldsymbol{e}(\mathscr{P}_{k\times l})\right)\subseteq\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}}
.

Proof.

For a monomial
t_{i_{1},j_{1}}\cdots t_{i_{r},j_{r}}\in\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}
we have

\displaystyle\Delta(t_{i_{1},j_{1}}\cdots t_{i_{r},j_{r}})

\displaystyle=\Delta(t_{i_{1},j_{1}})\cdots\Delta(t_{i_{r},j_{r}})=\sum_{1\leq a_{1},\ldots,a_{r}\leq n}t_{i_{1},a_{1}}\cdots t_{i_{r},a_{r}}\otimes t_{a_{1},j_{1}}\cdots t_{a_{r},j_{r}}.

Set
m^{\prime}:=n-k
and
n^{\prime}:=n-l
. From the above equality it follows that

{{}\Gamma}_{n}\left(\boldsymbol{e}(t_{i_{1},j_{1}}\cdots t_{i_{r},j_{r}})\right)=\sum_{1\leq a_{1},\ldots,a_{r}\leq n}t_{i_{1}+m^{\prime},a_{1}}\cdots t_{i_{r}+m^{\prime},a_{r}}\otimes\partial_{j_{r}+n^{\prime},a_{r}}\cdots\partial_{j_{1}+n^{\prime},a_{1}}.

Thus

{{}\Gamma}_{n}(\boldsymbol{e}(t_{i_{1},j_{1}}\cdots t_{i_{r},j_{r}}))\in\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}}
(see Remark
3.8.4
).
∎

Lemma
7.1.2
justifies that the following definition is valid.

Definition 7.1.3
.

We define
{{}\Gamma}_{k,l,n}:\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}\to\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}}
to be the
unique map that makes the diagram

\textstyle{\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boldsymbol{e}_{k\times l}^{n\times n}}
\scriptstyle{{{}\Gamma}_{k,l,n}}
\textstyle{\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{{}\Gamma}_{n}}
\textstyle{\mathscr{PD}_{n\times n}^{\mathrm{gr}}}
\textstyle{\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

commutative.

Lemma 7.1.4
.

The map
{{}\Gamma}_{k,l,n}
is injective.

Proof.

Since
{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k\times l}^{n\times n}
is an injective map, this follows from Lemma
7.1.1
.
∎

Lemma 7.1.5
.

{{}\Gamma}_{k,l,n}(\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}^{(d)})=\left(\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{gr},(d,d)}_{k,l,n}\right)_{(\epsilon_{R})}
for
d\geq 0
.

Proof.

First we prove that

{{}\Gamma}_{k,l,n}(\mathscr{P}_{k\times l})\subseteq\left(\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{k,l,n}\right)_{(\epsilon_{R})}
.
By Definition
7.1.3

it suffices to prove that
{{}\Gamma}_{n}(\mathscr{P}_{n\times n})\subseteq\left(\mathscr{PD}_{n\times n}^{\mathrm{gr}}\right)_{(\epsilon_{R})}
. By standard properties of the antipode of
U_{R}
,

(98)

\sum x_{2}S^{-1}(x_{1})=\epsilon_{R}(x)1\quad\text{for }x\in U_{R}.

It follows that
for
x\in U_{R}
and

u\in\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}
we have

\displaystyle x\cdot{{}\Gamma}_{n}(u)

\displaystyle=\sum(x_{1}\cdot u_{1})\otimes(x_{2}\cdot\iota(u_{2}))

\displaystyle=\sum\big(u_{11}\langle\iota(u_{12}),S^{-1}(x_{1})\rangle\big)\otimes\big(\iota(u_{2})_{1}\langle\iota(u_{2})_{2},x_{2}\rangle\big)

(By (
55
) and (
56
))

\displaystyle=\sum\langle\iota(u_{2}),S^{-1}(x_{1})\rangle\langle\iota(u_{3}),x_{2}\rangle u_{1}\otimes\iota(u_{4})

(By coassociativity)

\displaystyle=\sum\epsilon_{R}(x)\langle\iota(u_{2}),1\rangle u_{1}\otimes\iota(u_{3})

(By (
13
) and (
98
))

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{R}(x)\sum u_{1}\otimes\iota(u_{2})=\epsilon_{R}(x){{}\Gamma}_{n}(u).

(By counit relation of
\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}
)

Thus we have proved
x\cdot{{}\Gamma}_{n}(u)=\epsilon_{R}(x)\,{{}\Gamma}_{n}(u)
, that is,

{{}\Gamma}_{n}(u)\in\left(\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{n\times n}\right)_{(\epsilon_{R})}
.
From (
95
) it follows that

{{}\Gamma}_{k,l,n}\left(\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}^{(r)}\right)\subseteq\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{gr},(r,r)}_{k,l,n}
.
Consequently,

(99)

{{}\Gamma}_{k,l,n}\left(\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}^{(r)}\right)\subseteq\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{gr},(r,r)}_{k,l,n}\cap\left(\mathscr{PD}_{n\times n}^{\mathrm{gr}}\right)_{(\epsilon_{R})}=\left(\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{gr},(r,r)}_{k,l,n}\right)_{(\epsilon_{R})}.

By Lemma
7.1.4
, to complete the proof
it suffices to verify that the two sides of (
99
) have equal dimensions.
Since
k,l\leq n
, from (
94
) and
Proposition
3.6.1
it follows that both of these vector spaces have dimension equal to
\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{d,r}}\mathsf{d}(\lambda,k)\mathsf{d}(\lambda,l)
where

\mathsf{d}(\lambda,k)
(respectively,
\mathsf{d}(\lambda,l)
) denotes the dimension of the
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{k})
-module (respectively,
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{l})
-module) associated to
\lambda
.
∎

8.
The product
\star_{k,l,n}
on
\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}

Throughout this section we assume that
m=n
(so that

U_{L}\cong U_{R}\cong U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
) and
1\leq k,l\leq n
.

8.1.
An explicit formula for the product of
\mathscr{PD}_{n\times n}^{\mathrm{gr}}

Recall that
\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}
and
\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}
are subalgebras of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})^{\bullet}
(see Definition
3.2.3
and
Definition
3.4.1
).
Also recall that given
f,g\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})^{\bullet}
, we define
\big\langle f\otimes g,\mathcal{R}^{(n)}\big\rangle

and
\big\langle f\otimes g,\underline{\mathcal{R}}^{(n)}\big\rangle

as in
(
36
).

Proposition 8.1.1
.

Let
a,a^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}
and
b,b^{\prime}\in\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}
.
Then the product of

\mathscr{PD}_{n\times n}^{\mathrm{gr}}
satisfies

(a\otimes b)(a^{\prime}\otimes b^{\prime})=\sum\langle\iota(a_{1}^{\prime})^{\natural}\otimes(b_{1})^{\natural},\mathcal{R}^{(n)}\rangle\langle\iota(a_{3}^{\prime})\otimes b_{3},\mathcal{R}^{(n)}\rangle aa_{2}^{\prime}\otimes b_{2}b^{\prime},

where
(\Delta_{\mathscr{P}}\otimes 1)\circ\Delta_{\mathscr{P}}(a^{\prime})=\sum a^{\prime}_{1}\otimes a^{\prime}_{2}\otimes a^{\prime}_{3}
and

(\Delta_{\mathscr{P}}\otimes 1)\circ\Delta_{\mathscr{P}}(b)=\sum b_{1}\otimes b_{2}\otimes b_{3}
, with
\Delta_{\mathscr{P}}
and
\Delta_{\mathscr{D}}
denoting the coproducts of
\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}
and
\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}
respectively.

Proof.

We need to compute
\left(\check{\underline{\mathcal{R}}}_{LR}\right)_{B,A}(b\otimes a^{\prime})
where
B:=\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}
and
A:=\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}
.
Recall from (
27
) that

\check{\underline{\mathcal{R}}}_{LR}=(\check{\underline{\mathcal{R}}}_{L})_{13}(\check{\underline{\mathcal{R}}}_{R})_{24}
. The map

\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}\to U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})^{\circ}\ ,\ a\mapsto a\circ\xi_{-1/q}

intertwines between the
U_{R}
-module structures
\mathcal{R}_{\mathscr{P}}

and right translation
on
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})^{\circ}
, in the sense of Remark
2.2.1
.
Thus
Lemma
2.7.2
implies that

(100)

(\underline{\mathcal{R}}_{R})_{24}(b\otimes a^{\prime})=\sum b_{1}\otimes a^{\prime}_{1}\langle b_{2}\otimes(a_{2}^{\prime}\circ\xi_{-1/q}),\underline{\mathcal{R}}^{(n)}\rangle.

Similarly, the maps

\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}\to U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})^{\circ}\ ,\ b\mapsto b^{\natural}\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}\to U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})^{\circ}\ ,\ a\mapsto(a\circ\xi_{-q})^{\natural}

intertwine the actions
\mathcal{L}_{\mathscr{D}}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\mathscr{P}}
(on
\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}
and

\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}
respectively)
with right translation.
This is because the map
u\mapsto u^{\natural}
on
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})^{\circ}
that is defined in (
49
) is an anti-automorphism of the coalgebra structure of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})^{\circ}
.
Thus

(101)

\left(\underline{\mathcal{R}}_{L}\right)_{13}(b\otimes a^{\prime})=\sum b_{2}\otimes a^{\prime}_{2}\langle(b_{1})^{\natural}\otimes(a_{1}^{\prime}\circ\xi_{-q})^{\natural},\underline{\mathcal{R}}^{(n)}\rangle.

From (
100
) and (
101
)
it follows that

(102)

(a\otimes b)(a^{\prime}\otimes b^{\prime})=\sum\langle(b_{1})^{\natural}\otimes(a_{1}^{\prime}\circ\xi_{-q})^{\natural},\underline{\mathcal{R}}^{(n)}\rangle\langle b_{3}\otimes(a_{3}^{\prime}\circ\xi_{-1/q}),\underline{\mathcal{R}}^{(n)}\rangle aa_{2}^{\prime}\otimes b_{2}b^{\prime}.

Since
S^{2}(x)=\xi_{q^{2}}(x)
for
x\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
, we have

\iota(v)\circ S=v\circ\xi_{-1/q}\circ S^{2}=v\circ\xi_{-q}

for
v\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})^{\circ}
, and thus Lemma
3.3.1
implies that

(v\circ\xi_{-q})^{\natural}=(\iota(v)\circ S)^{\natural}=\iota(v)^{\natural}\circ S^{-1}
. By a similar argument
v\circ\xi_{-1/q}=\iota(v)\circ S^{-1}
.
Thus in (
102
) we can substitute
(a_{1}^{\prime}\circ\xi_{-q})^{\natural}
by
\iota(a_{1}^{\prime})^{\natural}\circ S^{-1}
and
a_{3}^{\prime}\circ\xi_{-1/q}
by
\iota(a^{\prime}_{3})\circ S^{-1}
.
After these substitutions, the assertion of the proposition follows from (
46
).
∎

8.2.
The product
\star_{k,l,n}
on
\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}
and the map

{{}\Gamma}_{k,l,n}

We start by defining a binary product

\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}\otimes\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}\to\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}\ ,\ u\otimes v\mapsto u\star_{n}v.

Definition 8.2.1
.

For
u,v\in\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}
we
set

(103)

u\star_{n}v:=\sum\big\langle\iota(v_{1})^{\natural}\otimes\iota(u_{3})^{\natural},\mathcal{R}^{(n)}\big\rangle\big\langle\iota(v_{3})\otimes\iota(u_{2}),\mathcal{R}^{(n)}\big\rangle u_{1}v_{2},

where the sum ranges over summands of
(\Delta_{\mathscr{P}}\otimes 1)\circ\Delta_{\mathscr{P}}(u)=\sum u_{1}\otimes u_{2}\otimes u_{3}
and

(\Delta_{\mathscr{P}}\otimes 1)\circ\Delta_{\mathscr{P}}(v)=\sum v_{1}\otimes v_{2}\otimes v_{3}
.

For the next proposition recall that
{{}\Gamma}_{n}:\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}\to\mathscr{PD}_{n\times n}^{\mathrm{gr}}
is the map defined in (
95
).

Proposition 8.2.2
.

{{}\Gamma_{n}}(u\star_{n}v)={{}\Gamma_{n}}(u){{}\Gamma_{n}}(v)
for
u,v\in\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
8.1.1
for
a:=u_{1}
,
b:=\iota(u_{2})
,
a^{\prime}:=v_{1}
and
b^{\prime}:=\iota(v_{2})
we obtain

\displaystyle{{}\Gamma_{n}}(u){{}\Gamma_{n}}(v)

\displaystyle=\sum(u_{1}\otimes\iota(u_{2}))(v_{1}\otimes\iota(v_{2}))

\displaystyle=\sum\langle\iota(v_{1})^{\natural}\otimes\iota(u_{4})^{\natural},\mathcal{R}^{(n)}\rangle\langle\iota(v_{3})\otimes\iota(u_{2}),\mathcal{R}^{(n)}\rangle u_{1}v_{2}\otimes\iota(u_{3})\iota(v_{4}).

Since
u\mapsto\iota(u)
is an anti-automorphism of algebras, by (
103
) we also have

\displaystyle{{}\Gamma_{n}}(u\star_{n}v)

\displaystyle=\sum\langle\iota(v_{1})^{\natural}\otimes\iota(u_{4})^{\natural},\mathcal{R}^{(n)}\rangle\langle\iota(v_{4})\otimes\iota(u_{3}),\mathcal{R}^{(n)}\rangle u_{1}v_{2}\otimes\iota(v_{3})\iota(u_{2}).

After changing the indices as
(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3},u_{4})=(u_{1},u_{21},u_{22},u_{3})
and
(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4})=(v_{1},v_{2},v_{31},v_{32})
by coassociativity, the equality
{{}\Gamma_{n}}(u\star_{n}v)={{}\Gamma_{n}}(u){{}\Gamma_{n}}(v)
reduces to

(104)

\sum\langle\iota(v_{31})\otimes\iota(u_{21}),\mathcal{R}^{(n)}\rangle\iota(u_{22})\iota(v_{32})=\sum\langle\iota(v_{32})\otimes\iota(u_{22}),\mathcal{R}^{(n)}\rangle\iota(v_{31})\iota(u_{21}).

Set
f:=\iota(v_{3})
and
g:=\iota(u_{2})
. Since
u\mapsto\iota(u)
is an anti-automorphism of coalgebras, from (
46
) it follows
that (
104
) is equivalent to

\sum\langle f_{2}\otimes g_{2},\mathcal{R}^{(n)}\rangle g_{1}f_{1}=\sum\langle f_{1}\otimes g_{1},\mathcal{R}^{(n)}\rangle f_{2}g_{2},

which is a consequence of (
38
).
∎

Recall that throughout this section
1\leq k,l\leq n
.

Lemma 8.2.3
.

Let
\boldsymbol{e}:=\boldsymbol{e}_{k\times l}^{n\times n}
be as
in (
97
).
Then

\boldsymbol{e}(u)\star_{n}\boldsymbol{e}(v)\in\boldsymbol{e}(\mathscr{P}_{k\times l})
for
u,v\in\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}
.

Proof.

Set

D:={{}\Gamma}_{n}(\boldsymbol{e}(u)\star_{n}\boldsymbol{e}(v))
. By Lemma
7.1.5
we have

{{}\Gamma}_{n}(\boldsymbol{e}(u))={{}\Gamma}_{k,l,n}(u)\in\left(\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{k,l,n}\right)_{(\epsilon_{R})}\quad\text{and}\quad{{}\Gamma}_{n}(\boldsymbol{e}(v))={{}\Gamma}_{k,l,n}(v)\in\left(\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{k,l,n}\right)_{(\epsilon_{R})}.

Since
\left(\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}}\right)_{(\epsilon_{R})}
is a subalgeba of
\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}}
, from Proposition
8.2.2
it follows that

D\in\left(\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}}\right)_{(\epsilon_{R})}
.
Again by Lemma
7.1.5

there exists
w\in\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}
such that
D={{}\Gamma}_{k,l,n}(w)=\Gamma_{n}(\boldsymbol{e}(w))
.
From injectivity of
\Gamma_{n}
(see Lemma
7.1.1
) it follows that
\boldsymbol{e}(u)\star_{n}\boldsymbol{e}(v)=\boldsymbol{e}(w)
. Consequently we obtain
\boldsymbol{e}(u)\star_{n}\boldsymbol{e}(v)\in\boldsymbol{e}(\mathscr{P}_{k\times l})
.
∎

Lemma
8.2.3
validates the following definition.

Definition 8.2.4
.

For
u,v\in\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}
we
define a binary product

\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}\otimes\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}\to\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}\ ,\ u\otimes v\mapsto u\star_{k,l,n}v,

by setting

u\star_{k,l,n}v:=\boldsymbol{e}^{-1}\left(\boldsymbol{e}(u)\star_{n}\boldsymbol{e}(v)\right)
where
\boldsymbol{e}:=\boldsymbol{e}_{k\times l}^{n\times n}
is the map (
97
).

Proposition 8.2.5
.

Let
u,v\in\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}
. Then the following statements hold:

(i)

{{}\Gamma}_{k,l,n}(u\star_{k,l,n}v)={{}\Gamma}_{k,l,n}(u){{}\Gamma}_{k,l,n}(v)
.

(ii)

If
u\in\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}^{(r)}

and
v\in\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}^{(s)}
then

u\star_{k,l,n}v\in\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}^{(r+s)}
and
{{}\Gamma}_{k,l,n}(u\star_{k,l,n}v)\in\left(\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{gr},(r+s,r+s)}_{k,l,n}\right)_{(\epsilon_{R})}
.

Proof.

(i)
By Proposition
8.2.2
we have

\displaystyle{{}\Gamma}_{k,l,n}(u\star_{k,l,n}v)

\displaystyle={{}\Gamma}_{k,l,n}(\boldsymbol{e}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{e}(u)\star_{n}\boldsymbol{e}(v)))

\displaystyle={{}\Gamma}_{n}(\boldsymbol{e}(u)\star_{n}\boldsymbol{e}(n))={{}\Gamma}_{n}(\boldsymbol{e}(u)){{}\Gamma}_{n}(\boldsymbol{e}(v))={{}\Gamma}_{k,l,n}(u){{}\Gamma}_{k,l,n}(v).

(ii) By definitions of
\star_{k,l,n}
and

{{}\Gamma}_{k,l,n}
the assertions reduce to proving that for
u\in\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}^{(r)}
and

v\in\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}^{(s)}

we have

u\star_{n}v\in\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}^{(r+s)}
and

{{}\Gamma}_{n}(u\star_{n}v)\in\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr},(r+s,r+s)}_{n\times n}
. The latter assertions follow
from (
103
) and the fact that for
d\geq 0
we have
\Delta_{\mathscr{P}}(\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}^{(d)})\subseteq\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}^{(d)}\otimes\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}^{(d)}
.
∎

8.3.
The map
\Upsilon

Let

\Upsilon:\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}\otimes\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}\to\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}\otimes\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}
be the map defined by

(105)

\displaystyle\Upsilon(u\otimes v)

\displaystyle:=\sum\langle\iota(v_{1})^{\natural}\otimes\iota(u_{3})^{\natural},{\mathcal{R}^{(n)}}\rangle\langle\iota(v_{3})\otimes\iota(u_{2}),\mathcal{R}^{(n)}\rangle u_{1}\otimes v_{2}.

Lemma 8.3.1
.

Let
\mathsf{m}_{n\times n}:\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}\otimes\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}\to\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}
denote the usual product of the algebra
\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}
.
Then the following statements hold.

(i)

u\star_{n}v=\mathsf{m}_{n\times n}\circ\Upsilon(u\otimes v)
for
u,v\in\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}
.

(ii)

u\star_{k,l,n}v=\boldsymbol{e}^{-1}\big(\mathsf{m}_{n\times n}(\Upsilon(\boldsymbol{e}(u)\otimes\boldsymbol{e}(v)))\big)
for
u,v\in\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}
where
\boldsymbol{e}:=\boldsymbol{e}_{k\times l}^{n\times n}
is as in (
97
).

Proof.

Straightforward from
Definition
8.2.1
.
∎

Lemma 8.3.2
.

\langle\partial_{i_{1},j_{1}}\cdots\partial_{i_{r},j_{r}},1\rangle=0
unless when
i_{k}=j_{k}
for
1\leq k\leq r
.

Proof.

Follows immediately from the definition of the
\partial_{i,j}
and the canonical pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
between
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})^{\circ}
and
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
.
∎

Proposition 8.3.3
.

Let
\boldsymbol{e}:=\boldsymbol{e}_{k\times l}^{n\times n}
be as in (
97
). Set
\mathscr{W}_{d_{1},d_{2}}:=\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}^{(d_{1})}\otimes\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}^{(d_{2})}
for
d_{1},d_{2}\geq 0
and

\mathscr{W}^{\prime}_{d_{1},d_{2}}:=(\boldsymbol{e}\otimes\boldsymbol{e})(\mathscr{W}_{d_{1},d_{2}})
. Then
\Upsilon(\mathscr{W}^{\prime}_{d_{1},d_{2}})\subseteq\mathscr{W}^{\prime}_{d_{1},d_{2}}
.

Proof.

From the defining formula of
\Upsilon
and the fact that the coproduct of
\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}
maps
\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}^{(a)}
into
\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}^{(a)}\otimes\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}^{(a)}
we obtain
\Upsilon\left(\mathscr{P}_{n,n}^{(a)}\otimes\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}^{(b)}\right)\subseteq\mathscr{P}_{n,n}^{(a)}\otimes\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}^{(b)}
. The claim follows if we prove that

(106)

\Upsilon(\boldsymbol{e}(u)\otimes\boldsymbol{e}(v))\in\boldsymbol{e}\otimes\boldsymbol{e}\left(\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}\otimes\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}\right)\quad\text{ for }u,v\in\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}.

It suffices to prove this assertion for monomials
u=t_{i_{1},j_{1}}\cdots t_{i_{r},j_{r}}
and
v=t_{p_{1},q_{1}}\cdots t_{p_{s},q_{s}}
in
\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}
.
Set
m^{\prime}:=n-k
and
n^{\prime}:=n-l
. Then

((\Delta\otimes 1)\circ\Delta)(\boldsymbol{e}(u))=\sum\boldsymbol{e}(u)_{1}\otimes\boldsymbol{e}(u)_{2}\otimes\boldsymbol{e}(u)_{3},

where
for indices
1\leq a_{1},b_{1},\ldots,a_{r},b_{r}\leq n
we have

\boldsymbol{e}(u)_{1}=t_{m^{\prime}+i_{1},a_{1}}\cdots t_{m^{\prime}+i_{r},a_{r}}\quad,\quad\boldsymbol{e}(u)_{2}=t_{a_{1},b_{1}}\cdots t_{a_{r},b_{r}}\quad,\quad\boldsymbol{e}(u)_{3}=t_{b_{1},n^{\prime}+j_{1}}\cdots t_{b_{r},n^{\prime}+j_{r}}.

Similarly,

((\Delta\otimes 1)\circ\Delta)(\boldsymbol{e}(v))=\sum\boldsymbol{e}(v)_{1}\otimes\boldsymbol{e}(v)_{2}\otimes\boldsymbol{e}(v)_{3},

where for indices
1\leq c_{1},d_{1},\ldots,c_{s},d_{s}\leq n
we have

\boldsymbol{e}(v)_{1}=t_{m^{\prime}+p_{1},c_{1}}\cdots t_{m^{\prime}+p_{s},c_{s}}\quad,\quad\boldsymbol{e}(v)_{2}=t_{c_{1},d_{1}}\cdots t_{c_{s},d_{s}}\quad,\quad\boldsymbol{e}(v)_{3}=t_{d_{1},n^{\prime}+q_{1}}\cdots t_{d_{s},n^{\prime}+q_{s}}.

In the rest of this proof we set

\mathsf{a}:=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{r})\ ,\ \mathsf{b}:=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{r})\ ,\ \mathsf{c}:=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{s})\ ,\ \mathsf{d}:=(d_{1},\ldots,d_{s}).

Step 1.

From (
105
) it follows that

(107)

\Upsilon(\boldsymbol{e}(u)\otimes\boldsymbol{e}(v))=\sum_{\mathsf{a,b,c,d}}M(\mathsf{b,c})M^{\prime}(\mathsf{a,b,d})t_{m^{\prime}+i_{1},a_{1}}\cdots t_{m^{\prime}+i_{r},a_{r}}\otimes t_{c_{1},d_{1}}\cdots t_{c_{s},d_{s}},

where using the fact that the map
u\mapsto u^{\natural}
of (
49
) induces an isomorphism between

U(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})^{\circ}
and
\left(U(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})^{\circ}\right)^{\mathrm{cop}}
we have

M(\mathsf{b,c}):=\langle\partial_{m^{\prime}+p_{s},c_{s}}\cdots\partial_{m^{\prime}+p_{1},c_{1}}\otimes\partial_{b_{r},n^{\prime}+j_{r}}\cdots\partial_{b_{1},n^{\prime}+j_{1}}\,,\,\mathcal{R}^{(n)}\rangle

and

M^{\prime}(\mathsf{a,b,d}):=\langle\partial_{n^{\prime}+q_{s},d_{s}}\cdots\partial_{n^{\prime}+q_{1},d_{1}}\otimes\partial_{b_{r},a_{r}}\cdots\partial_{b_{1},a_{1}}\,,\,\mathcal{R}^{(n)}\rangle.

Note that by definition, both of the
R
-matrix pairings

M(\mathsf{b},\mathsf{c})
and
M^{\prime}(\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b},\mathsf{d})

correspond to the action of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
on
\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}\subseteq U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})^{\circ}
by
right
translation
\mathcal{R}_{\mathscr{D}}
(See Subsection
2.7
).

Step 2.

We prove that if
c_{t}\leq m^{\prime}
for some
1\leq t\leq s
then

M(\mathsf{b,c})=0
. To this end, we investigate the effect of the action of
\mathcal{R}^{(n)}
on the first component in
M(\mathsf{b,c})
, i.e., on
\partial_{m^{\prime}+p_{s},c_{s}}\cdots\partial_{m^{\prime}+p_{1},c_{1}}
.
Recall that

\mathcal{R}^{(n)}
acts by a product of 2-tensors of the form
E_{\beta}\otimes F_{\beta}
, where
\beta=\varepsilon_{\ell_{1}}-\varepsilon_{\ell_{2}}
for
1\leq\ell_{1}<\ell_{2}\leq n
, followed by
e^{h\sum_{i=1}^{n}H_{i}\otimes H_{i}}
(which acts by scalars on tensor product of monomials in the
\partial
’s and we can ignore it in the argument that follows). The
s
-fold coproduct of
E_{\beta}
is a sum of
s
-tensors of the form
X:=X_{s}\otimes\cdots\otimes X_{1}
with components in
\{E_{\beta},K_{\beta},1\}
. From Remark
3.6.2
it follows that the action of
X
on any monomial
\partial_{m^{\prime}+p_{s},\bar{c}_{s}}\cdots\partial_{m^{\prime}+p_{1},\bar{c}_{1}}

does not increase the indices
\bar{c}_{1},\ldots,\bar{c}_{s}
and leaves the indices
n^{\prime}+p_{1},\ldots,n^{\prime}+p_{s}
unchanged.
Thus Lemma
8.3.2
implies that
M(\mathsf{b,c})=0
.

Step 3.

We prove that if
d_{t}\leq n^{\prime}
for some
1\leq t\leq s
then
M^{\prime}(\mathsf{a,b,d})=0
.
The argument is similar to Step 2, by investigating the action of root vectors
E_{\beta}
on the first component of
M^{\prime}(\mathsf{a,b,d})
, that is on
\partial_{n^{\prime}+q_{s},d_{s}}\cdots\partial_{n^{\prime}+q_{1},d_{1}}
.

Step 4.
We prove that if
b_{t}\leq n^{\prime}
for some
1\leq t\leq r
then
M(\mathsf{b,c})=0
. Again the argument is similar to Step 2. This time use use the fact that the action of the root vectors
F_{\beta}
does not decrease the indices
n^{\prime}+j_{1},\ldots,n^{\prime}+j_{r}
.

Step 5.

We prove that if
a_{t}\leq n^{\prime}
for some
1\leq t\leq r
then
M(\mathsf{b,c})M^{\prime}(\mathsf{a,b,d})=0
. The proof is slightly more complicated than Steps 2–4. By Step 4 we can assume that
\min\{b_{1},\ldots,b_{r}\}\geq n^{\prime}+1
.
As in Steps 2–4 we can express

M^{\prime}(\mathsf{a,b,d})
as a sum over the values

(108)

C_{\partial_{E},\partial_{F},\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{N}}\big(\langle\left(E_{\beta_{1}}\cdots E_{\beta_{N}}\right)\cdot\partial_{E}\,,1\rangle\big)\big(\langle\left(F_{\beta_{1}}\cdots F_{\beta_{N}}\right)\cdot\partial_{F}\,,1\rangle\big),

where
\partial_{E}:=\partial_{n^{\prime}+q_{s},d_{s}}\cdots\partial_{n^{\prime}+q_{1},d_{1}}
,

\partial_{F}:=\partial_{b_{r},a_{r}}\cdots\partial_{b_{1},a_{1}}
and

C_{\partial_{E},\partial_{F},\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{N}}
is a scalar in
\Bbbk
that results from the action of
e^{h\sum_{i=1}^{n}H_{i}\otimes H_{i}}
(again, this scalar does not play a role in the argument that follows). For
\beta=\varepsilon_{\ell_{1}}-\varepsilon_{\ell_{2}}
with
1\leq\ell_{1}<\ell_{2}\leq n
we have

(109)

E_{\beta}\cdot\partial_{\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}}=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }\ell_{2}\neq\ell^{\prime\prime},\\
(-1)^{\ell_{2}-\ell_{1}-1}\partial_{\ell^{\prime},\ell_{1}}&\text{ if }\ell_{2}=\ell^{\prime\prime},\end{cases}

and

(110)

F_{\beta}\cdot\partial_{\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}}=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }\ell_{1}\neq\ell^{\prime\prime},\\
(-1)^{\ell_{2}-\ell_{1}-1}\partial_{\ell^{\prime},\ell_{2}}&\text{ if }\ell_{1}=\ell^{\prime\prime}.\end{cases}

First suppose that there exists
1\leq N^{\prime}\leq N
such that
\beta_{N^{\prime}}=\varepsilon_{\ell_{1}}-\varepsilon_{\ell_{2}}
with

\ell_{1}\leq n^{\prime}
. Then by (
109
) and
an argument similar to Step 2 we obtain

\langle\left(E_{\beta_{1}}\cdots E_{\beta_{N}}\right)\cdot\partial_{E}\,,1\rangle=0
, hence the corresponding value (
108
) vanishes. Next suppose that
for all
1\leq N^{\prime}\leq N
we have

\beta_{N^{\prime}}=\varepsilon_{\ell_{1}}-\varepsilon_{\ell_{2}}
where
\ell_{1}\geq n^{\prime}+1
.
The

r
-fold coproduct of
F_{\beta_{1}}\cdots F_{\beta_{N}}
is a sum of
r
-tensors
X=X_{r}\otimes\cdots\otimes X_{1}
whose components are products of the
F_{\beta_{N^{\prime}}}
and the
K_{\beta_{N^{\prime}}}^{-1}
. If no
F_{\beta_{N^{\prime}}}
occurs in
X_{t}
then
Lemma
8.3.2
implies that
\langle X\cdot\partial_{F}\,,1\rangle=0
unless
a_{t}=b_{t}\geq n^{\prime}+1
. If at least one
F_{\beta_{N^{\prime}}}
occurs in
X_{t}
then from (
110
) it follows that

\langle X\cdot\partial_{F}\,,1\rangle=0
unless
a_{t}\geq n^{\prime}+1
(because we must have
a_{t}=\ell_{1}
). Since we have assumed that
a_{t}\leq n^{\prime}
we obtain
\langle(F_{\beta_{1}}\cdots F_{\beta_{N}})\cdot\partial_{F}\,,1\rangle=0
. Thus all the values (
108
) vanish and we have
M^{\prime}(\mathsf{a,b,d})=0
.

Step 6.
From Steps
2–5 it follows that the only 2-tensors on the right hand side of (
107
) that have a nonzero coefficient are those that
belong to
(\boldsymbol{e}\otimes\boldsymbol{e})\left(\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}\otimes\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}\right)
. This completes the proof of (
106
).
∎

Proposition 8.3.4
.

\Upsilon
induces linear bijections
\mathscr{P}_{n,n}^{(r)}\otimes\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}^{(s)}\to\mathscr{P}_{n,n}^{(r)}\otimes\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}^{(s)}
for all
r,s\geq 0
.

Proof.

Since
\mathscr{P}_{n,n}^{(r)}\otimes\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}^{(s)}
is finite dimensional, it suffices to prove that
\Upsilon
is an injection.
Set
\Upsilon^{\iota}:=(\iota^{-1}\otimes\iota^{-1})\circ\Upsilon\circ(\iota\otimes\iota)
with
\iota
as in (
52
). Since
\iota
is an antiautomorphism of bialgebras and preseves the grading of
\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}
, we have

\Upsilon^{\iota}:\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}^{(r)}\otimes\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}^{(s)}\to\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}^{(r)}\otimes\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}^{(s)}\quad,\quad\Upsilon^{\iota}(u\otimes v)=\sum\langle(v_{3})^{\natural}\otimes(u_{1})^{\natural},\mathcal{R}^{(n)}\rangle\langle v_{1}\otimes u_{2},\mathcal{R}^{(n)}\rangle u_{3}\otimes v_{2}.

It suffices to prove injectivity of
\Upsilon^{\iota}
.

Step 1.

Define
\Upsilon^{(1)}:\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}^{(r)}\otimes\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}^{(s)}\to\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}^{(r)}\otimes\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}^{(s)}
by

\Upsilon^{(1)}(u\otimes v):=\sum\langle u_{1}\otimes v_{1},\underline{\mathcal{R}}^{(n)}\rangle u_{2}\otimes v_{2}.

From Lemma
2.7.2
(i)
we have

\sum f_{1}\otimes g_{1}\langle g_{2}\otimes f_{2},\underline{\mathcal{R}}^{(n)}\rangle=\sigma\left(\sum g_{1}\otimes f_{1}\langle g_{2}\otimes f_{2},\underline{\mathcal{R}}^{(n)}\rangle\right)=\sigma\circ\underline{\mathcal{R}}^{(n)}(g\otimes f)=\big(\mathcal{R}^{(n)}\big)^{-1}(f\otimes g).

From this and Lemma
2.7.2
(ii) it follows that

\displaystyle\Upsilon^{(1)}\circ

\displaystyle\Upsilon^{\iota}(u\otimes v)=\sum\langle(v_{4})^{\natural}\otimes(u_{1})^{\natural},\mathcal{R}^{(n)}\rangle\langle v_{1}\otimes u_{2},\mathcal{R}^{(n)}\rangle\langle u_{3}\otimes v_{2},\underline{\mathcal{R}}^{(n)}\rangle u_{4}\otimes v_{3}

\displaystyle=\sum\langle(v_{3})^{\natural}\otimes(u_{1})^{\natural},\mathcal{R}^{(n)}\rangle\langle\big({\mathcal{R}}^{(n)}\big)^{-1}(v_{1}\otimes u_{2}),\mathcal{R}^{(n)}\rangle u_{3}\otimes v_{2}=\sum\langle(v_{2})^{\natural}\otimes(u_{1})^{\natural},\mathcal{R}^{(n)}\rangle u_{2}\otimes v_{1}.

Step 2.

Define
\Upsilon^{(2)}:\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}^{(r)}\otimes\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}^{(s)}\to\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}^{(r)}\otimes\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}^{(s)}
by
\Upsilon^{(2)}(u\otimes v):=u^{\natural}\otimes v
.
Set

\breve{\Upsilon}:=\left(\Upsilon^{(2)}\right)^{-1}\circ\left(\Upsilon^{(1)}\circ\Upsilon^{\iota}\right)\circ\Upsilon^{(2)}.

Since
u\mapsto u^{\natural}
is an antiautomorphism of coalgebras on
\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}
, we obtain

\breve{\Upsilon}(u\otimes v)=\sum\langle(v_{2})^{\natural}\otimes u_{2},\mathcal{R}^{(n)}\rangle u_{1}\otimes v_{1}.

Step 3.

From Lemmas
3.4.2
,
3.4.3
and
3.4.4
it follows that the assignment
v\mapsto(v\circ S)^{\natural}
induces an isomorphism of coalgebras

\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}\to\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}

that preserves the grading.
Define a map

\Upsilon^{(3)}:\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}^{(r)}\otimes\mathscr{P}_{n\times n}^{(s)}\to\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}^{(r)}\otimes\mathscr{D}_{n\times n}^{(s)}\ ,\ \Upsilon^{(3)}(u\otimes v):=u\otimes((v\circ S)^{\natural}).

Using (
46
) we obtain

\left(\Upsilon^{(3)}\right)^{-1}\circ\breve{\Upsilon}\circ\Upsilon^{(3)}(u\otimes v)=\sum\langle(v_{2}\circ S)\otimes u_{2},\mathcal{R}^{(n)}\rangle u_{1}\otimes v_{1}=\sum\langle u_{2}\otimes v_{2},\underline{\mathcal{R}}^{(n)}\rangle u_{1}\otimes v_{1}.

Lemma
2.7.2
(i) implies that

\left(\Upsilon^{(3)}\right)^{-1}\circ\breve{\Upsilon}\circ\Upsilon^{(3)}(u\otimes v)=\underline{\mathcal{R}}^{(n)}(u\otimes v)
.
Since the map
u\otimes v\mapsto\underline{\mathcal{R}}^{(n)}(u\otimes v)
is an injection,
\Upsilon^{\iota}
is also an injection.
∎

Corollary 8.3.5
.

Let
\boldsymbol{e}:=\boldsymbol{e}_{k\times l}^{n\times n}
be as in (
97
) and let

\mathsf{m}_{k\times l}:\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}\otimes\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}\to\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}
be the usual product of
\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}
.
Then the following statements hold.

(i)

u\star_{k,l,n}v=\mathsf{m}_{k\times l}\circ(\boldsymbol{e}^{-1}\otimes\boldsymbol{e}^{-1})\circ\Upsilon(\boldsymbol{e}(u)\otimes\boldsymbol{e}(v))

for
u,v\in\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}
.

(ii)

For
r,s\geq 0
the map
\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}^{(r)}\otimes\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}^{(s)}\to\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}^{(r+s)}
given by
u\otimes v\mapsto u\star_{k,l,n}v
is surjective.

Proof.

(i) By Proposition
8.3.3
we have
\Upsilon(\boldsymbol{e}(u)\otimes\boldsymbol{e}(v))\in(\boldsymbol{e}\otimes\boldsymbol{e})(\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}\otimes\mathscr{P}_{k\times l})
. The assertion follows from the relation
\mathsf{m}_{k\times l}=\boldsymbol{e}^{-1}\circ\mathsf{m}_{n\times n}\circ(\boldsymbol{e}\otimes\boldsymbol{e})
and Lemma
8.3.1
(ii).

(ii)
By Proposition
8.3.4
and
Proposition
8.3.3
the map

\Upsilon_{k,l,n}:=(\boldsymbol{e}^{-1}\otimes\boldsymbol{e}^{-1})\circ\Upsilon\circ(\boldsymbol{e}\otimes\boldsymbol{e})

is a linear bijection on
\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}^{(r)}\otimes\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}^{(s)}
. From (i) it follows that
u\star_{k,l,n}v=\mathsf{m}_{k\times l}(\Upsilon_{k,l,n}(u\otimes v))
.
The latter equality reduces the assertion to surjectivity of
\mathsf{m}_{k\times l}:\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}^{(r)}\otimes\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}^{(s)}\to\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}^{(r+s)}
, which is a trivial statement.
∎

9.
Proofs of Theorem
A
and Theorem
B

We begin by describing our strategy for proving Theorems
A

and
B
.
Lemma
3.15.1
implies that Theorem
A
(ii) follows by symmetry from Theorem
A
(i).
Furthermore, Lemma
6.1.2

implies that
Theorem
A
(i) is the special case of Theorem
B
for
k=l=m
. Thus, it suffices to prove Theorem
B
.

We now give an outline of the proof of Theorem
B
. By Corollary
3.13.4
we have a
U_{R}
-equivariant isomorphism of
\Bbbk
-algebras
\mathrm{gr}(\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n})\cong\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}}
.
Recall that by definition,
\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}}
is a subalgebra of

\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr}}:=\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{m\times n}
where
m:=\max\{k,l\}
.
Theorem
B
for
\mathrm{gr}(\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n})
is equivalent to the following assertion for
\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{k,l,n}
.

Theorem B
′
.

The algebra
\left(\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}}\right)_{(\epsilon_{R})}
is generated by
the

\tilde{\mathsf{L}}_{i,j}^{\mathrm{gr}}

for

1\leq i\leq k
and
1\leq j\leq l
, where

\tilde{\mathsf{L}}_{i,j}^{\mathrm{gr}}
is defined as in
(
91
).

Let

\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}}
denote the subalgebra generated by the
\tilde{\mathsf{L}}_{i,j}^{\mathrm{gr}}
for
1\leq i\leq k
and
1\leq j\leq l
.
Lemma
6.1.4
implies that
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\big(\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}}\big)_{(\epsilon_{R})}
.
Let
\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr},(r,s)}
be defined as in (
68
).
Since by (
94
)
we have
\left(\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{gr},(r,s)}_{k,l,n}\right)_{(\epsilon_{R})}=0
for
r\neq s
,
to prove Theorem
B′
it suffices to verify that

(111)

\left(\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr},(r,r)}\right)_{(\epsilon_{R})}\subseteq\mathcal{B}\quad\text{for }r\geq 0.

We will verify (
111
) in the case

n\geq\max\{k,l\}

in Subsection
9.1

and
in the case
n<\max\{k,l\}

in Subsection
9.2
.
This completes the proof of Theorem
B′
in both cases.
Then in Subsection
9.3
we
reduce Theorem
B
for
\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}
to Theorem
B′
.

9.1.
Proof of Theorem
B′
when
n\geq\max\{k,l\}

We prove by induction on
r

that

\left(\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr},(r,r)}\right)_{(\epsilon_{R})}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{r}\quad\text{for }r\geq 0,

where

\mathcal{B}_{r}:=\mathrm{Span}_{\Bbbk}\left\{\tilde{\mathsf{L}}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{i_{1},j_{1}}\cdots\tilde{\mathsf{L}}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{i_{r},j_{r}}\,:\,1\leq i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\leq k\ ,\ 1\leq j_{1},\ldots,j_{r}\leq l\right\}.

For
r=0
the assertion is trivial. For
r=1
,
from Lemma
7.1.5
it follows that

\left(\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr},(1,1)}\right)_{(\epsilon_{R})}
is spanned by the
\tilde{\mathsf{L}}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{i,j}=\Gamma_{k,l,n}(\tilde{t}_{i,j})
for
1\leq i\leq k
and
1\leq j\leq l
, where
\tilde{t}_{i,j}
is defined as in (
5
) for
a:=k
and
b:=l
.
Finally, assume
r>1
and
choose any
D\in\left(\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{(r,r)}\right)_{(\epsilon_{R})}
. By Lemma
7.1.5
we have

D={{}\Gamma}_{k,l,n}(u)
for some

u\in\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}^{(r)}
.
By Corollary
8.3.5
(ii) the linear map

\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}^{(1)}\otimes\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}^{(r-1)}\to\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}^{(r)}\ ,\ u\otimes v\mapsto u\star_{k,l,n}v

is a surjection. Thus, we can express
u
as a sum of products of the form
u^{\prime}\star_{k,l,n}u^{\prime\prime}
where
u^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}^{(1)}
and

u^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{P}_{k\times l}^{(r-1)}
. By Proposition
8.2.5
(i),

{{}\Gamma}_{k,l,n}(u^{\prime}\star_{k,l,n}u^{\prime\prime})={{}\Gamma}_{k,l,n}(u^{\prime})\,{{}\Gamma}_{k,l,n}(u^{\prime\prime}).

From Lemma
7.1.5
and the induction hypothesis
it follows that

\Gamma_{k,l,n}(u^{\prime})\in\mathcal{B}_{1}
and

\Gamma_{k,l,n}(u^{\prime\prime})\in\mathcal{B}_{r-1}
.
Consequently,
D={{}\Gamma}_{k,l,n}(u)=\sum{{}\Gamma}_{k,l,n}(u^{\prime}\star_{k,l,n}u^{\prime\prime})=\sum{{}\Gamma}_{k,l,n}(u^{\prime})\,{{}\Gamma}_{k,l,n}(u^{\prime\prime})\in\mathcal{B}_{r}
.

9.2.
Proof of Theorem
B′
when
n<\max\{k,l\}

Set
\underline{k}:=\min\{k,n\}
and
\underline{l}:=\min\{l,n\}
.
We use a reduction to
Theorem
B′

for the case of
\mathscr{A}_{\underline{k},\underline{l},n}^{\mathrm{gr}}
, which follows from Subsection
9.1
. This technique is also used in
[
LZZ11
]
. However,
the arguments of
[
LZZ11
]
do
not
extend routinely to the present setting. The reason is that unlike
[
LZZ11
]
, the
products of the generators
\tilde{\mathsf{L}}_{i,j}^{\mathrm{gr}}
are not weight vectors for the Cartan subalgebras of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{k})
and
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{l})
.
As explained below, in order to circumvent this technical difficulty we use Proposition
2.6.2
.

Set
\underline{m}:=\max\{\underline{k},\underline{l}\}
and
m:=\max\{k,l\}
. Recall that by definition,

\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}}
is
a subalgebra of
\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr}}=\mathscr{PD}_{m\times n}^{\mathrm{gr}}
and

\mathscr{A}_{\underline{k},\underline{l},n}^{\mathrm{gr}}
is a subalgebra of

\mathscr{PD}_{\underline{m}\times n}^{\mathrm{gr}}
.
Let

\boldsymbol{e}^{\mathrm{gr}}:=(\boldsymbol{e}^{\mathrm{gr}})_{\underline{m}\times n}^{m\times n}:\mathscr{PD}_{\underline{m}\times n}^{\mathrm{gr}}\to\mathscr{PD}_{m\times n}^{\mathrm{gr}}

be the map defined in Proposition
3.9.4
(i).
By checking the images of generators of
\mathscr{A}_{\underline{k},\underline{l},n}^{\mathrm{gr}}
we obtain

\boldsymbol{e}^{\mathrm{gr}}\left(\mathscr{A}_{\underline{k},\underline{l},n}^{\mathrm{gr}}\right)\subseteq\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}}.

Lemma 9.2.1
.

\boldsymbol{e}^{\mathrm{gr}}\left(\left(\mathscr{A}_{\underline{k},\underline{l},n}^{\mathrm{gr}}\right)_{(\epsilon_{R})}\right)=\left(\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}}\right)_{(\epsilon_{R})}\cap\boldsymbol{e}^{\mathrm{gr}}\left(\mathscr{A}_{\underline{k},\underline{l},n}^{\mathrm{gr}}\right)
.

Proof.

This follows from
U_{R}
-equivariance of the map
\boldsymbol{e}^{\mathrm{gr}}
(see Proposition
3.9.4
).
∎

Recall that
\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr}}
is a
module over
U_{LR}\otimes U_{LR}=U_{L}\otimes U_{R}\otimes U_{L}\otimes U_{R}
. Let
U_{L}^{(k,l)}
be the subalgebra of
U_{LR}\otimes U_{LR}
defined by

U_{L}^{(k,l)}:=\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{k,m}(U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{k}))\otimes 1\otimes\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{l,m}(U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{l}))\otimes 1.

Proposition 9.2.2
.

The
U_{L}^{(k,l)}
-submodule of
\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr}}
that is generated by

\boldsymbol{e}^{\mathrm{gr}}\left(\left(\mathscr{A}_{\underline{k},\underline{l},n}^{\mathrm{gr}}\right)_{(\epsilon_{R})}\right)
is equal to

\left(\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}}\right)_{(\epsilon_{R})}
.

Proof.

Set

d:=\min\{\underline{k},\underline{l}\}=\min\{k,l,n\}
.
First note that by (
93
) and (
94
) we have isomorphisms of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{k})\otimes U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{l})
-modules

(112)

\left(\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr},(r,s)}\right)_{(\epsilon_{R})}=0\text{\quad for $r\neq s$\quad and\quad}\left(\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr},(r,r)}\right)_{(\epsilon_{R})}\cong\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{d,r}}V_{\lambda}^{*}\otimes V_{\lambda},

where
V_{\lambda}^{*}
(respectively,
V_{\lambda}
) denotes an irreducible
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{k})
-module (respectively,

U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{l})
-module).
Similarly, using
the equivariance of
\boldsymbol{e}^{\mathrm{gr}}
from
Proposition
3.9.4
(ii) we obtain

\left(\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}}\right)_{(\epsilon_{R})}\cap\boldsymbol{e}^{\mathrm{gr}}\left(\mathscr{A}_{\underline{k},\underline{l},n}^{\mathrm{gr},(r,s)}\right)=0\quad\text{ for }r\neq s,

and
an isomorphism of
U_{q}\left(\mathfrak{gl}_{\underline{k}}\right)\otimes U_{q}\left(\mathfrak{gl}_{\underline{l}}\right)
-modules

(113)

\left(\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}}\right)_{(\epsilon_{R})}\cap\boldsymbol{e}^{\mathrm{gr}}\left(\mathscr{A}_{\underline{k},\underline{l},n}^{\mathrm{gr},(r,r)}\right)=\boldsymbol{e}^{\mathrm{gr}}\left(\left(\mathscr{A}_{\underline{k},\underline{l},n}^{\mathrm{gr},(r,r)}\right)_{(\epsilon_{R})}\right)\cong\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{d,r}}\bar{V}_{\lambda}^{*}\otimes\bar{V}_{\lambda},

where
\bar{V}_{\lambda}^{*}

(respectively,
\bar{V}_{\mu}
) is an irreducible
U_{q}\left(\mathfrak{gl}_{\underline{k}}\right)
-module (respectively,

U_{q}\left(\mathfrak{gl}_{\underline{l}}\right)
-module). In the latter relation we use the bar on
\bar{V}_{\lambda}^{*}
and
\bar{V}_{\lambda}
to distinguish
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{\underline{k}})
-modules from
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{k})
-modules and

U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{\underline{l}})
-modules from
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{l})
-modules.
To complete the proof, we need to verify that the summand

\bar{V}_{\lambda}^{*}\otimes\bar{V}_{\lambda}

of (
113
) generates the summand
V_{\lambda}^{*}\otimes V_{\lambda}
of (
112
) as a
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{k})\otimes U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{l})
-module.
In what follows, we prove the latter assertion.
Let
v_{\lambda}^{*}
be a highest weight vector of
the
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{k})
-module
V_{\lambda}^{*}
and let

v_{\lambda}
be a lowest weight vector of
the
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{l})
-module

V_{\lambda}
. It suffices to prove that

(114)

v_{\lambda}^{*}\otimes v_{\lambda}\in\bar{V}_{\lambda}^{*}\otimes\bar{V}_{\lambda}.

The weight of
v_{\lambda}
with respect to the standard Cartan subalgebra of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{l})
is
obtained by applying the longest element of the Weyl group
S_{l}
to the coefficients of
q^{\sum_{i=1}^{d}\lambda_{i}\varepsilon_{i}}
(which is the highest weight of
\bar{V}_{\lambda}
). hence the weight of
v_{\lambda}
is

q^{\sum_{i=1}^{d}\varepsilon_{l-i+1}\lambda_{i}}
. By a similar reasoning, the
weight of
v_{\lambda}^{*}
with respect to the standard Cartan subalgebra of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{k})
is

q^{-\sum_{i=1}^{d}\varepsilon_{k-i+1}\lambda_{i}}
.
Since
k-\underline{k}\leq k-d
, we have
K_{\varepsilon_{i}}\cdot v_{\lambda}^{*}=v_{\lambda}^{*}
for
K_{\varepsilon_{i}}\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{k})
satisfying
1\leq i\leq k-\underline{k}
. Similarly, from
l-\underline{l}\leq l-d
it follows that
K_{\varepsilon_{i}}\cdot v_{\lambda}=v_{\lambda}

for
K_{\varepsilon_{i}}\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{l})
satisfying
1\leq i\leq l-\underline{l}
. Next we express
v_{\lambda}^{*}\otimes v_{\lambda}
as a linear combination of the basis of
\mathscr{PD}^{\mathrm{gr}}
that consists of the monomials (
59
) (see Proposition
3.8.3
). Since
v_{\lambda}^{*}\otimes v_{\lambda}\in\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}}
, the monomials that occur
must satisfy

(115)

a_{i,r}=b_{j,r}=0\quad\text{for }1\leq i\leq m-k,\ 1\leq j\leq m-l,\ 1\leq r\leq n.

By Remark
3.6.2

each of the occurring monomials is a joint eigenvector for the action of

\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{k,m}(K_{\varepsilon_{i}})\otimes 1\otimes\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{l,m}(K_{-\varepsilon_{j}})\otimes 1\quad\text{ where }1\leq i\leq k\text{ and }1\leq j\leq l,

with eigenvalue

q^{-\sum_{r=1}^{n}(a_{m-k+i,r}+b_{m-l+j,r})}.

If this eigenvalue is 1 for
i\leq k-\underline{k}
and
j\leq l-\underline{l}
, then
we must have

(116)

a_{m-k+i,r}=b_{m-l+j,r}=0\quad\text{ for }1\leq i\leq k-\underline{k},\ 1\leq j\leq l-\underline{l},\ 1\leq r\leq n.

From (
115
) and (
116
) (and the general fact that joint eigenfunctions with distinct eigenvalues are linearly independent) it follows that all of the occurring monomials belong to
\boldsymbol{e}^{\mathrm{gr}}(\mathscr{A}_{\underline{k},\underline{l},n}^{\mathrm{gr}})
.
Consequently,
v_{\lambda}^{*}\otimes v_{\lambda}
belongs to the left hand side of (
113
). In addition,
v_{\lambda}^{*}\otimes v_{\lambda}
is the tensor product of a lowest weight vector for a
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{\underline{k}})
-module isomorphic to
\bar{V}_{\lambda}^{*}
and a highest weight vector for a

U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{\underline{l}})
-module isomorphic to
\bar{V}_{\lambda}
. From the decomposition of the right hand side
of (
113
) we obtain
that
v_{\lambda}^{*}\in\bar{V}_{\lambda}^{*}
and
v_{\lambda}\in\bar{V}_{\lambda}
. This completes the proof of (
114
).
∎

We are now ready to complete the proof of (
111
).
From Theorem
B′
for
\mathscr{A}_{\underline{k},\underline{l},n}^{\mathrm{gr}}
(which is established in Subsection
9.1
) it follows that

\boldsymbol{e}^{\mathrm{gr}}\left(\left(\mathscr{A}_{\underline{k},\underline{l},n}^{\mathrm{gr}}\right)_{(\epsilon_{R})}\right)\subseteq\mathcal{B}.

Thus, by Proposition
9.2.2
it suffices to prove that
\mathcal{B}
is stable under the action of
U_{L}^{(k,l)}
.
The key idea to prove this is that the span of the generators of
\mathcal{B}
is stable under an algebra larger than
U_{L}^{(k,l)}
. This enables us to use
Proposition
2.6.2
.
Let
\tilde{U}_{L}^{(k,l)}
be the subalgebra of
U_{LR}\otimes U_{LR}=U_{L}\otimes U_{R}\otimes U_{L}\otimes U_{R}
defined as follows:

(i)

If
k\leq l
, then
\tilde{U}_{L}^{(k,l)}:=\overline{U}_{k,l}\otimes 1\otimes U_{L}\otimes 1
where
\overline{U}_{k,l}
is the subalgebra of
U_{L}=U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{l})
that is generated by

\{E_{i}\}_{i=1}^{l-1}\cup\{F_{i}\}_{i=l-k+1}^{l-1}\cup\{K_{\varepsilon_{i}}\}_{i=1}^{l}.

(ii)

If
k>l
, then

\tilde{U}_{L}^{(k,l)}:=U_{L}\otimes 1\otimes\overline{U}_{k,l}\otimes 1
where

\overline{U}_{k,l}
is the subalgebra of
U_{L}=U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{k})
that is generated by

\{E_{i}\}_{i=k-l+1}^{k-1}\cup\{F_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k-1}\cup\{K_{\varepsilon_{i}}\}_{i=1}^{k}.

Note that in both cases we have
U_{L}^{(k,l)}\subseteq\tilde{U}_{L}^{(k,l)}
.

Proposition 9.2.3
.

\mathcal{B}
is stable under the action of
\tilde{U}_{L}^{(k,l)}
.

Proof.

This follows from Proposition
2.6.2
by setting
H:=U_{L}
,
\mathcal{C}:=\mathcal{C}^{(m)}
where
m:=\max\{k,l\}
,
\check{R}:=\check{\underline{\mathcal{R}}}_{L}
,
H^{\prime}:=U_{R}
,
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}:=\mathcal{C}^{(n)}
,
\check{R}^{\prime}:=\check{\underline{\mathcal{R}}}_{R}
,
A:=\mathscr{P}
,
B:=\mathscr{D}
,
\overline{H}:=\overline{U}_{k,l}

and

\mathcal{E}:=\mathrm{Span}_{\Bbbk}\left\{\tilde{\mathsf{L}}_{i,j}^{\mathrm{gr}}\,:\,1\leq i\leq k\text{ and }1\leq j\leq l\right\}.

Checking that
\mathcal{E}

is stable under the action of
{\tilde{U}}_{L}^{(k,l)}
is a direct calculation based on Remark
3.6.2
. Also,
according to Proposition
3.2.2

we can choose
\omega_{V,W},\overline{\omega}_{V,W}\in H\otimes H
satisfying the condition of Definition
2.5.1
(ii)
to be finite linear combinations of
2
-tensors of the form

KF_{\beta}F_{\beta^{\prime}}F_{\beta^{\prime\prime}}\cdots\otimes K^{\prime}E_{\beta}E_{\beta^{\prime}}E_{\beta^{\prime\prime}}\cdots,

where
\beta,\beta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime\prime},\ldots
are positive roots (see Definition
3.2.1
) and
K,K^{\prime}
are in the Cartan subalgebra. Verifying that the assumptions of Proposition
2.6.2
(i) and
Proposition
2.6.2
(ii)
on
\omega_{V,W}
and
\overline{\omega}_{V,W}
hold is then a direct calculation based on the formulas that express the root vectors as commutators of the
E_{i}
and the
F_{i}
(see Definition
3.2.1
).
∎

9.3.
Proof of Theorem
B
for
\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}

In this subsection we deduce
Theorem
B
for
\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}
from
Theorem
B′
. Let
\mathbb{K}
be any field.
As usual
a
\mathbb{K}
-algebra
\mathcal{A}
is called
filtered
if it has a filtration

\mathcal{A}^{0}\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{1}\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{2}\subseteq\cdots

such that
\mathcal{A}^{i}\mathcal{A}^{j}\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{i+j}
for
i,j\geq 0
. We assume that filtered algebras always satisfy
\mathcal{A}^{0}=\mathbb{K}
.
As usual
\mathrm{gr}(\mathcal{A}):=\bigoplus_{i=-1}^{\infty}\mathcal{A}^{i+1}/\mathcal{A}^{i}
denotes the associated graded algebra of
\mathcal{A}
, where by convention
\mathcal{A}^{-1}=0
.
The following general lemma is standard and can be proved by induction.

Lemma 9.3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a filtered algebra and let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{r}\in\mathcal{A}^{1}
be such that their images in

\mathcal{A}^{1}/\mathcal{A}^{0}
generate
\mathrm{gr}(\mathcal{A})
. Then
a_{1},\ldots,a_{r}
generate
\mathcal{A}
.

The passage from Theorem
B′
to Theorem
B
relies on the folowing proposition.

Proposition 9.3.2
.

Let
\mathcal{A}:=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{A}^{(i)}
be a graded
\mathbb{K}
-algebra and
let
\mathcal{B}
be a filtered
\mathbb{K}
-algebra. Set
\mathcal{A}^{i}:=\bigoplus_{j=0}^{i}\mathcal{A}^{(j)}
for
r\geq 0
, so that
\mathbb{K}:=\mathcal{A}^{0}\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{1}\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{2}\subseteq\cdots
is a filtration of
\mathcal{A}
.
Let
\mathsf{F}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
be a filtration-preserving linear map such that

\mathrm{gr}(\mathsf{F}):\mathrm{gr}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathrm{gr}(\mathcal{B})
is an isomorphism of algebras.
Suppose that

a_{1},\ldots,a_{r}\in\mathcal{A}^{(1)}
generate
\mathcal{A}
. Then
\mathsf{F}(a_{1}),\ldots,\mathsf{F}(a_{r})
generate
\mathcal{B}
.

Proof.

Set
\overline{\mathsf{F}}:=\mathrm{gr}(\mathsf{F})
. Since
\mathcal{A}\cong\mathrm{gr}(\mathcal{A})
, we can consider
\overline{\mathsf{F}}
as a map
\mathcal{A}\to\mathrm{gr}(\mathcal{B})
. Set
b_{i}:=\mathsf{F}(a_{i})

for
1\leq i\leq r
. Then
b_{i}+\mathcal{B}^{0}=\overline{\mathsf{F}}(a_{i})
, hence
b_{1}+\mathcal{B}^{0},\ldots,b_{r}+\mathcal{B}^{0}
generate
\mathrm{gr}(\mathcal{B})
. Thus Lemma
9.3.1
implies that
b_{1},\ldots,b_{r}
generate
\mathcal{B}
.
∎

We return to the proof of Theorem
B
for
\mathcal{A}_{k,l,n}
. We verify that the assumptions of Proposition
9.3.2
hold for
\mathcal{A}:=\left(\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}}\right)_{(\epsilon_{R})}
,
\mathcal{B}:=\left(\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}\right)_{(\epsilon_{R})}
and
\mathsf{F}:=\mathsf{P}_{k,l,n}
. Since
\mathsf{P}_{k,l,n}:\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}}\to\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}
is an isomorphism of
U_{R}
-modules, we have

\mathsf{F}(\mathcal{A})=\mathcal{B}
.
For
r\geq 0
set

(117)

\mathcal{A}^{(r)}:=\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr},(r,r)}\cap\mathcal{A}\quad\ ,\ \mathcal{A}^{r}:=\bigoplus_{s=0}^{r}\mathcal{A}^{(s)}\ ,\ \tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(r)}:=\mathsf{P}_{k,l,n}\left(\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr},(r,r)}\right)\ \text{and}\ \tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{r}:=\bigoplus_{s=0}^{r}\tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(s)}.

Since the
U_{R}
-action on
\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}}
leaves the subspaces
\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr},(r,s)}
stable, we have
\mathcal{A}=\bigoplus_{r=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{A}^{(r)}
.
Define a filtration on
\mathcal{B}
by setting
\mathcal{B}^{r}:=\mathsf{F}(\mathcal{A}^{r})
for
r\geq 0
.
Since we also have
\mathcal{B}=\mathsf{F}(\mathcal{A})
, the map
\mathrm{gr}(\mathsf{F}):\mathrm{gr}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathrm{gr}(\mathcal{B})
is an isomorphism of graded vector spaces. Next we prove that the latter map is an isomorphism of algebras. To this end, it suffices to verify that

(118)

{\mathsf{F}}(D){\mathsf{F}}(D^{\prime})-\mathsf{F}(DD^{\prime})\in\mathcal{B}^{i+j-1}\quad\text{for }D\in\mathcal{A}^{(i)}\text{ and }D^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}^{(j)}.

By Proposition
3.13.3
the left hand side of (
118
) belongs to
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{i+j-1}
. Since
\mathcal{A}_{k,l,n}
is a
U_{R}
-module algebra and
\mathsf{F}
is a
U_{R}
-module homomorphism, we have
\mathsf{F}(D),\mathsf{F}(D^{\prime}),\mathsf{F}(DD^{\prime})\in\mathcal{B}
. It follows that the left hand side of (
118
) also belongs to
\mathcal{B}
. But since the map
\mathsf{P}_{k,l,n}:\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr}}\to\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}
is a bijection,

\tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{r}\cap\mathcal{B}=\mathsf{P}_{k,l,n}\left(\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr},(r,r)}\right)\cap\mathsf{P}_{k,l,n}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)=\mathsf{P}_{k,l,n}\left(\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}^{\mathrm{gr},(r,r)}\cap\mathcal{A}\right)\subseteq\mathcal{B}^{r}\quad\text{for }r\geq 0.

For
r=i+j-1
this implies the inclusion (
118
). Thus we have proved that
the assumptions of
Proposition
9.3.2

hold for
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathsf{F}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
chosen as above.
By Theorem
B′
the
\tilde{\mathsf{L}}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{i,j}
for
1\leq i\leq k
and
1\leq j\leq l
generate
\mathcal{A}
, hence by Proposition
9.3.2

the

\tilde{\mathsf{L}}_{i,j}
generate
\mathcal{B}
. This completes the proof of Theorem
B
for
\mathscr{A}_{k,l,n}
.

10.
Proof of Theorem
C

In this section we give the proof of
Theorem
C
(i). The proof of
Theorem
C
(ii) is analogous.
As a byproduct,
in Corollary
10.4.1

we obtain explicit generators for
\phi_{U}^{-1}(\mathscr{L}_{\mathfrak{h},\bullet})
and

\phi_{U}^{-1}(\mathscr{R}_{\mathfrak{h},\bullet})
.
Henceforth we use
E_{i}
,
F_{i}
and the
K_{\lambda}
for
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}\varepsilon_{1}+\cdots+\mathbb{Z}\varepsilon_{n}
to denote elements of
U_{R}=U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
.

10.1.
Parity condition on the
\lambda

For
\lambda,\mu\in\mathbb{Z}\varepsilon_{1}+\cdots+\mathbb{Z}\varepsilon_{n}

expressed as
\lambda:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}\varepsilon_{i}
and

\mu:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mu_{i}\varepsilon_{i}

we define
\langle\lambda,\mu\rangle
as in (
43
).
We also set
\lambda<\mu
if there exists
1\leq r<n
such that
\lambda_{i}=\mu_{i}
for all
i\leq r
and
\lambda_{r+1}<\mu_{r+1}
. This defines a total order on
\mathbb{Z}\varepsilon_{1}+\cdots+\mathbb{Z}\varepsilon_{n}
. The following lemma is trivial.

Lemma 10.1.1
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a finite subset of
\mathbb{Z}\varepsilon_{1}+\cdots+\mathbb{Z}\varepsilon_{n}

and let
\lambda_{\max}
denote the maximum of
\mathcal{S}
with respect to
<
. Let
\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}
be such that
\gamma_{n}\geq 1
and
\gamma_{i}\geq 1+\max_{\lambda,\mu\in\mathcal{S}}\left\{\sum_{i<j\leq n}|\lambda_{j}-\mu_{j}|\gamma_{j}\right\}
for
i<n
. Set
\gamma:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\gamma_{i}\varepsilon_{i}
. Then

\langle\lambda_{\max},\gamma\rangle>\langle\mu,\gamma\rangle
for all
\mu\in\mathcal{S}
such that

\mu\neq\lambda_{\max}
.

Proposition 10.1.2
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be a finite subset of
\mathbb{Z}\varepsilon_{1}+\cdots+\mathbb{Z}\varepsilon_{n}
. Let
x:=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{I}}c_{\lambda}K_{\lambda}\in U_{\mathfrak{h},R}
where
c_{\lambda}\in\Bbbk^{\times}
for
\lambda\in\mathcal{I}
, and assume that
x\in\mathring{U}_{R}
. Then for all
\lambda:=\sum\lambda_{i}\varepsilon_{i}\in\mathcal{I}

and
1\leq i\leq n-1

we have
\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{i+1}\in 2\mathbb{Z}^{\geq 0}
.

Proof.

Step 1.

Set
D:=\phi_{U}(x)
. By (
71
) we have
\phi_{U}(\mathrm{ad}_{y}(x))=(1\otimes y)\cdot D
for
y\in U_{R}
. Since
\mathscr{PD}
is a locally finite
U_{R}
-module,
\phi_{U}(\mathrm{ad}_{U_{R}}(x))
is a finite dimensional subspace of
\mathscr{PD}
. Furthermore for every
f\in\mathscr{P}
, if we set

W_{f}:=\mathrm{ad}_{U_{R}}(x)\cdot f:=\{\mathrm{ad}_{y}(x)\cdot f\,:\,y\in U_{R}\}
,
then
\dim W_{f}\leq d_{\circ}

where

d_{\circ}:=\dim(\phi_{U}(\mathrm{ad}_{U_{R}}(x)))
.
Note that the upper bound
d_{\circ}
on
\dim W_{f}
is independent of
f
.

Step 2.

Fix
\alpha_{i}:=\varepsilon_{i}-\varepsilon_{i+1}

where

1\leq i\leq n-1
. It suffices to prove that
\langle\lambda,\alpha_{i}\rangle\in 2\mathbb{Z}^{\geq 0}
for
\lambda\in\mathcal{I}
.
For
r\geq 1
we have

\mathrm{ad}_{E_{i}^{r}}K_{\lambda}=\prod_{j=0}^{r-1}\left(1-q^{\langle\lambda,\alpha_{i}\rangle-2j}\right)E_{i}^{r}K_{\lambda}K_{i}^{-r}.

Now take a nonzero
U_{\mathfrak{h},R}
-weight vector
f\in\mathscr{P}
of weight
q^{-\gamma}
for
\gamma:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\gamma_{i}\varepsilon_{i}
, where
(\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{n})
is an
n
-tuple of non-negative integers. We have

\displaystyle\mathrm{ad}_{E_{i}^{r}}(x)\cdot f

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{I}}c_{\lambda}\mathrm{ad}_{E_{i}^{r}}K_{\lambda}\right)\cdot f

(119)

\displaystyle=q^{r\langle\gamma,\alpha_{i}\rangle}\left(\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{I}}c_{\lambda}q^{-\langle\lambda,\gamma\rangle}\prod_{j=0}^{r-1}\left(1-q^{\langle\lambda,\alpha_{i}\rangle-2j}\right)\right)E_{i}^{r}\cdot f.

Step 3.

For any
\lambda\in\mathcal{I}
, if
\langle\lambda,\alpha_{i}\rangle\in 2\mathbb{Z}^{\geq 0}
then
\prod_{j=0}^{r-1}\left(1-q^{\langle\lambda,\alpha_{i}\rangle-2j}\right)=0
for all sufficiently large
r
. Thus, if we set
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}:=\left\{\lambda\in\mathcal{I}\,:\,\langle\lambda,\alpha_{i}\rangle\not\in 2\mathbb{Z}^{\geq 0}\right\}
then there exists
r_{\circ}=r_{\circ}(\mathcal{I})
such that for all
r\geq r_{\circ}
we have

(120)

\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{I}}c_{\lambda}q^{-\langle\lambda,\gamma\rangle}\prod_{j=0}^{r-1}\left(1-q^{\langle\lambda,\alpha_{i}\rangle-2j}\right)=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}c_{\lambda}q^{-\langle\lambda,\gamma\rangle}\prod_{j=0}^{r-1}\left(1-q^{\langle\lambda,\alpha_{i}\rangle-2j}\right).

Note that the lower bound
r_{\circ}
is independent of
\gamma
.

Step 4.

Assume that
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\neq\varnothing
. Choose
r_{\circ}\in\mathbb{N}
according to Step 3. Without loss of generality we can also assume that
r_{\circ}\geq d_{\circ}
. Next choose
r\geq r_{\circ}
.
After possibly scaling
x
by a nonzero element of the polynomial ring
\mathbb{C}[q]
we can assume that the
c_{\lambda}
are nonzero elements in
\mathbb{C}[q]
. Let
\lambda_{\max}
denote the maximum of
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
with respect to
<
.
Choose
\gamma
as in Lemma
10.1.1
(with
\mathcal{S}:=\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
). Since the condition on the coefficient
\gamma_{i}
only depends on
\gamma_{j}
for
j>i
, we can also assume that
\gamma_{i}-\gamma_{i+1}\geq 1
.
For
\lambda\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
let
q^{N(r,\lambda)}
be the lowest power of
q
that occurs after expanding and simplifying

c_{\lambda}q^{-\langle\lambda,\gamma\rangle}\prod_{j=0}^{r-1}\left(1-q^{\langle\lambda,\alpha_{i}\rangle-2j}\right)
.
We have

N(r,\lambda_{\max})\leq-\langle\lambda_{\max},\gamma\rangle+\deg c_{\lambda_{\max}}(q),

because the lowest power
q^{N(r,\lambda)}
is obtained as follows:
from each factor

\left(1-q^{\langle\lambda,\alpha_{i}\rangle-2j}\right)
we can choose
1
if

{\langle\lambda,\alpha_{i}\rangle-2j}>0
and
q^{\langle\lambda,\alpha_{i}\rangle-2j}
otherwise.
For all other
\lambda\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
we have

N(r,\lambda)\geq-\deg c_{\lambda}(q^{-1})-\langle\lambda,\gamma\rangle-r|\langle\lambda,\alpha_{i}\rangle|-r(r-1).

By the choice of
\gamma
, for

\lambda\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
such that
\lambda\neq\lambda_{\max}
we have
\langle\lambda_{\max},\gamma\rangle\geq 1+\langle\lambda,\gamma\rangle
. Thus

\langle\lambda_{\max},k\gamma\rangle\geq k+\langle\lambda,k\gamma\rangle\quad\text{
for }k\in\mathbb{N}.

Next choose
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
k\geq 2r_{\circ}
and

(121)

k\geq\max_{\lambda\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime},\lambda\neq\lambda_{\max}}\big\{\deg c_{\lambda_{\max}}(q)+\deg c_{\lambda}(q^{-1})+2r_{\circ}|\langle\lambda,\alpha_{i}\rangle|+2r_{\circ}(2r_{\circ}-1)\big\}.

If we substitute

\gamma
by
k\gamma
,
from (
121
) we obtain
that

N(r,\lambda_{\max})<N(r,\lambda)
for all
\lambda\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\backslash\{\lambda_{\max}\}
and
r_{\circ}\leq r\leq 2r_{\circ}
. Together with Step 3, this proves that for the latter choice of
\gamma
we have

\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{I}}c_{\lambda}q^{-\langle\lambda,\gamma\rangle}\prod_{j=0}^{r-1}\left(1-q^{\langle\lambda,\alpha_{i}\rangle-2j}\right)\neq 0\quad\text{for }r_{\circ}\leq r\leq 2r_{\circ},

because the coefficient of
q^{N(r,\lambda_{\max})}
is nonzero.

Step 5.
The
\gamma
chosen at the end of Step 4 satisfies
\gamma_{i}-\gamma_{i+1}\geq 2r_{\circ}
, or equivalently

\langle-\gamma,\alpha_{i}\rangle\leq-2r_{\circ}
(because
k\geq 2r_{\circ}
). Choose
f\in\mathscr{P}
of
U_{\mathfrak{h},R}
-weight
q^{-\gamma}
(for example
f:=t_{1,1}^{\gamma_{1}}\cdots t_{1,n}^{\gamma_{n}}
). A standard argument based on representation theory of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
implies
E_{i}^{r}\cdot f\neq 0
for
0\leq r\leq 2r_{\circ}
.
Since the vectors
E_{i}^{s}\cdot f
for
0\leq s\leq 2r_{\circ}
have distinct
U_{\mathfrak{h},R}
-weights, they are linearly independent.
From Step 2 and Step 4 it follows that
the vectors
\mathrm{ad}_{E_{i}^{s}}(x)\cdot f
for
r_{\circ}\leq s\leq 2r_{\circ}

are also linearly independent. Consequently,
\dim W_{f}\geq r_{\circ}+1\geq d_{\circ}+1
. This contradicts Step 1.
∎

10.2.
Proof of
\lambda_{1}\leq 0

We begin with the following observation.

Remark 10.2.1
.

Let
D\in\mathscr{PD}
and let
\mathsf{a}:=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
be an
n
-tuple of non-negative integers. We use the notation

\partial^{\mathsf{a}}:=\partial_{1,1}^{a_{1}}\cdots\partial_{1,n}^{a_{n}}

and

t^{\mathsf{a}}:=t_{1,n}^{a_{n}}\cdots t_{1,1}^{a_{1}}

for an
n
-tuple of integers
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
.
Assume that
D\cdot t^{\mathsf{a}}=ct^{\mathsf{a}}
for some
c\in\Bbbk
.
Recall the basis of
\mathscr{PD}
that consists of the monomials (
61
).
We can write
D
as
D=D_{1}+D_{2}+D_{3}
where

(i)

D_{1}
is a linear combination of
basis vectors of the form
t^{\mathsf{b}^{\prime}}\partial^{\mathsf{b}^{\prime}}
where
\mathsf{b}^{\prime}
is an
n
-tuple of non-negative integers,

(ii)

D_{2}
is a linear combination of basis vectors of the form
t^{\mathsf{a}^{\prime}}\partial^{\mathsf{b}^{\prime}}
where
\mathsf{a}^{\prime}
and
\mathsf{b}^{\prime}
are
n
-tuples of non-negative integers and
\mathsf{a}^{\prime}\neq\mathsf{b}^{\prime}
, and

(iii)

D_{3}
is a linear combination of the remaining basis vectors in (
61
).

Using Lemma
3.17.1

and then
Lemma
3.17.12

we obtain
D\cdot t^{\mathsf{a}}=(D_{1}+D_{2})\cdot t^{\mathsf{a}}=D_{1}\cdot t^{\mathsf{a}}
.

Example 10.2.2
.

Set
\lambda:=\varepsilon_{1}+\cdots+\varepsilon_{n}
and
x:=K_{\lambda}\in U_{R}
. Then
x\cdot t_{1,1}^{r}=q^{-r}t_{1,1}^{r}
for
r\geq 1
. From Remark
10.2.1
,
Lemma
3.17.12

and Remark
3.17.10

it follows that if
\phi_{U}(1\otimes x)\in{\mathscr{R}_{\bullet}}
then the eigenvalue of
t_{1,1}^{r}
with respect to
\phi_{U}(1\otimes x)
should be a ratio of two polynomials such as
\phi_{1}(q)/\phi_{2}(q)
where
\deg\phi_{2}
is bounded above (independently of
r
). Thus,

\phi_{U}(1\otimes x)\not\in{\mathscr{R}_{\bullet}}
and
in particular
{\mathscr{R}_{\bullet}}\subsetneq\mathscr{R}
. Consequently,
K_{\lambda}
is a locally finite element of
U_{R}
that does not belong to
\mathring{U}_{R}
.

Proposition 10.2.3
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be a finite subset of
\mathbb{Z}\varepsilon_{1}+\cdots+\mathbb{Z}\varepsilon_{n}
. Let
x:=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{I}}c_{\lambda}K_{\lambda}\in U_{\mathfrak{h},R}
where
c_{\lambda}\in\Bbbk^{\times}
for
\lambda\in\mathcal{I}
, and assume that
x\in\mathring{U}_{R}
. Then for all
\lambda:=\sum\lambda_{i}\varepsilon_{i}\in\mathcal{I}
we have
\lambda_{1}\leq 0
.

Proof.

Set
D:=\phi_{U}(x)
, so that
D\in\mathscr{PD}
.
Write
D=D_{1}+D_{2}+D_{3}
as in Remark
10.2.1

and suppose that
D_{1}=\sum_{\mathsf{a}\in\mathcal{Z}}\mathbf{z}_{\mathsf{a}}t^{\mathsf{a}}\partial^{\mathsf{a}}
where
\mathcal{Z}
is a finite set of
n
-tuples of non-negative integers and the
\mathbf{z}_{\mathsf{a}}\in\Bbbk^{\times}
.
After scaling
x
by a nonzero element of
\mathbb{C}[q]
if necessary, we can assume that the
c_{\lambda}
and the
\mathbf{z}_{\mathsf{a}}
are nonzero polynomials in
q
.
Recall that

t^{\gamma}:=t_{1,n}^{\gamma_{n}}\cdots t_{1,1}^{\gamma_{1}}

for

\gamma:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\gamma_{i}\varepsilon_{i}
in
\mathbb{Z}\varepsilon_{1}+\cdots+\mathbb{Z}\varepsilon_{n}
.
Then

D\cdot t^{\gamma}=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{I}}c_{\lambda}q^{-\langle\lambda,\gamma\rangle}t^{\gamma}.

Also,
by Lemma
3.17.12

and Remark
10.2.1
we obtain
D\cdot t^{\gamma}=D_{1}\cdot t^{\gamma}=\sum_{\mathsf{a}\in\mathcal{Z}}\mathbf{z}_{\mathsf{a}}(q)\phi_{\mathsf{a}}(q^{2})t^{\gamma}
, where the
\phi_{\mathsf{a}}
are polynomials in
q
